   THESIS ABSTRACT

Monitoring is crucial for conservation, but elusive species, including some species of coastal
delphinids, represent particular challenges. Traditional visual surveys can often underestimate
metrics such as abundance. For Indian Ocean humpback dolphins (Sousa plumbea) in South
Africa, near-shore habitats and low encounter rates hinder photographic data collection. Passive
acoustic monitoring (PAM) offers a non-invasive alternative for species monitoring. This thesis
investigates the whistling behaviour of S. plumbea in South Africa, focusing on exploring the use
of signature whistles for individual acoustic detection. Using boat-based recordings (2016–2023)
from five sites throughout their South African range, it examines whistle variation across
different geographic scales (including a comparison of whistle-contour time-frequency
parameters across the extremes of the entire geographic range of the species) and the effect of
group composition and behaviour on signature whistle production rates, aiming to optimise
passive acoustic detection methods. The efficacy of individual acoustic detection using PAM was
directly compared to visual detection (Photo ID) through a two-week survey period in Mossel
Bay. Following this, an extended acoustic monitoring period along a portion of the south coast
subpopulation investigated individual acoustic monitoring across three sites, using five
hydrophones over six weeks, providing abundance estimates, as well as density estimates
generated using spatially explicit capture-recapture (SECR). Macrogeographic variation in
whistle contours was evident between South Africa and India, two populations at the furthest
extent of the species' geographic range (Random Forest model correct classification rate; South
Africa 84% (35.2% chance) and India 93% (64.8% chance)). Moderate variation within South
Africa ranged from 39–90% across study sites, indicating increased acoustic variation with
geographic distance. Group composition factors such as group size, group spread, calf presence,
and behaviour had little impact on signature whistle production. These findings suggest that
these factors are not crucial for optimising the detection of signature whistles in the acoustic
monitoring of S. plumbea. Comparing monitoring methods, photographic identification was more
cost-effective (6,400 ZAR.dolphin-1) than acoustic methods (12,266.40 ZAR.dolphin-1). However,
acoustic detection was diurnal, identifying more individuals and signature whistles at night and
across the entire survey area. Visual surveys detected dolphins at only two locations, emphasising
PAM's effectiveness as an alternative method for detecting S. plumbea. Abundance estimates from
signature whistle detected along a 120 km stretch of South Africa's south coast revealed limited
individual movement, with low abundance (SECR: 23–26; POPAN: 26, 95% CI 23–30) and density
(0.000061–0.000439 animals.ha-1). This proof-of-concept study explored mooring configurations
and model optimisation, demonstrating that expanding the geographic range of the acoustic array
could be more effective for increasing unique recaptures than clustering acoustic receivers in a
smaller area. This thesis demonstrates that PAM is a reliable alternative to photographic surveys
to study the elusive coastal species S. plumbea and produces sufficient data to understand
population abundances and movements using individual acoustic signatures. These techniques
offer a scalable and reproducible approach to producing population estimates, which can equip
wildlife practitioners and scientists with the tools to monitor this elusive species reliably over
time and space.

   ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS
This work would not have been possible without the unwavering support of my supervisors, who
cheered me on until the very last moment.
John, thank you for keeping me on track. You’ve literally written the book on ‘How to write a PhD’.
Your words of advice have been invaluable. Thank you for having the patience to spend hours
pouring over paragraph structure with me. Simon, the voice of reason, thank you for your
constant encouragement and wicked sense of humour throughout some very long office days.
And Tess – I couldn’t have picked a better adventure buddy. You’ve shown me science can be done
anywhere, anytime, including in a coffee shop while juggling a newborn. It’s been a very busy few
years, and in that time, you’ve had a baby, and I managed to birth this PhD! Two great feats. Thank
you for being a friend and a mentor.

the events of this last year to have happened. Gui will forever be a huge part of my journey over
the last few years. His constant enthusiasm and positivity, mixed with endless thought-provoking

and working with so many inspiring and supportive people. But to name a few...

process and to my funders: the South African National Research Foundation, the PADI

    LIST OF FIGURES
Figure 1.1. Sousa plumbea distribution throughout South Africa indicates two regions of
occurrence, namely, the populations of the south and east coasts. Nationally, Sousa plumbea have
an extreme coastal range, limited to less than 20 m water depth and not extending more than 2-
5 km off the coastline, creating high spatial use overlap with Marine Protected areas throughout
their coastal range. Spatial data sources: Environmental Systems Research Institute (ESRI, 2021),
South African National Biodiversity Institute (SANBI, 2021), and IUCN Red List Assessment
(Braulik et al., 2015).

Figure 1.2. Thesis overview diagram to demonstrate the conceptual framework of the thesis. The
framework shows each data chapter (2-5), including chapter themes and datasets used and how
they build on one another throughout the thesis.
Figure 2.1. Sousa plumbea study sites distributed throughout South Africa and India. A) The red
dots indicate encounters where Sousa plumbea were recorded. B) The Indian population were
recorded throughout the Maharashtra region (Bopardikar et al., 2018). C) The South African
population were recorded in both the south and east coast regions. Within South Africa, five
separate study sites were included, including four on the south coast (West to East: False Bay,
Struisbaai, St Sebastian Bay and Mossel Bay) and one on the east coast (Richards Bay), as
indicated by yellow circles on the figure.
Figure 2.2. A) Diagram of acoustic measurements on a spectrogram, illustrating an example
whistle contour with time (s) on the X-axis and frequency (kHz) on the Y-axis. Measurements
shown are 1) Maximum frequency, 2) Minimum frequency, 3) Frequency range, 4) Start
frequency, 5) End frequency, 6) Duration, and 7) Inflection points. B). Diagram of acoustic
measurements on a spectrogram, using a Sousa plumbea whistle contour example from the south
coast, South Africa. In this example, the maximum frequency, start frequency and end frequency
are all equal.

Figure 2.3. Variation of Sousa plumbea whistle parameters measured between South African and
Indian datasets. All five sites in South Africa were pooled together (green, n = 1181) and
compared to India (blue, n = 2260). Coloured dots show the distribution of the data for each
population. In each plot, the box represents the inter-quartile range (IQR, middle 50%) of each
data set, with centre lines representing median values, and the lines extending from the box
(whiskers) represent the range within 1.5 times the IQR from the lower and upper quartiles.
Points outside this range are considered outliers and shown as black dots.
Figure 2.4. Principal component plot showing the first two components based on Sousa plumbea
whistle contour measurements, derived from the seven measured parameters across the six
pooled study sites in South Africa and the study site in India. Data points for South Africa are in
green and India data points are in blue. The coloured ellipses contain 95% of data points for each
population. A high degree of overlap (similarity) is found between these geographically separated
populations based on the first two principal components. Principal component one explains
59.7% of the variance and component two explains 26.4% of the variance.
Figure 2.5. Variation of Sousa plumbea whistle parameters measured across the five South African
study sites (From left to right and west to east; False Bay Red, Struisbaai Blue, St Sebastian Bay
Green, Mossel Bay Purple and Richards Bay Orange). Coloured dots show the distribution of the
data for each region. The five frequency-based measurements (minimum and maximum
frequency, start and end frequency and frequency range) are presented on the left-hand side
graph, duration (measured in seconds) is presented in the middle graph, and the Inflection count
is on the right-side graph. In each plot, the box represents the inter-quartile range (IQR, middle
50%) of each data set, with centre lines representing median values, and the lines extending from

Figure 4.1. Map of moored hydrophone locations (left) and boat surveys (right) for Sousa plumbea
in Mossel Bay, South Africa, from 19th March – 5th April 2021. Moorings were numbered from
mooring 1 (south) – mooring 6 (north). Moored hydrophones are spaced equally ~2 km apart
along the coastal range of Sousa plumbea. Boat-based surveys are shown with boat survey tracks
in green (one location per minute) with tracking during Sousa plumbea encounters in Red. Both
maps show a 15 m water depth area in orange and 20 m water depth area in brown.
Figure 4.2. Workflow used to identify Sousa plumbea whistle encounters from passive acoustic
recordings. The species occur sympatrically with Tursiops aduncus in Mossel Bay and therefore a
workflow to confidently identify Sousa plumbea whistle encounters from passive acoustic
recordings collected in Mossel Bay, South Africa was necessary. Whistle and moan detector
(PAMGuard; Gillespie et al., 2014 www.pamguard.org) was used to identify dolphin whistle
encounters in the recordings and a custom trained species classifier in PAMGuard used to identify
Sousa plumbea whistle encounters.
Figure 4.3. Signature whistle type detections (SWT, acoustically unique individuals) of Sousa
plumbea in Mossel Bay, South Africa across the 18-day deployment period in Mossel Bay, South
Africa, from 19th March – 5th April 2021. The moorings the SWTs are detected on are represented
by different colours and the size of the dot represents the number of detections of that whistle
type. Detections are grouped by 24-hour bins (days).
Figure 4.4. The signature whistle count (signal-to-noise ratio of 2 or 3 and including SWTUC;
unclassified whistle types) of Sousa plumbea at each of the six moorings in Mossel Bay, along the
south coast of South Africa, representing the spatial variability of dolphin detections between
sites. Bars represent the signature whistle count of signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) 2 and 3 whistles
at each mooring. Bars are divided by signature whistle type (SWT, proxy for individuals).
Figure 4.5. Diurnal patterns of signature whistles detected from Sousa plumbea, in Mossel Bay,
South Africa, from 19th March – 5th April 2021, classified into signature whistle types (SWTs). The
night period is specified as 1800-0600. 10 different SWTs (number of individuals) were detected
throughout the deployment period. The plot shows the total signature whistle count for every
SWT detected over 24-hour time bins. A) Plot includes all SWTs including unclassified SWTs
(SWTUC), which are included at the bottom of the stacked bar plot (yellow) and B) Plot removes
SWTUC and outlier encounters (SWT05, 0900).

Figure 4.6. Generalised Additive Models showing the probability of detections of signature
whistle type, SWT (plot A - gam( unique_SWT_per_hour ~ factor(hour) -1, family = Poisson(log)))
and signature whistle count, SW (plot B - gam( SW_per_hour ~ factor(hour), family =
Poisson(log))) over a diurnal period for Sousa plumbea in Mossel Bay, South Africa, from 19th
March – 5th April 2021. The grey region represents the 95% CI region. Both plot A, signature
whistle types (SWTs, count of individuals) and plot B, signature whistles (SWs, total whistle
count) indicated a diurnal pattern, with increased detection over the night period 1800-0600. In
both models, SWTUC and outliers were omitted.

whistle types (SWTs) were detected across all moorings combined over the full 42-day
deployment. One capture occasion = one calendar week. The highest number of weeks on which
SWTs were detected was three, with no SWTs detected across more than four weeks.

    LIST OF TABLES
Table 1.1. Summary of literature review of existing acoustic repertoire data across the genus
Sousa. Key publications covering all four call types (whistles, clicks, burst pulses and low-
frequency narrowband calls (LFNs)). The whistle repertoire literature review for the Sousa genus
is presented in Table 2.11.
Table 1.2. Summary of study design and capture-recapture results from previous studies on Sousa
plumbea in South Africa (adapted and updated from James et al., 2015).
Table 2.1. Summary table of the six study sites from South Africa and India, detailing the recording
equipment, sampling and bit rates, as well as the total number of field days at each location. The
euclidean distances (in km) between neighbouring recording sites are also provided, with False
Bay, the westernmost site, serving as the starting point (0 km).
Table 2.2. Summary of Sousa plumbea encounters recorded across the six South African and
Indian study sites. Each row indicates a unique acoustic encounter, defined as starting when the
hydrophone was placed in the water and continuing until the dolphins were out of acoustic range.
Encounter information includes the best group size, primary and (if apparent) secondary
behaviours (see Table 3.3).
Table 2.3. Descriptions of the seven contour measurements used for analysis (Bopardikar et al.,
2018; Wang et al., 2013). All frequency measurements were measured in kilohertz (kHz) and time
measurements in seconds (s). Measurements were all calculated in Raven Pro v1.6 (Center for
Conservation Bioacoustics, 2019). The first four measurements were calculated automatically
from the annotation box function. Start frequency, end frequency and inflection points were
manually measured.

Table 2.4. Summary of all acoustic encounters and the total analysed contours across all six study
sites in South Africa and India for Sousa plumbea. Only contours with a good signal-to-noise ratio
of 2 or 3 or > 6 dB signal-to-noise threshold were used for further analysis (presented in
percentage of contours analysed). Totals are accumulated throughout the table, except for the
percentage of contours analysed which are separated based on the South African and Indian
datasets.
Table 2.5. Descriptive statistics were calculated for the seven whistle contour parameters of Sousa
plumbea, measured for both South Africa (with all five study sites combined) and India. For each
measurement, the minimum and maximum values, median (inter quartile range, IQR), mean
(standard deviation, SD), and coefficient of variance (CoV)% were calculated. Frequency
parameters were measured in kilohertz (kHz) and duration was measured in seconds (s).
Table 2.6. Principal Component loadings for the seven contour measurements of Sousa plumbea
whistle contours that were recorded in South Africa (with the five study sites pooled together)
and India. Values in bold had loadings greater than 0.5 or less than -0.5 (high factor loadings).
Principal component one was responsible for 59.7% of the overall variance, with the first two
principal components accounting for 86.1% of the variance.
Table 2.7. Confusion matrix for Sousa plumbea from both South Africa and India using a Random
Forest algorithm based on the seven contour measurements. Overall model accuracy between
South Africa and India was 89.7%. Classification of the test data is presented in percentages (%).
Percentages in bold represent values of correct contour classification. Levels of chance for correct
classification are presented alongside observed locations.

    1 GENERAL INTRODUCTION

   ACOUSTIC COMMUNICATION IN DELPHINIDS

Communication is the transfer of information from signaller to receiver and can occur through

visual, olfactory, chemical, acoustic senses, or, at very short distances, through touch (Labra et al.,

1999; Marler, 1961). The complexity of a species' communication system is often linked to its

level of social organisation, and understanding how species communicate can provide

information on the species' relationship both to its conspecifics and environment (Klump, 2020).

Signals can contain information on innate or fixed aspects such as size, age, and sex (Seyfarth et

al., 2010) or variable aspects such as health, arousal, and rank (Bergman & Sheehan, 2013;

Briefer, 2020; Taylor et al., 2010). The ability to recognise other species, groups or even specific

individuals within the group can be indicative of social complexity and sophisticated

communication processes within a species (Yorzinski, 2017). Animals can prioritise a certain

type of communication system based on circumstance, such as proximity to conspecifics, or

environmental conditions, such as aquatic or high-density habitats (Ey & Fischer, 2009). In such

environments, acoustic signalling is beneficial due to the flexibility in the rapid transmission of

signals and effective propagation over varied distances (Ladich & Winkler, 2017).



Delphinids, a family of over 35 species, have a range of social structures from large pods of 1000s

of individuals with continuous fission-fusion dynamics (Richards et al., 2013) to strong

matrilineal groups (Deecke et al., 2010) and are present throughout the global oceans (Best,

2007) leading to the development of a sophisticated acoustic repertoire (Janik, 2009; Luís et al.,

2021). Delphinids can produce two main types of sound: clicks (including burst pulse sounds)

and tonal frequency-modulated sounds (including low-frequency narrow-band sounds, LFNs,

and whistles) (Jones et al., 2020; Luís et al., 2021; Popper, 1980). Clicks are short signals that

reach into the ultrasonic frequency range and occur in trains with an inter-click interval of more

than 10 ms (Au et al., 1974; Jones et al., 2020). They are most commonly used in echolocation to

                                                                                                    16
                                             https://scholar.sun.ac.za




perceive the environment, especially during foraging (Herman & Tavolga, 1980). Burst pulse

signals are rapid click trains, typically defined as having an inter-click interval of less than 10 ms

(Luís et al., 2021). The frequency of click trains and burst pulses fluctuates depending on function

and behavioural context. Burst pulses appear to be involved in communication interactions

(Lammers 2003), as the inter-click interval exceeds the round-trip propagation time required to

receive individual click echoes (Ivanov, 2004). In a review of sounds produced by bottlenose

dolphins (Tursiops truncatus), Jones et al., (2020) describe low-frequency narrow band (LFN)

sounds as having a fundamental frequency of less than 2 kHz (0.26 – 1.28 kHz) and durations less

than 0.41 s (0.01 – 0.41 seconds). LFNs are rarely reported within the literature, and it is difficult

to determine how widely used these sounds are (Jones et al., 2020).



Whistles are narrowband frequency-modulated sounds and one of the more commonly studied

call types due to their dominant use by many species and ease of measurement (Herzing, 2014;

Janik, 2009). Most whistling delphinid species focus their energy below 20 kHz (Popper, 1980),

yet the maximum recorded fundamental frequencies can reach over 40 kHz in T. truncatus (41.80

kHz: Hiley et al., 2017). Whistles function as a communicative signal, and they are commonly

produced under socialising contexts (La Manna et al., 2019; Marley et al., 2017; Papale et al.,

2017). Whistles can convey arousal states and behaviour (Quick & Janik, 2008) and also function

to convey identity (Sayigh et al., 1999) within the frequency modulation of the tonal sound

(Kershenbaum et al., 2013; Sayigh et al., 1999). The fission-fusion group dynamics of delphinid

species, such as common (Delphinus spp.) or bottlenose dolphins (Tursiops spp.), lead to a greater

level of individual distinctiveness within their whistle repertoire to account for the fluidity of

these associations and ensure the maintenance of complex social relationships and behaviour

(Fearey et al., 2019; Reiss et al., 1997).




                                                                                                   17
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   SIGNATURE WHISTLES IN DELPHINIDS

Individual recognition in animals occurs through multiple senses in different species. However,

in the marine environment, communication through acoustic channels is the most powerful and

widespread (Ladich & Winkler, 2017). Unlike voice cues, which can affect all animal calls,

bottlenose dolphins can encode individual identity information within the frequency modulation

pattern of whistles (Janik & Slater, 1997; Sayigh et al., 2017). These individually unique whistles

are termed ‘signature whistles’ (Janik, 2009). Caldwell and Caldwell (1965) first identified these

individually distinctive frequency contours in bottlenose dolphins and hypothesised that these

were used to transmit identity information. A signature whistle has since been defined more

formally as a learned, individually distinctive whistle type in a dolphin’s repertoire that

broadcasts the identity of the whistle owner as a contact call (Sayigh et al., 2007). Individually

distinct calls are extremely rare in the animal kingdom (Boughman & Moss, 2006). Signature

whistles develop in the first year of a calf's life through vocal learning and the auditory experience

of the environment and conspecifics therein (Sayigh et al., 1990). MacFarlane (2016) identified

three potential functions for signature whistles: maintaining contact, regaining lost contact, and

advertising identity. As signature whistles are developed through vocal learning, this ability

means that whistle matching is also possible (Janik, 2000). Individuals are known to whistle

match and copy the signature whistles of those with whom they form a strong social bond (King

et al., 2014; Probert et al., 2021). However, this copying occurs in small numbers and usually

when the copied individual is present (King et al., 2013), with the copied whistles identifiable

through parameter variations within the copy (King et al., 2013).



Research on signature whistles has been conducted for over 60 years (Janik & Sayigh, 2013),

mainly in T. truncatus, using either captive (Janik & Slater, 1998) or temporarily restrained wild

individuals (Esch et al., 2009; Sayigh et al., 1990, 1999). As signature whistles act predominantly

as cohesion calls, they can constitute up to 100% of whistles produced during these separation

events (when isolated or restrained from conspecifics) (Janik & Slater, 1998). However, this value

                                                                                                   18
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




is considerably reduced in recordings from non-restrained wild populations (38% - 70% (Cook

et al., 2004). Functioning primarily as contact calls in these freely interacting contexts, signature

whistles are produced at higher rates when animals meet at sea (Quick & Janik, 2008), mother

and calves reunite (Smolker et al., 1993) and when engaged in certain behaviours, such as

socialising, that require a form of individual recognition (Sayigh et al., 1999). In bottlenose

dolphins, these contact calls were identified as bouts of stereotyped whistles emitted within 1 to

10 seconds of each other (Janik, 2013). Although most widely documented in bottlenose dolphins,

signature whistles have been reported in other odontocete species, such as narwhals (Monodon

monoceros) (Shapiro, 2006), short-finned pilot whales (Globicephala macrorhynchus) (Sayigh et

al., 2013), melon-headed whales (Peponocephala electra) (Kaplan et al., 2014) and common

dolphins (Delphinus delphis) (Fearey et al., 2019). Some studies have also documented likely

signature whistle use within the genus Sousa using opportunistic recording events (Cheng et al.,

2017; Van Parijs & Corkeron, 2001a).




                                                                                                  19
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   PRACTICAL USES OF SIGNATURE WHISTLES FOR RESEARCH

Research approaches using individual identification (capture-recapture) can provide long-term

metrics, from the population level down to the individual, such as abundance, habitat range or

movement, social networks, and group size (Hammond, 1986; Karlsson et al., 2005). Individual

identification from naturally occurring markings is useful when more invasive marking

techniques are challenging to perform, detect, or are ethically inappropriate (Terry et al., 2005).

Furthermore, individually distinctive acoustic signals are less invasive and have been suggested

as a suitable alternative to active marking or photographic identification (Janik et al., 2013).

Signature whistles are naturally occurring, individually distinctive and stable over time (up to 43

years, Sayigh et al., 2022) and overcome many of the biases inherent in using artificial marks or

marks from scarring or injuries (James et al., 2022). Although previously suggested by Janik et al.,

(2013), Longden et al., (2020) first identified signature whistles as a potentially suitable

technique to generate abundance estimates in a Namibian population of bottlenose dolphins

(Tursiops truncatus) by using signature whistles in a capture-recapture framework where the

results compared favourably with established photographic methods (Friday et al., 2008; Urian

et al., 2015) . Further work has also investigated the role of recording duty cycles and fine-scale

hydrophone placement on the use of signature whistles in a capture-recapture framework

(Fearey et al., 2022). Forming part of the SWORD project (Signature Whistles for Occurrence,

Recapture and Density, Fearey et al., 2021), this proposed methodology, using signature whistles

in a capture-recapture framework for acoustic-based monitoring, has yet to be applied outside

the genus Tursiops.




                                                                                                 20
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   ACOUSTIC BEHAVIOUR OF SOUSA SPP
There are around 50 studies on the acoustic behaviour across all four species in the genus Sousa,

with Sousa chinensis the most widely published, around Malaysia and China (Hoffman et al., 2015;

Sims et al., 2012; Wang et al., 2013), then Sousa sahulensis in Australia (de Freitas et al., 2015;

Hoffman et al., 2017; Schultz & Corkeron, 1994a; Van Parijs & Corkeron, 2001b), as well as a single

publication on the acoustic behaviour of the IUCN Critically Endangered Sousa teuszii in western

Africa (Weir, 2010) (Table 1.1). S. chinensis has the most widely defined whistle repertoire with

maximum reported whistle frequencies of 33 kHz (Wang et al., 2013). Whistle measurements

vary across studies, with some studies presenting limited information (Sims et al., 2012; Zbinden

et al., 1977) and some are limited by the bandwidth of recording equipment (Schultz & Corkeron,

1994a; Van Parijs & Corkeron, 2001b; Weir, 2010).




To date, three published studies have examined the acoustic characteristics of Sousa plumbea in

Pakistan, India and Zanzibar. Zbinden et al., (1977) published the first acoustic study on S.

chinensis (now S. plumbea) from the Indus River delta region, Pakistan. The study was limited by

equipment frequency range but demonstrated a long whistle duration of up to 2.5 seconds and

call combinations of burst pulse trains and whistle production (screams). Nearly four decades

later, Bopardikar et al., (2018) published a full description of the whistle repertoire (single

element tonal signals) for S. plumbea along the Maharashtra coast, India, with a frequency range

of up to 33 kHz and durations of up to 1.6 seconds. The first click analysis for the genus was

published by Yang et al., (2020), who showed a high rate of species classification of S. plumbea

compared to sympatric T. aduncus. Across all studies, caution should be taken in extrapolating

findings to other S. plumbea populations, as interspecific and intraspecific differences in time

frequency features across regions have been documented in Sousa spp. (Hoffman et al., 2015;

Wang et al., 2013).

                                                                https://scholar.sun.ac.za

   THE STUDY SPECIES: SOUSA PLUMBEA

Sousa plumbea was recognised as a distinct species from S. chinensis in 2014 based on molecular

analysis, skeletal morphology, external morphology and colouration (Jefferson & Rosenbaum,

2014; Mendez et al., 2013). The range of S. plumbea extends from their most southwestern point

in False Bay, South Africa, along the southern and eastern African coastlines to as far as the west

coast of India (Plön et al., 2015). In the southern African subregion, S. plumbea has been recorded

from South Africa, Mozambique, Tanzania (including Zanzibar), Kenya, the Comoro Islands and

Madagascar (Best, 2007).



Globally, S. plumbea is not highly abundant anywhere in its range, with global abundance likely in

the low ten thousands (Braulik et al., 2015). Previous research in East Africa on S. plumbea has

shown small, declining populations from several localised studies but a region wide assessment

has not been done. Research in Maputo Bay and Bazaruto Bay, southern Mozambique, indicated

low densities of 0.47 dolphins per km2 (Peddemors, 1999) and a population in the Maputo Bay

area of ~105 individuals in the late 1990s (Guissamulo & Cockcroft, 2005). In Zanzibar, further

along the east coast of Africa, there has been a 63% reduction in the population size since 2002,

with the last population abundance estimate of 19 (95% CI 14-25) in the Menai Bay conservation

area in 2015 (Sharpe & Berggren, 2019). Limited information on population density is available

across the rest of their African range, especially at a broader scale (Braulik et al., 2015). In South

Africa, group size in S. plumbea is typically small, as previous studies have only seen a maximum

of 30 individuals in a group, but groups are more commonly less than 10 (Plön et al., 2015).

Several population estimates have been made throughout their South Africa range, mostly in

localised areas such as in one or two bays, and from ~ 20 to 96 individuals (Table 1.2). A collection

of photo identification catalogues from previous studies collected around South Africa from 2000

to 2016 identified 247 unique individuals along the whole South African distribution, suggesting

a total South African population of fewer than 500 animals (Vermeulen et al., 2018), substantially


                                                                                                   26
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




lower than the 1000 estimated as the total country population by Karczmarski (1996) from photo

ID data available in the late 1990s.




The general life history of S. plumbea in South Africa reflects that of a medium-sized coastal

delphinid that occupies a very narrow habitat niche, typically less than 3 km from shore and in

waters shallower than 25 m. Populations occur around a variety of coastal habitats, including

rocky reefs, coastal lagoons, and shallow, protected bays (Braulik et al., 2015). Diet records for S.

plumbea have been mainly restricted to specimens accidentally caught in shark nets along the

Kwazulu-Natal (KZN) east coast, with little data available for the south coast region. The few

records on their diet show them feeding mainly on estuarine and demersal fish (Peddemors,

1999). Their coastal habitat influences the feeding methods they adopt. This habitat means they

are often associated with large estuarine systems and harbours. Although considered a marine

dolphin species, there are a few reports of dolphins entering river systems, travelling up to 1 km

within the Swartkops and Bushmans rivers in the Eastern Cape and into the Knysna estuary in

the Western Cape (Plön et al., 2015). These sightings are often associated with brackish effluent

in the lower estuarine environment during high tides. However, in the Breede River, Western

Cape, there have been confirmed sightings and reports of S. plumbea as far as 20 km upriver (Sea

Search, unpublished data).




   MOVEMENT OF SOUSA PLUMBEA

Sousa plumbea are the most threatened marine mammal species in South Africa (Plön et al., 2015),

making it essential for researchers to conduct comprehensive and effective long-term

conservation assessments. Understanding the degree of dispersal and individual movement can

highlight ecologically sensitive areas that require protection (Gormley et al., 2012). Previous

photographic capture-recapture studies that assessed residency patterns or movement have

largely focused on discrete regional areas around South Africa, which have been inadequate to

                                                                                                  27
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




provide a comprehensive assessment of the entire population (Bouveroux et al., 2018; James et

al., 2015; Vermeulen et al., 2018) (Table 1.2). In all these assessments, connectivity between

locations was poorly understood based on the limited data available (Durham, 1994; Jobson,

2006; Karczmarski et al., 1999; Keith et al., 2013; Saayman & Tayler, 1973).



In South Africa, S. plumbea is thought to be split into two distinct populations, one off the south

coast and one off the east coast (Figure 1.1). The connectivity between these two regions is

minimal, as indicated by low encounter rates (Durham, 1994; Keith et al., 2002), few strandings

(Findlay et al., 1992), limited sightings during aerial surveys (Cockcroft et al., 1989), and

infrequent captures in shark nets along southern KZN and the Transkei region (Atkins et al.,

2013). This limited connectivity is further supported by the absence of matches in photographic

identification catalogues between Algoa Bay and KZN, regions approximately 1050 km apart,

reinforcing the existence of two geographically distinct populations (Karczmarski et al., 1999;

Vermeulen et al., 2018). The south coast population is less well studied, with most information

available from studies within specific bays, notably Algoa Bay, which was well studied in the early

1990s and again 16 years later, showing significant decreases in sightings rates, group sizes, the

number of overall identified animals and an increase in the number of solitary animals

(Bouveroux et al., 2018; Karczmarski et al., 1999; Karczmarski & Cockcroft, 1998; Koper et al.,

2016). A series of student projects in Plettenberg Bay described similar trends throughout the

region (Conry, 2017; Greenwood, 2013; Jobson, 2006) as well as Bouveroux et al.,’s (2019) study

on social networks and some early shore-based observations throughout the southeastern

coastline (Saayman & Tayler, 1973; Saayman, 1972). West of Plettenberg Bay, only a single

published estimate exists on population size in Mossel Bay (James et al., 2015) and a general

description of sighting rates and seasonality from whale-watching data (Vinding, 2016).



Only one published paper has studied the movement of S. plumbea individuals at a national scale

along the South African coastline using available photo ID data from 2000-2016 (Vermeulen et


                                                                                                28
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




al., 2018). The study found the average number of re-sightings of individuals across this period

was six for the whole distribution, decreasing to 3.3 for individuals occurring in the south coast

population only. Median range estimates for individuals were 120 km, with an overall range of

movement of 30 km to 500 km, but most movements were less than 200 km suggesting that this

is a typical home range or along-shore range. The study highlighted general 'hotspots' for the

species along the coastline. However, it indicated that, due to their reported range distance, the

protection of existing discrete marine protected areas (MPAs) may not be sufficient to protect

this species.




Figure 1.1. Sousa plumbea distribution throughout South Africa indicates two regions of
occurrence, namely, the populations of the south and east coasts. Nationally, S. plumbea have an
extreme coastal range, limited to less than 20 m water depth and not extending more than 2-5 km
off the coastline, creating high spatial use overlap with Marine Protected areas throughout their
coastal range. Spatial data sources: Environmental Systems Research Institute (ESRI, 2021),
South African National Biodiversity Institute (SANBI, 2021), and IUCN Red List Assessment
(Braulik et al., 2015).

   SOCIALITY IN SOUSA PLUMBEA

Group structures within cetaceans range from stable matriarchal family pods, as seen in orcas

(Orcinus orca) (Deecke et al., 2010), to the variable and complex fission-fusion groups of

bottlenose dolphins (Tursiops spp.), where group membership can change every 10-20 mins

(Connor & Krützen, 2015; Galezo et al., 2018). The drivers of fission-fusion assortment are based

on the benefits and constraints of the group over time and space (Aureli et al., 2008). However,

group structure within this is far more complex, and it is essential to understand the variability

in individual relationships within a group, which are key to social complexity (Hunt et al., 2019).

It has been shown that the same individuals can display different social structures in different

conditions, for example, when they compete to increase their reproductive success (Gowans,

2019). Aureli et al., (2008) demonstrated that individual variation in habitat, affiliations and

group size are essential factors to consider in assessing the social structure of a population.



In S. plumbea, there has been relatively little published research regarding their social

organisation (Bouveroux et al., 2019; Karczmarski et al., 1999; Saayman & Tayler, 1973). In South

Africa, only 10s of animals have been identified a handful of times each in previous studies

(Bouveroux et al., 2019; James et al., 2015; Koper et al., 2016; Vermeulen et al., 2018) (Table 1.2).

In Algoa Bay S. plumbea had social bonds that were fluid and mostly of short-duration affiliations

(Karczmarski et al., 1999). A more recent study in the Plettenberg Bay area (Bouveroux et al.,

2019), detected two occurrences of stronger pairwise affiliations. Bouveroux et al., (2019)

demonstrated a generally low level of gregariousness, with a well-differentiated social network

with half the sampled dolphins (14 of 29 seen three or more times) showing strong social bonds

between individuals, mostly as pairs. They suggest that the higher social bonds seen in this study

could be a result of the decreasing group sizes and overall population in the area.




                                                                                                  31
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Along the south coast of South Africa, a substantial decrease in the average group size of S.

plumbea has been observed in several locations. For example, in Plettenberg Bay, average group

sizes dropped from seven to four (Conry, 2017; Jobson, 2006), while in Algoa Bay, the average

group size dropped from seven to three (Bouveroux et al., 2018; Karczmarski et al., 1999).

General sociality in S. plumbea in South Africa is thought to be reflective of their small group sizes

and an increase in the number of solitary individuals (Koper et al., 2016), limiting the number of

potential associations (Gowans et al., 2007).




   CURRENT CONSERVATION STATUS OF SOUSA PLUMBEA IN SOUTH AFRICA

Research on S. plumbea in South Africa since the late 1960s (see review by Elwen et al., 2011) has

covered a wide range of topics, including socio-ecology (Karczmarski et al., 1999; Koper et al.,

2016; Saayman, 1972), diet (Barros & Cockroft, 1999), anatomy (Frainer et al., 2019; Plön et al.,

2012), regional distribution, habitat use, and movements (Atkins et al., 2005; Bouveroux et al.,

2018; Koper et al., 2016; Vermeulen et al., 2018), local population abundance estimates (James et

al., 2015; Keith et al., 2002), vulnerability to direct human impacts such as bycatch in shark nets

(Atkins et al., 2013, 2016; Cockcroft, 1990, 1994), vessel traffic (Karczmarski et al., 1997; Koper

et al., 2016) and health assessment, and pollutant loads (Aznar-Alemany et al., 2019; Cockcroft,

1999; Gui et al., 2016; Lane et al., 2014). However, these regional studies are limited in their

capacity to provide insights into broader population health and dynamics. These studies have

provided crucial baseline information and identified potential threats to the species. Still, they

lack key information, including a reliable national abundance estimate, which is necessary to

document future population trends.



In South Africa, although no empirical data are available on population trends at a broader

national scale, Koper et al., (2016) and Bouveroux et al., (2018) suggested a significant decline in

the frequency of occurrence, average group size, number of calves and the number of identified

                                                                                                   32
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




individuals when compared to a similar study in the early 1990s in Algoa Bay (Karczmarski et al.,

1999). For Plettenberg Bay, a decrease in population size of 50% between 2002 and 2012 has

been suggested, although this was based on limited sampling effort (Greenwood, 2013). In

Richard's Bay, shark net mortalities have contributed to an unsustainable loss of S. plumbea (4.3

individuals or 5% - 10% of the population per year; (Atkins et al., 2013, 2016). These data

strongly indicate an overall population decline along South Africa’s coastline. Considering these

inferred population declines, S. plumbea was up-listed to “Endangered” in South Africa on the

IUCN Red List (Plön et al., 2015). This was based on an estimated extent of occurrence of < 5,000

km², a continuing decline in habitat quality (and likely area of occupancy) in key resource areas

correlating with coastal development, the fragmentation of populations, and continuing adult

mortality from anthropogenic disturbances (especially shark-nets; Plön et al., 2015). A quarter of

the photographed South African population has been recorded with injured or abnormal

rostrums, possibly caused by either fishing line entanglements, extreme feeding behaviours or

genetic depression, leading to apparent conditions such as broken or misaligned jaws (Frainer et

al., 2023). Most observed individuals were adults; therefore, it is difficult to know the extent of

such impact on the population, as it is unknown how many juveniles did not survive these injuries

or abnormalities. However, the wide prevalence throughout the South African range is likely

driven by their small, fragmented population (Frainer et al., 2023).



Overall, there are limited population estimates throughout the species' full South African range.

As a result, population trends across their range must often be inferred by assessing the extent of

mortality or the degree of decline in relative sighting rates or group sizes. However, where data

are available to evaluate changes over time, there are clear indications of significant declines in

abundance, relative sighting rates, and/or group sizes. The reported levels of threats due to their

restricted habitat and high reported mortality in portions of their range means that S. plumbea

population size is almost definitely decreasing in South Africa (Braulik et al., 2015).




                                                                                                33
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   STUDY LOCATION: SOUTH AFRICA

South Africa has a long, diverse coastline spanning 2,798 km, from rocky reefs to sandy beaches

and estuaries. Mixing between the warmer Agulhas current and the cool Benguela current creates

dynamic ocean currents, causing highly fluctuating water temperatures. The most westerly range

of S. plumbea is at False Bay, at the most south-westerly tip of the continent, with no range

extension into the westerly cooler Benguela region. The mean water temperatures along S.

plumbea’s coastal distribution range from 19 °C – 26 °C in the northeast, while the cooler south

coast region has mean water temperatures of 14 °C – 20 °C (Dallas, 2008). Several major estuaries

occur along the coastline, with many under regular management (Whitfield et al., 2012). Estuaries

have been shown to be a likely habitat for the species, overlapping with many anthropogenic

impacts, with some individuals being seen upriver in brackish, estuarine regions (Plön et al.,

2015). South Africa’s coastline is highly developed, with the vast majority of the coastline affected

by human structures, including large coastal developments, active harbours, urbanisation, and

development in estuaries (Claassens et al., 2023). However, a significant section of the S. plumbea

range in the Western Cape falls within protected areas such as De Hoop, Stilbaai, Goukamma,

Robberg, and Tsikamma marine protected areas (Mann-Lang et al., 2021) (Figure 1.1).




                                                                                                  34
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




    THE SOUSA CONSORTIUM

A lack of scientific knowledge has been recognised as a major threat to S. plumbea conservation

actions in South Africa, with many aspects of the species’ life history and current changes to its

population poorly understood. Given the lack of data on this species, a nationwide research

collaboration, the SouSA consortium, was established in May 2016 (Vermeulen et al., 2018). The

consortium aims to address the need for more knowledge on the conservation biology of S.

plumbea in South Africa nationally and work as a collective group to achieve these aims. The

consortium was recognised at the World Marine Mammal Conference in 2019 as a leading

example in collaboration (S. Dines Judges Discretionary Award) and has collectively published

several publications on S. plumbea, collating data at a national scale (Frainer et al., 2023; Plön et

al., 2021; Vermeulen et al., 2018). The SouSA consortium is currently working with public, private

and international stakeholders, such as the IUCN SOS group, to develop an effective biodiversity

action plan for S. plumbea in South Africa. In 2024, the Indian Ocean Humpback Dolphin

Conservation Network (HuDoNET) was established. HuDoNET was modelled on the successful

initiatives of the Conservation Consortium of the Atlantic humpback dolphin (CCAHD) and the

SouSA Consortium, with the aim to develop a build connections and collaboration among

stakeholders throughout the species’ range to encourage coordinated conservation actions

(Atkins et al., 2024).




                                                                                                  35
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




        THESIS OVERVIEW: CONCEPTUAL THESIS FRAMEWORK


The limitations of visual and olfactory senses in the marine environment have led to the evolution

of sophisticated acoustic communication systems in cetaceans (Herman & Tavolga, 1980).

Dolphins are known for their high vocal activity, producing a variety of acoustic signals, including

frequency-modulated whistles used for communication (Jones et al., 2020). Among these,

signature whistles are unique signals that are highly stable and stereotyped, serving as

identification markers and contact calls in specific contexts (Caldwell & Caldwell, 1965; Sayigh et

al., 2007). Evidence for signature whistle use has been presented across several species of

odontocetes (see review in Janik, 2009), including preliminary evidence in Sousa spp. (Cheng et

al., 2017; Gridley et al., Appendix 1; Van Parijs & Corkeron, 2001a). This thesis aims to understand

the whistling behaviour of S. plumbea in South Africa and explore how signature whistles can be

effectively used for acoustic monitoring of this elusive coastal species. Here, I present a

conceptual framework for my thesis (Figure 1.2). The framework describes each data chapter

(Chapters 2-5) and the main themes within. The figure presents how the chapters are linked, from

investigating general whistle characteristics and variability across spatial scales in Chapter 2 to a

focus on the behavioural effects on signature whistle production in Chapter 3. In Chapter 4, the

use of passive acoustic monitoring to recapture signature whistles is compared to a more

standard approach of boat-based photo ID over a short field study period. Finally, individual

detection over time and space for capture-recapture models is investigated in Chapter 5. Using

signature whistles for occurrence, recapture and density (SWORD, Fearey et al., 2021), the thesis

provides baseline data on how individual passive acoustic monitoring can best be utilised for this

threatened species.




                                                                                                  36
                                                               https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Figure 1.2. Thesis overview diagram to demonstrate the conceptual framework of the thesis. The framework shows each data chapter (2-5),
including chapter themes and datasets used and how they build on one another throughout the thesis.

    2 MICRO AND MACROGEOGRAPHIC VARIATION WITHIN THE
    WHISTLE REPERTOIRE OF INDIAN OCEAN HUMPBACK
    DOLPHINS, SOUSA PLUMBEA

                                                                                                48
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   ABSTRACT
Understanding geographic variation in a species’ acoustic repertoire can provide information on
the degrees of mixing, isolation and plasticity in species that vocally learn. This is the first study
to describe the acoustic behaviour and geographic variation of Sousa plumbea whistles across
different geographic scales. Acoustic characteristics of whistle contours were compared from the
extreme west and east of the species' global range (South Africa and Maharashtra, India). In South
Africa, 1181 whistle contours were analysed from 21 encounters across five different study sites
(19 hrs 17 mins of recordings), spanning two geographically isolated regions across the south
and east coasts. In India, 2260 whistle contours were analysed from 11 encounters in one region
(3 hrs 6 mins of recordings). Contours were described based on seven temporal and spectral
measurements. Comparisons were made between 1) South Africa and India (macrogeographic
variation) and 2) between study sites in South Africa, the south and east coast (macrogeographic
variation), and between south coast study sites (microgeographic variation). Principal
component analysis (PCA) was used as a data reduction technique to visualize the collinear
relationships among the temporal and frequency whistle characteristics. Random forest
algorithms (RF) were then used to investigate fine-scale differences in whistle measurements
across groups at different geographical scales. There was considerable overlap in the acoustic
properties of whistles from S. plumbea recorded at their geographical range limits. However,
overall classification with random forest was good (89.7% overall accuracy). In South Africa,
classification to region was achieved with moderate success (64.9% overall classification). On
inspection, Richards Bay (east coast) was distinct from the south coast study sites and whistles
contours were classified with high accuracy (90% classification rate). Across the four south coast
study sites, there was also considerable overlap and no evidence of isolation through distance.
Overall, the whistle characteristics of S. plumbea recorded in South Africa were within the species
range for most acoustic parameters, but occasionally exceeded the maximum frequency
previously documented. The high degree of overlap of whistle features and low classification
rates across the south coast sites indicate little microgeographic variation within the south coast
of South Africa. Although there were whistle features that allowed for a degree of classification
across these geographic scales, there were broad species-specific characteristics conserved
across their range, which could assist with species ID classification.




                                                                                                   49
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   INTRODUCTION

Acoustic signals serve a crucial communicative function within animal societies, providing

information about the signaller's identity, size, health, reproductive fitness (static information),

or information on external factors such as predator threats and senders' motivational state

(dynamic information) (Erbe & Thomas, 2022). The transfer of information within these signals

between individuals is strongly linked to survival and reproductive strategies. Variation in these

acoustic signals can be seen between and within populations and has been documented in taxa,

including frogs (Morais et al., 2012), birds (Hamao et al., 2016), insects (Pinto-Juma et al., 2005)

and mammals (Lameira et al., 2010).



Acoustic variation can result from genetic differences (Collins & Terhune, 2007), environmental

effects (see acoustic adaption hypothesis Morton, 1975) and learning (Garland et al., 2020).

Morphological features of the vocal apparatus can drive variation, along with an individual's age

and body size (Fischer et al., 2004). Properties of the environment can also promote acoustic

variation due to the influences of sound propagation to limit signal degradation (Morton, 1975).

Closed habitats, such as forests, with a high density of vegetation have a greater number of

surfaces for reverberation and absorption of signals. Here, birds adapt to concentrate energy in

lower frequency, narrow band sounds for better propagation of signals (Dabelsteen et al., 2005;

Ey & Fischer, 2009). Vocal production learning is the ability to produce new sounds by reference

to auditory feedback and is an important consideration for acoustic variation, documented in six

animal groups (Humans, bats, cetaceans, pinnipeds, elephants and birds including songbirds,

parrots and hummingbirds; see Petkov & Jarvis, 2012). As individuals mix across social groups,

signals can converge or differ from more distant individuals (Tyack & Sayigh, 1997). This leads to

the behavioural matching of acoustic signals, accrued through copying or improvisation (King &

McGregor, 2016).




                                                                                                 50
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Intra-specifically, acoustic variation between populations can be distinguished at two spatial

scales: macro- and microgeographic variation (Mundinger & Rumbaugh, 1982). Macrogeographic

variation occurs over long distances and between geographically separated populations (Krebs

& Kroodsma, 1980). In delphinids, for example, killer whales (Orcinus orca) in the North Pacific

and Northeast Atlantic show macrogeographic variation in high-frequency whistle features

between populations with little to no mixing of individuals (Samarra et al., 2015).

Microgeographic variation is defined as variation between populations with the potential for

mixing in a single or near-continuous population (Krebs & Kroodsma, 1980). For example, Rossi-

Santos and Podos, (2006) demonstrated evidence of microgeographic variation in the whistles of

the estuarine dolphin, Sotalia fluviatilis, with higher start and minimum frequencies of whistles

to the north of their Brazilian coastal range and greatest differences occurring between the most

distant sites, highlighting discontinuity in acoustic behaviour along a seemingly continuous range.




Delphinid acoustic signals can be categorised into clicks and whistles (including burst pulse

sounds), with differences in levels of variation between these types (Janik, 2009). Clicks, often

produced in click trains, are short signals which can reach very high-frequency ranges, are often

used for echolocation, and can also serve a communicative function in some species (Arranz et

al., 2016). Burst pulses are rapid click trains used for short-range and long-distance

communication (Deecke et al., 2000; Lammers et al., 2006). Whistles are frequency-modulated

tonal sounds, mostly used as communicative signals (Popper, 1980). Whereas clicks are innate

and vary little between individuals and within a species (though, for an example of click variation

in white-sided dolphins, see Soldevilla et al., 2010), whistles are acquired through vocal

production learning (Fripp et al., 2005) and can vary across geographic scales, especially in

isolated or disjunct populations (Janik, 2009).



The geographic variation in whistle diversity within a species can be investigated in multiple

ways. Studies can investigate acoustic variation using amplitude, measuring spectral density

                                                                                                51
                                          https://scholar.sun.ac.za




(Morisaka et al., 2005) or changes to the frequency modulation curves of contours (Rendell et al.,

1999). Spectral and temporal features of whistle contours provide information on the acoustic

characteristics of the species, with standardised measurements allowing for comparison across

studies (Bazúa-Durán et al., 2002; Gridley et al., 2012; Morisaka et al., 2005; Oswald et al., 2003;

Rendell et al., 1999) In dolphins, whistles are often characterised by the start and end frequency,

minimum and maximum frequency, as well as the duration of signal and the number of inflection

points, as these features are thought to carry information on species-specific signalling (Gridley

et al., 2015; Jones et al., 2020; Morisaka et al., 2005).




Sousa plumbea are listed as globally endangered on the IUCN Red List, with a global range from

the Bay of Bengal, India, furthest east through to False Bay, South Africa, furthest west (Braulik et

al., 2015). In South Africa, they are also listed as locally endangered, with a population of fewer

than 500 individuals (Plön et al., 2016; Vermeulen et al., 2018). The species theoretically ranges

from False Bay in the west (~ 18.47 ˚E) to the eastern South Africa-Mozambique border (~ 32.90

˚E) but with animals split into two geographically distinct populations, one along the south coast

and the other in KwaZulu-Natal (KZN) on the east coast, mostly centred around Richards Bay

(Keith et al., 2013). A clear gap in sightings and strandings along a 245 km exposed rocky coast of

the “Wild Coast” of the Eastern Cape and southern KZN coastline indicates individuals are unlikely

to regularly cross this area, especially when considering the species median range of 120 km

(Vermeulen et al., 2018). This has led to the ‘two - population hypothesis’ (Braulik et al., 2015;

Caputo et al., 2020; Plön et al., 2015; Vermeulen et al., 2018) that these south and east coasts may

represent different sub-populations and, consequently, management units (Plön et al., 2021). Yet,

a recent genetic study based on samples collected from 1979 - 2017, comparing the two proposed

subpopulations, found extremely low mitochondrial diversity, suggesting that South African

humpback dolphins form a single genetic population (Lampert et al., 2021). However,

behavioural adaptations often proceed genetics, especially with mtDNA and other regional

differences in acoustic signals, which may facilitate intraspecific recognition (Tibbetts & Dale,

                                                                                                  52
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




2007). However, this has yet to be explored in S. plumbea along their South African range.

Acoustic variation has only previously been explored in other Sousa spp., with Hoffman et al.,

(2015) finding variation in S. chinensis frequency variables between two locations along the

western peninsular Malaysia coastline, suggesting the differences in whistles may be related to

adaptations to the local acoustic habitat or unique whistles developing due to social interactions

within each location. Monitoring of acoustic behaviour in S. plumbea can provide information on

behavioural adaptation at timescales faster than genetics and may be useful to help elucidate

isolation between these two populations in South Africa as well as behavioural barriers to

interbreeding.




This study compares the acoustic characteristics of S. plumbea whistles across geographic ranges

to investigate the degree of variation in their whistle features. It investigates variation between

South Africa and India, two populations at the furthest western and eastern limits of the species'

global distribution, and between populations within South Africa to investigate levels of both

macro and microgeographic variation.




                                                                                                53
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Hypotheses


   1) Variation between South Africa and India.

       – The highest level of variation will be seen between these two populations due to

      macrogeographic variation (no mixing between these two geographically isolated

      populations)

   2) Variation within South Africa.


      – The south and east coast study sites will show macrogeographic variation due to the

      lack of continuous mixing of individuals between these two populations, supporting a

      two-population hypothesis in South Africa.


       – The least variation will be seen amongst the four south coast study sites (False Bay,

      Struisbaai, St Sebastian Bay, Mossel Bay), which have known mixing of individuals along

      a continuous range and, therefore will show no or little microgeographic variation.




                                                                                            54
                                          https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   METHODS

Data collection

In South Africa, data were collected from five study sites (four south coast sites: False Bay,

Struisbaai, St Sebastian Bay and Mossel Bay and one east coast site, Richards Bay) between 2016-

2023 (Table 2.1). Recordings were collected during boat-based surveys focused on dedicated S.

plumbea encounters (south coast survey site distance False Bay – Mossel Bay 140 km and distance

from Mossel Bay – Richards Bay 1260 km). Data from India were collected along the Sindhudurg

coast of Maharashtra, the eastern species range during transects covering a distance of 121 km.

(Bopardikar et al., 2018) (Figure 2.1).



In South Africa, locations were chosen based on the known S. plumbea range and sightings from

previous studies (Keith et al., 2013; James et al., 2015; Vermeulen et al., 2018). Boat surveys were

conducted during daylight hours with favourable weather conditions within 5 km of the shoreline

(Beaufort sea state ≤ 3, swell < 2 meters). The boats used were either a 10 m Butt Cat with a raised

viewing platform for observation (Mossel Bay), a 6.2 m Rigid Hulled Inflatable Boat (RHIB) (False

Bay, Struisbaai, St Sebastian Bay) or a 5.5 m RHIB (Richards Bay), all with four-stroke engines for

minimal animal disturbance from engine noise or boat proximity. Encounters began when S.

plumbea were first sighted within 50 m of the boat. The boat approached from behind or to the

side of the group, and for acoustic recording the engines and echosounder were turned off when

safe to do so, and the boat drifted alongside or towards the group of dolphins. In some events, due

to the proximity to the surf zone, the engine would be kept idle or the boat would slowly move

alongside the group (less than 5 knots) while the hydrophone was still in the water. Acoustic

recordings were made using a SoundTrap 300 HF (flat frequency response of 20 Hz - 150 kHz ±

3 dB; Ocean Instruments Inc., New Zealand), or HTI-96-MIN hydrophones (flat frequency

response of 2 Hz – 30 kHz ± 1 dB; High Tech Inc., U.S.) attached to a TASCAM DR 680 recorder

(TASCAM, U.S.) (Table 2.1). An acoustic encounter started when the hydrophone was placed in

the water and continued until the dolphins were out of acoustic range ( < 1 km Jensen et al., 2012).

                                                                                                 55
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




During acoustic encounters, data on group sizes and surface behaviours were recorded to ensure

that whistle repertoire was sampled over various behavioural contexts and group compositions

(Table 2.2). During acoustic encounters, continuous scans of other dolphin species and S. plumbea

groups were made within visual range to ensure the recording data was true to the focal group.

Data used in this study were restricted to single-species recordings (i.e. visual confirmation of

species during the encounter and secondary assessment of species within recordings (e.g.

presence of SNR 1 Delphinus delphis removed whole encounter) to reduce any effects of

interspecific communication.



In India, data were collected during visual surveys conducted in the months of May and October

2014, December 2015, and January 2016 using a small, 11-meter-long survey vessel, within 5 km

of the shoreline. All recordings were made only during daylight hours and low sea states

(Beaufort ≤ 3) over 121 km of coastline. A Sensor Technology SQ26-08 hydrophone (Sensor

Technology Ltd., Collingwood, Canada) with a sensitivity of -169 dB re 1 V/μPa and a M-Audio

Track II (Cetacean Research Technology Inc., Seattle, WA) was used to collect recordings in May

and October 2014. The same hydrophone was connected to a Zoom H1 handheld recorder (Zoom

Corp., Tokyo, Japan) to collect acoustic data in December 2015 and January 2016. The

hydrophone model had a flat frequency response (62 dB) in the relevant frequency range (1 – 34

kHz). All recordings were made using a sampling rate of 96 kHz and 24-bit resolution in the

standard WAV file format. For each recording, the hydrophone was deployed at a depth of 1.5 –

2.0 m, and the engine of the survey vessel was turned off to minimise interference with the

acoustic recordings.

                                     https://scholar.sun.ac.za

Figure 2.1. Sousa plumbea study sites distributed throughout South Africa and India. A) The red dots
indicate encounters where Sousa. plumbea were recorded. B) The Indian population were recorded
throughout the Maharashtra region (Bopardikar et al., 2018). C) The South African population were
recorded in both the south and east coast regions. Within South Africa, five separate study sites were
included, including four on the south coast (West to East: False Bay, Struisbaai, St Sebastian Bay and
Mossel Bay) and one on the east coast (Richards Bay), as indicated by yellow circles on the figure.




                                                                                            57
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Data processing


South African data were visually analysed in Raven Pro v1.6 (Center for Conservation

Bioacoustics, 2019) for S. plumbea whistle contours. Whistle contours were defined as continuous

frequency-modulated tonal signals (Janik et al., 2013). The fast Fourier transform (FFT)

parameters used to examine the data were 1024 point Hann window and 90% overlap. Contours

were graded according to a visual assessment of each contour's signal-to-noise ratio (SNR). SNR

was assigned from 1 – 3, 1) the signal was faint or partially masked but visible on the spectrogram

2) the signal was clear and unambiguous and 3) the signal was prominent and dominated the

spectrogram (Gridley et al., 2012). Only good-quality contours (those graded SNR 2 or 3, not

masked) were used in further analysis. Contours were manually annotated, and parameters from

the lowest frequency signal, also known as the fundamental frequency, were measured in Raven

Pro v1.6 (Table 2.3 and Figure 2.2a). A comparable approach to analysis was undertaken for

acoustic data collected from S. Plumbea in India by Bopardikar et al., (2018), enabling comparison

between sites. Cross-checking between manual acoustic analysis (S. Dines and I. Bopardikar) was

conducted to ensure consistency in approaches. Although many parameters can be measured

from acoustic signals in Raven Pro v1.6, several are considered standard in whistle acoustic

analysis, and these were chosen to compare between sites (seven whistle parameters) (Figure

2.2b). To ensure data quality, only contours considered good SNR (2 or 3) were used from South

Africa and > 6 dB signal-to-noise threshold in India (Table 2.1). A description of these

measurements followed comparable studies of the genus Sousa, and include minimum, maximum,

median (with interquartile range expressed as Q1 – Q3), mean (standard deviation), and

coefficient of variation (CoV = (SD/mean) * 100) to describe inter and intra-population variability

(Bopardikar et al., 2018; Dong et al., 2019; Yuan et al., 2021). Ultrasonic frequencies were

recorded and classed as contours with maximum frequencies > 20 kHz (Oswald et al., 2004;

Samarra et al., 2010).

Statistical analysis


Statistical analyses were conducted using R (version 4.0.2; R Core Team, 2020) to investigate

whistle variability 1) between South Africa (data between all five study sites pooled) and India,

and 2) within South Africa, between all five study sites and between south and east coast regions.




As an initial step, the seven whistle measurements were tested for collinearity at both the regional

and site level, with high collinearity values detected between nearly all the frequency-based

measurements (r = > 0.7). Due to the high collinearity between frequency characteristics, a

principal component analysis (PCA) was applied to the data (R function: prcomp, package: stats)

as a data reduction technique to visualize the collinear relationships among the temporal and

frequency whistle characteristics. The PCA converted parameter measurements into

uncorrelated axes called principal components (PCs), with each successive PC accounting for as

much variance as possible in the dataset. PCA assumptions (linear relation between variables,

sampling adequacy, and absence of outliers) were verified. PCA does not require balanced sample

sizes across different groups as it is a dimensionality reduction technique that works on the entire

dataset.




Random forest algorithms (RF) were then used to investigate fine-scale differences in whistle

measurements across groups at different geographical scales. RF is a supervised classification

method based on averaging multiple decision trees (Breiman, 2001). Using a tuned process, RF

generated trees by randomly subsampling training data (75% for each model). Precision was

assessed by the number of correct predictions per group (i.e. whistle contours assigned to the

correct site or population, expressed as a percentage) and compared to the prediction explained

by chance, Cohen's Kappa statistic. The global accuracy of the model precision is calculated as a

proportion of the total correct classification from an RF procedure. Random forest algorithms

were conducted using the package randomForest (Breiman, 2001). The number of trees was set

                                                                                                 64
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




to 1000 because the global error rate generally stabilised from this value. The number of variables

selected at each node was set at the square root of the total number of variables included in the

RF. To understand the contribution of each variable to the model prediction, the Gini variable

importance index was used (Breiman, 2001) and to understand what variables provided more

accuracy to the model the mean decrease in accuracy was used. The higher the value of mean

decrease accuracy or mean decrease Gini score, the higher the importance of the variable in the

model (Breiman, 2001). Both levels of geographic variation investigation were performed using

this general method.




The RF was conducted on the full datasets from South Africa and India. RF analysis between the

five South African study sites was conducted on the full whistle contour data sets for each site,

except for Mossel Bay, which had a significantly higher sample size. Therefore, Mossel Bay was

randomly subsampled to 155 contours based on the mean full sample sizes of the other four study

sites to provide a more equal sample size across sites and to avoid overfitting the model based on

Mossel Bay (R package tidyverse; dplyr Hadley & Garrett, 2016).




                                                                                                65
                                          https://scholar.sun.ac.za




    RESULTS

In total, 207 boat-based surveys were conducted in South Africa from 2016 to 2023. Of these, S.

plumbea were encountered on 83, i.e. 40% of occasions, and acoustic data were available for 64

(77%) of these. However, removing encounters where dolphins were silent, undetected, or

potentially mixed with other species dramatically reduced usable data. Acoustic data from 21

(25%) encounters were subsequently used in this study. In total, 2298 contours were identified

from the five study sites in South Africa, with 1181 (51%) of these visually assessed as good

quality (SNR 2 or 3) and used for further analysis. Contours analysed from along the Sindhudurg

coast, India, totalled 2260 across 11 encounters (Table 2.4). There were notable differences

between group dynamics observed between both South African and Indian encounters. In South

Africa, S. plumbea were recorded in small single species groups with sizes of 1-10 individuals,

occurring with several sympatric whistling delphinid species (Tursiops spp. and D. delphinus)

(Frainer et al., 2023) as well as predation threats from species such as white sharks (Carcharodon

carcharias) along their distribution (Jewell et al., 2014; Plön et al., 2021). In India, S. plumbea were

recorded in larger group sizes (up to a maximum group size of 110, Table 2.2), recorded in single

species groups and occurring only with the sympatric finless porpoise (Neophocaena

phocaenoides), a non-whistling species (Morisaka, 2012).

South Africa and India


Contours produced by S. plumbea in South Africa had a larger mean frequency range than India

(South Africa = 4.9 (± 2.7) kHz, India = 2.7 (± 2.2) kHz) (Figure 2.5). The maximum frequency

contour recorded in South Africa reached 46.6 kHz, more than 13 kHz higher than the maximum

frequency contour from India (India Max Freq = 33 kHz). The prevalence of ultrasonic frequency

contours (> 20 kHz) was low across both populations. However, South Africa had a greater

proportion of ultrasonic contours, contributing to 3.2% of contours in South Africa compared to

1.1% in India. In general, most contours from S. plumbea were short (< 1 sec), with only 4.7% of

contours exceeding 1 sec duration in South Africa and 0.7%. in India. Chirps (contours with a

duration of < 0.1s) were most prevalent in India (41%) compared to South Africa (7.3%) and in

both populations the majority of contours were produced with 1-2 inflection points (South Africa

= 88.7%, India = 85.2%). Complex contours, with four or more inflection points were more

prevalent in India (India = 10%, South Africa = 3.7%) (Figure 2.3).

The first two principal components in the comparison between South Africa and India accounted

for 86.1% of the variation in the contour measurements (Table 2.6). Principal component one

accounted for over half of the total variability (59.7% of variance) and had high factor loadings (>

0.5) for the maximum frequency (0.56), start and end frequency (SF 0.5, EF 0.53), representing

an effect of frequency characteristics in the first principal component (Figure 2.4). Principal

component two accounted for 26.4% of the total variance and had high factor loadings for

frequency range (-0.78) and minimum frequency (0.53).

Figure 2.4. Principal component plot showing the first two components based on Sousa plumbea
whistle contour measurements, derived from the seven measured parameters across the six pooled
study sites in South Africa and the study site in India. Data points for South Africa are in green and

variance and component two explains 26.4% of the variance.




The random forest (RF) model for South Africa and India was conducted on the full data set for

both populations (n = 3441 whistle contours). The RF was, therefore, based on a training dataset

of 2581 whistle contours and a test dataset of 860 whistle contours. The tuned RF analysis

supported a strong model performance with 89.7% model accuracy. South Africa had a high

correct classification rate of 84% (compared to a chance rate of 35.2%), and India had a correct

classification rate of 93% (chance rate of 64.8%), indicating that South Africa had a stronger

classification with a correct classification of more than 50% more than chance (Table 2.7). The

models' Cohens Kappa statistic of 77% indicates substantial agreement between the predicted




                                                                                                 72
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




and actual classification showing good model performance between the two populations. The

most important variables for classification were duration, frequency range and inflection count.

South Africa and India was 89.7%. Classification of the test data is presented in percentages (%).

classification are presented alongside observed locations.
