    1) Within South Africa

Contours from five study sites within South Africa, across two regions (south coast and east

coast), were compared to investigate microgeographic and macrogeographic variation in acoustic

behaviour across their South African range (Table 2.8). The highest maximum frequency was

recorded in Mossel Bay (46.6 kHz), and the lowest minimum frequency was recorded in False Bay

(1.4 kHz). Median start frequencies were all lower than their respective end frequencies, except

for Richards Bay (median start frequency 9.8 kHz, median end frequency 9.2 kHz). The maximum

whistle contour duration was recorded in Mossel Bay (2.1 s); however, there was little overall

variation in median duration values across the sites (0.2 -0.4 s across the five sites). The number

of inflection points was also similar across sites with median values of 0 or 1 across the sites, with

the greatest number of inflection points recorded in Mossel Bay (11) (Figure 2.5).

Although the overall prevalence was low at 3.5% of all whistle contours across South Africa, ultrasonic

contours were recorded at all five study sites and during most of the data collection period (2016 - 2021)

(Figure 2.6). The maximum recorded frequency was recorded in Mossel Bay (46.6 kHz), with Mossel Bay

and False Bay having the greatest proportion of ultrasonic whistle contours recorded (2.1% and 6.7%,

respectively). Only two contours from Richards Bay were recorded as ultrasonic. Whistle contours with

a maximum frequency of more than 30 kHz were recorded in False Bay, St Sebastian Bay and Mossel Bay.

The principal component analysis from within South Africa found the first two principal components

accounted for 88.11% of the variation in the contour measurements. The first principal component

accounted for a large proportion of the variation, 78.06% and as in the South Africa/India analysis,

the maximum frequency had a high factor loading (0.58), along with end frequency (0.52). Similarly, in

principal component two (accounting for 10.05% of variation) again, minimum frequency and frequency

range had high factor loadings (0.5 and - 0.71 respectively) (Figure 2.7).

Using the subsampled Mossel Bay data, along with the full dataset from the other study sites, the total

South African dataset used for the RF algorithm was 774 whistle contours. The random forest analysis

was, therefore, based on a training dataset of 580 whistle contours and a test dataset of 194 whistle

contours. The tuned random forest found a moderate overall accuracy for the model (65%). Correct

classification was imbalanced across South Africa, with False Bay having the lowest correct classification

rate (39%), while correct classification ranged from 58% - 68% for the other three south coast sites

(Table 2.10). Misclassification rates were high across the south coast sites with misclassification highest

in non-neighbouring sites for all four south coast sites. Richards Bay had the highest correct classification

rate at 90%. These suggest that Richards Bay contours are more distinct, while south coast sites have a

greater level of misclassification, (such as False Bay; Struisbaai (11%) and Mossel Bay (41%)). A

moderate Cohens Kappa test statistic (55%) further indicates low agreement between the predicted and

actual classes in the test dataset. Whistle characteristics with high mean decrease accuracy (i.e. maximum

frequency, end frequency and duration) contributed to differentiating between South African study sites

the most.

neighbouring study sites are included from west to east (km). Overall model accuracy was 65%.

In comparison to published literature on whistle parameters in the genus Sousa, the mean maximum

frequencies for South Africa (11.3 kHz ± 4.3 SD) are higher than S. teuszii (8.2 kHz ± 3.1 SD Weir, 2010),

and S. chinensis in China (7.26 kHz ± 4.13 SD Dong et al., 2021 and 7.69 kHz ± 3.77 SD Wang et al., 2013)

(Table 2.11). Only S. chinensis in Malaysia and S. sahulensis in Australia have reported higher mean

maximum frequencies than South Africa (13.4 kHz ± 3.7 SD Hoffman et al., 2015 and 14.38 kHz ± 14.38

SD Van Parijs & Corkeron, 2001b, 12.3 kHz ± 4.1 SD Berg Soto et al., 2014). Reported mean durations

throughout the genus are similar, ranging from 0.16 secs (S. sahulensis, Van Parijs and Cockeron, 2001b)

– 0.76 secs (S. teuszii Weir, 2010). Inflection also shows little variation across the genus with South Africa,

yet the largest mean inflection points were reported from S. plumbea (1.2 ± 1.1 SD This study and 1.8 ±

1.7 SD Bopardikar et al., 2018). General conclusions from comparisons between studies are limited due

to variations in sampling rate, scope of studies and survey effort.

   DISCUSSION
This study describes the general characteristics of Sousa plumbea whistle contours within their Indian

Ocean range (South Africa and India) and compares these between South African regions to investigate

the degree of macro and microgeographic variation within the species. The whistle contour

measurements for South Africa fall within the range of those described throughout the Sousa genus

globally. Although there is a strong overlap in variability in whistle contour time-frequency

characteristics between each range, there was evidence of macrogeographic variation between South

Africa and India and moderate evidence of macrogeographic variation between the south and east coasts

of South Africa. Whereas there was very little microgeographic variation along the south coast of South

Africa. Although there were distinctions between the populations, the strong overlap between whistle

measurements indicates characteristics constrained by morphology with acoustic behavioural

adaptations based on environmental effects.




South Africa and India are two populations at the furthest west and east of the species’ Indian Ocean

range, over 1400 km apart, showing the greatest level of distinction between the different geographic

scales. Frequency parameters (maximum frequency, start and end frequency) accounted for the highest

level of variability in the data, yet there was a strong overlap in general whistle unit measurements.

Inflection, duration and frequency range were the most important measurements for accurate

classification. Frequency parameters are more innate and species-specific, limited by morphological

constraints and therefore variability in these measurements is recorded within the range expected within

the genus (Frainer et al., 2019, Table 2.8). Inflection and duration, however, are more variable whistle

contour features, reflecting differences in whistle characteristics due to behavioural states (Esch et al.,

2009) or group composition during encounters (Ansmann et al., 2007). In South Africa, S. plumbea were

recorded in small, single species groups with groups of 1-10 individuals, while Indian group sizes were

larger, around 20-30, with a maximum group size of 110. Encounters in India were driven around

foraging groups within the lagoon and estuarine environments (Table 2.2). Higher modulations and


                                                                                                        83
                                           https://scholar.sun.ac.za




complexity in tonal sounds show a stronger correlation to group size and social structure (May-Collado

& Wartzok, 2008). Therefore, the distinctiveness between South Africa and India is likely due to

differences in group composition and behaviour driving changes to contour duration and whistle

complexity (inflection point count).




Comparison between the south and east coast sites indicated that Richards Bay was more distinct than

the south coast sites with maximum frequency an important variable for both model accuracy and

classification. This supports the two-population hypothesis in that acoustic variation is greater between

the south and east coast populations than seen within the south coast population, likely due to increased

geographical isolation. Using mtDNA, Lampert et al., (2021) have shown these two populations to form a

single population historically, however, this study was limited by a lack of samples from the south coast

and more recent genetic structuring may be resolved should other genetic markers be investigated.

Range contraction and habitat fragmentation likely split these into geographically distinct populations

(Vermeulen et al., 2018). Further population declines have continued to separate these two populations,

with little mixing of the two existing today (Caputo et al., 2017). Frequency measurements (maximum

frequency, end frequency and start frequency) and duration were important variables for group

classification. Maximum frequency also had a higher degree of variability which can be seen in the box

and whisker plots, with the maximum frequency medians the most diverse of all the whistle

measurements included. The acoustic adaptation hypothesis could explain geographic variation between

the south and east coast regions, where acoustic signals are adapted to their environment to minimise

degradation and maximise signal transmission and long-range communication (Hardt & Benedict, 2020).

Environmental factors such as water depth, temperature and salinity can all affect the transmission and

receiving of sound, which can influence the transmission of whistles (Mercado & Frazer, 1998). Richards

Bay is an enclosed bay with an industrialised harbour and is a particularly important area for S. plumbea

in KwaZulu-Natal (KZN), with sightings rates reported to be higher than in other areas in KZN (Durham,

1994; Keith et al., 2013). This could explain the differences between these south and east coast



                                                                                                      84
                                             https://scholar.sun.ac.za




populations based on the habitat characteristics such as noise and depth that differ between these

regions. Maximum frequencies were one of the strongest discriminating variables between the two

regions. Populations that frequent shallower waters, with little wave action, have shown acoustic

adaptation to favour higher frequency parameters (Forrest et al., 1993). Water temperature and salinity,

although not measured within this study, likely also affect whistle transmission, with Richards Bay

situated in a subtropical region along the east coast with warmer waters and higher salinity than the

south coast (Van Niekerk et al., 2022). As well as habitat differences, the presence of boat activity has

shown to cause dolphins to modify their vocalisations and acoustic behaviour (May-Collado & Quiñones-

Lebrón, 2014; Perez-Ortega et al., 2021). Delphinids can adapt to underwater noise by 1) increasing

source levels, or 2) shifting frequency or temporal parameters of signals to avoid masking of signals

(Tyack & Janik, 2013). Boat activity has been seen to cause an upward shift in whistle frequency in the

presence of boats (Heiler et al., 2016; Papale et al., 2015; Perez-Ortega et al., 2021). Guerra et al., (2014)

also found bottlenose dolphins shifted to shorter whistle durations in the presence of boats when calves

were absent from the group. Likely the high use of the harbour environment and habitat characteristics

contributed to the distinctiveness between the east and south coast populations.




Within South Africa, the known mixing of individuals along the south coast likely explains the lack of

microgeographic variation, supported by the acoustic similarity seen amongst south coast study sites.

Individuals are known to move between False Bay and Mossel Bay (Vermeulen et al., 2018), with the

same individuals detected across multi-year encounters (presented in Appendix 1). The known detection

of individuals between south coast study sites may have been a cause for increased similarity between

these sites with certain repeated contours driving levels of similarity between sites. This could be through

both recording the same individuals or in that they share an acoustic environment, leading to vocal

learning of their whistle features (Janik & Slater, 1997). Vermeulen et al., (2018) investigated movements

of S. plumbea in their South African coastal range using photo ID and found dolphins were observed

moving throughout the south coastline, but most matches were found in the central area between Mossel



                                                                                                           85
                                            https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Bay and Plettenberg Bay, a site further east. The lack of south coast study sites further east of Mossel Bay,

restricts this study's ability to investigate the effects of this further. This degree of movement could

explain the higher misclassification rate amongst south coast sites, yet the degree of misclassification of

south study sites with Richards Bay indicates a degree of overlap in general throughout the two

populations, likely due to general species-specific whistle features.




In describing whistle contour characteristics throughout the South African population of S. plumbea, the

study recorded ultrasonic whistles across their South African range, exceeding the maximum frequencies

published across other studies within the Sousa genus. High-frequency contour production is a

characteristic across the genus with ultrasonic frequencies (>20 kHz) in species such as S. teuszii (Weir,

2010), S. chinensis (Dong et al., 2019; Sims et al., 2012) and S. sahulensis (Van Parijs & Corkeron, 2001b).

These results extend this range to S. plumbea’s furthest westerly range in South Africa, and the study

reports the highest frequency of Sousa whistles to date. Although infrequent, high-frequency contours (>

40 kHz) were recorded more frequently in South Africa than India and recorded from all five sites in

South Africa. The maximum recorded contour frequency reached 46.6 kHz and was recorded in Mossel

Bay in 2019. Morphologically, Sousa plumbea has a specialised structure next to the melon's small left

posterior branch, associated with higher frequencies and sound directionality (Frainer et al., 2019). This

structure is present in several other species that also feature high-frequency characteristics, including

Spinner dolphins (Stenella longirostris), Risso’s dolphins (Grampus griseus), white-beaked dolphins

(Lagenorhynchus albirostris), and Franciscana dolphins (Pontoporia blainvillei) (Frainer et al., 2019).

Equipment limitations and limited encounter durations in previous studies may mean these upper

bandwidth capabilities are widespread within this genus but were either not recorded (Schultz &

Corkeron, 1994; Van Parijs & Corkeron, 2001b) or investigated across other studies (Sims et al., 2012;

Zbinden et al., 1977). Although ultrasonic capabilities are present across the genus, general frequency

parameters vary, with S. chinensis in China (Dong et al., 2019; Sims et al., 2012; Wang et al., 2013) having

lower mean maximum frequencies (7.69 kHz – 7.83 kHz) than S. chinensis in Malaysia (11.8 kHz – 13.4



                                                                                                          86
                                              https://scholar.sun.ac.za




kHz, (Hoffman et al., 2015). Ultrasonic whistles have been recorded in other species, with whistles

ranging up to 75 kHz in Northeast Atlantic killer whales (Orcinus orca) (Samarra et al., 2010), with the

occurrence of these ultrasonic whistles attributed to both social and non-social behaviour or random

individual variation (May-Collado & Wartzok, 2008; Samarra et al., 2015). Ultrasonic whistles in the

freshwater dolphin, the Boto (Inia geoffrensis geoffrensis), with maximum frequencies up to 48.10 kHz,

are produced within groups when engaged in social or feeding activities (May-Collado & Wartzok, 2007).

Due to available recording equipment, the presence of ultrasonic whistles throughout South Africa is

likely a common feature within the genus’ morphological capabilities, with limited detection in previous

studies. Further investigation is needed into the behavioural and group composition around the

production of these whistles in S. plumbea.




The limited number of S. plumbea encounters and variation in sampling efforts across sites likely limited

the degree of variation seen across South Africa. Across 207 surveys, S. plumbea was encountered on 83

occasions, with acoustic data available for 64, which was further reduced to 21 encounters based on the

quality of acoustic recordings (including only using single-species encounters). The thresholds used to

include acoustic encounters in the study may have diminished the diversity of acoustic encounters.

Variation in the acoustic repertoire is known to be influenced by group size and behaviours in other

species (Bazúa-Durán & Au, 2002; May-Collado & Wartzok; 2008). Overall, very high research effort

provided data where only a quarter of encounters provided adequate acoustic recordings, and only ~

50% of whistles recorded were suitable for further analysis. Low-quality whistle contours were present

in the data due to low signal-to-noise ratios of contours (distant individuals, low amplitude calls) and

signal masking due to snapping shrimp, reef noise and ambient noise (such as bouy chains). Therefore,

applying a minimum threshold for contours was necessary to ensure the accuracy of the data. This high

effort, mixed with the threshold for whistle contour quality, demonstrates the substantial effort required

to research this endangered species and is likely a contributing factor to the limited data available on

their acoustic repertoire throughout their distribution.



                                                                                                       87
                                           https://scholar.sun.ac.za




In conclusion, this is the first study to investigate geographical variation in whistle contours from S.

plumbea at both macro and microgeographic scales in South Africa and India. Results indicate a degree

of increasing variation with increasing geographic isolation throughout the study range. The greatest

variation between South Africa and India was observed in frequency measurements, particularly in the

maximum recorded frequencies. The least variation was demonstrated amongst the four south coast sites

in South Africa, which is likely attributed to the regular mixing of individuals throughout this range. A

general overlap in whistle features throughout their South African range supports the use of whistle

characteristics for acoustic monitoring.

                                             https://scholar.sun.ac.za

    3 GROUP COMPOSITION AND BEHAVIOUR EFFECTS ON SIGNATURE
    WHISTLE PRODUCTION IN THE INDIAN OCEAN HUMPBACK DOLPHIN,
    SOUSA PLUMBEA

Prepared for publication in Bioacoustics Journal

                                                                                                           95
                                            https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   ABSTRACT

In South Africa, the Indian Ocean Humpback dolphin, Sousa plumbea, is the country's most threatened
marine mammal, with a small, fragmented distribution along their coastal range. Effective passive
acoustic monitoring of this species requires understanding their individual acoustic behaviour and how
variables such as group composition and behavioural states might affect their detection. Over seven years
(2016 – 2023), boat-based focal follows were conducted of coastal dolphins, including S. plumbea, across
five South African study sites (False Bay, Struisbaai, St Sebastian Bay, Mossel Bay and Richards Bay). Of
the 22 S. plumbea encounters with concurrent behavioural and GPS track data, 21 were suitable for
further analysis (after the removal of one outlier encounter). The study investigated whether 1) number
of signature whistle types (SWTs, number of individuals), 2) the total count of signature whistles (overall
SW production) and 3) temporal production (inter-signature whistle intervals, ISWI) varied with group
behaviour and composition, as these changes could impact individual detection probability within a
capture-recapture framework. Of the 1382 total S. plumbea whistles identified, 791 had moderate or good
signal-to-noise ratio. These were further analysed in a signature whistle framework using a bout analysis
approach to identify 20 SWTs (proxy for individuals). The median rate of SWTs detected in an encounter
was 0.03 SWTs/min (IQR 0-0.06). Across these encounters, 392 signature whistles were detected, with a
median SW production rate of 0.14 SW/min (IQR 0-0.49) per encounter. Generalised linear mixed models
found no effect of behaviour or group composition on SWT occurrence or SW production. Only ISWI
showed a temporal effect, with group size and group spread showing a decreased signature whistle
production, whereas calf presence increased signature whistle production. These results indicate little
inference on behaviour and group composition based on whistle production. However, a broader sample
size of SWT and SWs would likely provide stronger results. When designing capture-recapture studies
using signature whistles in this species, monitoring group composition and behavioural effects are likely
less important considerations when considering optimal detection design. Although behaviour and
context are demonstrated to affect acoustic behaviour and SW detection in other dolphin species, similar
effects are either not present, sample size-dependent, or masked by the overall low whistle production
rates in S. plumbea.




                                                                                                        96
                                            https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   INTRODUCTION
Animal behaviour is known to be influenced by activity and group composition, with individuals adapting

acoustically to these dynamic settings (Manning & Dawkins, 2012). Monitoring changes to acoustic

behaviour under different contexts can inform us about changes to the level of stress or arousal and these

have been used as a marker for welfare in domesticated and captive animals (Esch et al., 2009a; Laurijs

et al., 2021, Probert et al., 2021). Variations in acoustic behaviour can include changes to signal amplitude

(Nemeth & Brumm, 2009), the addition of spectral characteristics (Townsend & Manser, 2011) or a

change in temporal production rate (Digby et al., 2013). In odontocetes, behavioural state, group

composition and group size (Nowacek, 2005; Quick & Janik, 2008; Scarpaci et al., 2000) are known to

influence whistle production.




Traditional capture-recapture studies investigate the population size of species through trapping and

marking individuals to understand the rate of recaptures over several sampling occasions (Amstrup,

2010). The effectiveness of different markers used in these studies can often depend on the

characteristics and behaviour of the species (Carter et al., 2012; Wegge et al., 2004). The use of physical

tags requires capturing individuals, and for rare and elusive species, this is difficult and time-consuming.

In recent years, acoustic monitoring of animals using individually distinct calls has been suggested as an

alternative to tagging or photographic methods for capture-recapture studies (Longden et al., 2020;

Terry et al., 2005). However, as with all methods, using naturally occurring sounds as detection markers

has inherent biases, such as varying levels of detection probability between species, groups, and

individuals. The detection probability of an individual, based on the production of individual calls, may

be influenced by both intrinsic (arousal, hunger, health) and extrinsic (group behaviour and composition,

ambient noise, weather), as well as life history stage and individual personality (Briefer, 2020).

Understanding what influences individual acoustic production is key when designing the timing and

locations of long-term passive acoustic arrays.




                                                                                                          97
                                              https://scholar.sun.ac.za




In some delphinids, identity is encoded in unique signature whistles and is best studied in bottlenose

dolphins (Janik, 2009). Signature whistles are developed through vocal learning and experience within

an individual’s environment (Janik, 2009). Signature whistles have a high level of individual

distinctiveness in frequency modulation patterns, independent of voice cues (Janik et al., 2006). They are

formed during the first few weeks as a calf and are shown to have long-term stability of up to at least 43

years (Sayigh et al., 2022). Signature whistles are behaviour-dependent and act as contact calls between

conspecifics, used to maintain long-term associations and group cohesion within a fission-fusion society

(Janik & Slater, 2000). Signature whistles are most widely documented in Tursiops species, with over 60

years of research (Caldwell & Caldwell, 1965; Janik & Sayigh, 2013). Over the decades, evidence has

accumulated for signature whistle use across other odontocete species (Tursiops aduncus Gridley et al.,

2014, Stenella longirostris Rio, 2023, Stenella plagiodon Caldwell et al., 1973, Delphinus delphis Fearey et

al., 2019, Sousa chinensis Van Parijs & Corkeron, 2001, Pseudorca crassidens Rio & Rosales-Nanduca, 2024,

Lagenorhynchus obliquidens Caldwell et al., 1971, Sotalia guianensis Lima & Le Pendu, 2014, and Monodon

monoceros Shapiro, 2006).




Identification of an individuals’ unique signature whistle type is best done using recordings of a particular

animal during temporary capture events or isolation in captivity, where signature whistles are often

produced at a high rate and operationally defined as the most produced whistle contour in this context

(Janik, 2009b). In these circumstances, signature whistles account for up to 80 - 100% of the whistle

repertoire (Caldwell et al., 1990). In free-ranging dolphins, identification of signature whistles is more

difficult to ascertain due to the difficulty in identification of signallers. In the wild, signature whistles only

contribute between 38% and 70% of whistles in freely interacting Tursiops groups (Janik, 2009a).

Identifying signature whistles can be particularly challenging, especially in freely interacting wild

populations (Janik, 2013). As dolphins produce both signature whistles and a variety of non-signature

whistles, linking individual whistles to the signaller in a wild context and identifying which whistles are

signatures is difficult without the use of localisation (Quick & Janik, 2008). Janik et al., (2013) developed



                                                                                                               98
                                            https://scholar.sun.ac.za




a bout analysis method termed SIGnature IDentification (SIGID) to describe likely signature whistles in

wild dolphins through the use of a single hydrophone. Using this method, developed for T. trunctatus, a

whistle type is classified as a signature whistle when 4 or more highly similar whistles recorded are from

the same encounter, where on at least one occasion, the whistles were produced in a sequence with 75%

or more (i.e. minimum 3 out of 4) occurring within 1 to 10 s (Janik et al., 2013; Kriesell et al., 2014). As

acoustic behaviour differs between species (Kaplan & Reiss, 2017), the application of SIGID (i.e. bout

criteria) may need to be relaxed or made more stringent when working with other species, and this is an

important consideration when identifying new signature whistles in freely interacting groups (see Fearey

et al., 2019, D. delphis).




Signature whistles can be used to communicate identity and maintain individual relationships in fission-

fusion social structures, meaning signature whistle production is often linked to group size (Janik,

2009a). For smaller delphinid groups in the wild, whistle production generally increases as group size

increases (Cook et al., 2004; Jones & Sayigh, 2002) up to about 10 animals in a group, after which

individual whistle production starts to decrease in groups above 15 individuals due to increased potential

masking by other whistles (Quick & Janik, 2008). However, this is not true across all studies. In Portugal,

dos Santos et al., (2005) found no effect of changes to group size on signature whistle production per

individual, indicating differences in effects on production across Tursiops spp. populations. In larger

delphinid groups, whistle production varies, with species adopting a greater variety of communication

methods in acoustically complex soundscapes. In Spinner dolphins (Stenella longirostris), larger pods

have one individual within a subgroup who will call at a time and favour short-range burst pulses

(Lammers et al., 2006). In some environments, adaptation is not from the signaller but rather from the

signal receiver. Common dolphins (Delphinus delphis), which have group sizes of 500+ individuals, create

an acoustically cluttered environment where masking of calls is likely (Fearey et al., 2019). Nityananda

& Bee, (2011) refer to this as the ‘cocktail party effect’ and describe the ability of individuals to

differentiate a single auditory signal in the presence of a high amount of background noise. King penguin



                                                                                                         99
                                             https://scholar.sun.ac.za




(Aptenodytes patagonicus) chicks have also been shown to be able to perceive parental identity

information amongst large colonies within a very noisy soundscape (Aubin & Jouventin, 1998).




With signature whistles used to communicate identity and maintain individual relationships in fission-

fusion social structures, they are often linked to production under different group compositions (Quick

& Janik, 2008). Whistles, in general, are well suited to longer-range communication as they are relatively

omnidirectional in comparison to other odontocete call types and can propagate over longer distances

than clicks or burst pulses (Jones et al., 2020). This makes whistles more likely to be used in larger groups

where animals have the potential to be more dispersed (Cook et al., 2004). This idea was supported by

Lammers et al., (2006), who found that in Hawaiian spinner dolphins, whistles were more commonly

produced by dolphins spread further apart, suggesting whistles were used to maintain contact in more

dispersed groups. Specifically looking at signature whistle use, in their role as a cohesion call, Tursiops

spp. produce signature whistles when different groups meet at sea and when they join to form single

groups or exchange individuals, reflective of their fission-fusion group structure. However, (Quick &

Janik, 2012) noted that typically, only one individual from either group would call, leading to potential

variability in the effect of group composition across species.




One of the most important functions of signature whistles is in mother-calf pairs to maintain contact and

facilitate recognition when separated (Sayigh et al., 1990; Smolker et al., 1993; Tyack, 1997). In Tursiops

species, higher call rates are seen in groups with calves present (King et al., 2016; Kriesell et al., 2014).

Mothers significantly increase their signature whistle production in the first few weeks of the calf’s life

to initiate the recognition by calves within a group context (King et al., 2016). Production rates are also

seen to increase during the separation of mother-calf pairs, with the rate of calls increasing with the

distance of separation (King et al., 2016), as demonstrated in both captive or restrained ( King et al., 2016;

Sayigh et al., 1990) contexts and in wild, and freely ranging T. aduncus (Smolker et al., 1993). Mothers

and calves are strongly bonded and when separate would need to remain in contact/ reunite once

                                                                                                          100
                                            https://scholar.sun.ac.za




separated and, therefore, determine one another’s positions quickly, i.e. under any condition, such as at

night, in low visibility, or when threatened by predators, supporting the use of signature whistles to locate

and maintain contact with others (Mello et al., 2005).




Lastly, group behaviour effects on signature whistle production can be evidenced in both captive and

wild environments (Cook et al., 2004). In captivity, higher arousal levels from human-induced activity

can affect signature whistle production (Probert et al., 2021b). While natural variability, as seen in the

wild, due to cooperative behaviours, such as socialising and feeding, can also effect signature whistle

production (La Manna et al., 2019). Dolphins produce signature whistles at higher rates when under

stress, presumably as animals attempt to alert conspecifics to changes such as changing water levels in

pools (Rachinas-Lopes et al., 2017) or during temporary restraint (Esch, Sayigh, Blum, et al., 2009b).

Higher production rates are also seen due to arousal from scheduled activities and associated activity

cues, such as feeding and training times (Probert et al., 2021b). During entrapment events, whistle call

rates in Tursiops species ranged from 5 - 32.9 wh/min (Gridley et al., 2014), while other capture events

indicate median call rates of 10.9 (King & Janik, 2013) and 14.3 wh/min (Esch, Sayigh, & Wells, 2009b).

Outside of these specific, high arousal contexts, in free-swimming bottlenose dolphins, Quick & Janik

(2008), found the mean general whistle production rate for individuals to be lower at 1 wh/2 min when

travelling or 9.8 wh/2 min when engaged in social contexts. Higher whistle production rates have been

recorded in the wild as groups meet at sea, commonly seen in species with fission-fusion social systems

(Quick & Janik, 2012). Direct comparison between studies is difficult due to different group sizes,

environments, and behaviours, as well as studies measuring general whistles or signature whistles.

However, across studies, increased arousal (including stress) or socially cooperative events (such as co-

operative feeding) elevate whistling rates compared to general polarised movements such as travel

(Oswald et al., 2008).




                                                                                                         101
                                            https://scholar.sun.ac.za




In South Africa, the Indian Ocean humpback dolphin (Sousa plumbea) exhibits elusive life history traits

(Karczmarski et al., 1997; Koper et al., 2016). They are split into two geographically distinct populations,

with a total population of less than 500 and average movements of less than 200 km (Vermeulen et al.,

2018). Their limited range is extremely coastal, only extending up to 5 km offshore, and a maximum of

20 m water depth (Saayman & Tayler, 1973). Group sizes are typically small, especially compared with

sympatric delphinid species such as T. aduncus and D. delphis (Gridley, 2010; Reisinger & Karczmarski,

2010). Group sizes have decreased over the past decade with the number of solitary animals increasing

(Karczmarski, 1999). Although they occur in sympatry with several other whistling delphinid species (T.

aduncus, D. delphis, O. orca), direct interactions are rare (Frainer et al., 2023). Conservation efforts for

this species in South Africa are hampered by the difficulties in monitoring this species using standard

boat-based methods. Their small population, low group sizes, and their propensity for shallow surf zones

make boat-based focal follows difficult. Capture-recapture using individually distinctive calls has

therefore been suggested to acoustically monitor elusive species (Janik et al., 2013; Terry et al., 2005).

Signature whistles have previously been recognised in other Sousa species under restrained or stranded

contexts (Cheng et al., 2017; Van Parijs & Corkeron, 2001) with further evidence of signature whistles in

S. plumbea from Gridley et al., (Appendix 1) and Dines et al., (Appendix 2). If using signature whistles as

a proxy for individual detection and monitoring along coastal habitats is possible, understanding

variation in whistle production could be key to designing the locations and timing (such as duty cycling)

within a passive acoustic array for effective detection probability.




This study explores individual whistle production of S. plumbea to understand which group composition

and behavioural contexts influence individual whistling behaviour in this species. Whistle production

was investigated in three ways 1) SWT; count of signature whistle types, effectively the number of

individuals detected, 2) SW; count of total signature whistles produced, effectively the total group

signature whistle production in each encounter, and 3) IWSI; inter signature whistle intervals, the

temporal production rate of signature whistles for each individual (for whistle terms used throughout



                                                                                                        102
                                          https://scholar.sun.ac.za




study see Table 3.1). The study uses group size, group spread, calf presence and surface behaviour as

covariates to investigate how these may influence signature whistle production.




Hypotheses
   1) Group Size — As group sizes increase, the count of signature whistle types present during an

       encounter are expected to increase (i.e., the number of individuals calling), as are the number of

       whistles produced and their temporal production.

   2) Group spread—Dispersed and subgroup group spread will have a larger effect on signature

       whistle type production (number of individuals calling), signature whistle count (overall acoustic

       production),

   3) Calves – With the presence of calves in a group, signature whistle production will increase,

       however signature whistle type (number of individuals) will not significantly increase as

       communication will only increase between mother-calf pairs.

   4) Behaviour—Socialising will have the greatest effect on both signature whistle type production

       (the number of individuals calling) and signature whistle count (overall acoustic production).

                                            https://scholar.sun.ac.za

   METHODS
Data collection

The study was conducted off the south coast of South Africa from 2016 – 2023 (22 encounters over 21

recording days) using boat-based focal follows (five sites: False Bay, Struisbaai, St Sebastian Bay, Mossel

Bay, Richards Bay Figures 3.1, 3.2). All boat-based recording locations were within 5 km of the shoreline

and at a maximum 20 m water depth. Boat trips were conducted during daylight hours with favourable

weather conditions (Beaufort sea scale < 4). Research was conducted on either a 30 ft Buttcat (Mossel

Bay) or 6.2 m Rhib (False Bay, Struisbaai, St Sebastian Bay, Richards Bay), both with four-stroke engines

for minimal animal disturbance from engine noise or boat proximity. The boat-based focal group follows

began when S. plumbea was within 50 m of the boat. Engines were either turned off, if safe to do so, or

left idle while continuous scans for other dolphin groups continued. Acoustic data were collected via

dipping hydrophones (HTI-96-MIN; High Tech Inc., United States; flat frequency response of 2 Hz– 30

kHz ± 1 dB) connected to a digital recorder (Tascam DR-680; TEAC America Inc., United States; sampling

at 96 kHz and sample depth 24-bit stereo or Zoom H1) or via a SoundTrap, an autonomous acoustic

recorder, secured to a weighted line (Sound Trap 300 HF; Ocean Instruments, and New Zealand;

frequency response: 20 Hz - 150 kHz ± 3 dB, sensitivity: 183.3 dB re. 1 μPa). Both hydrophone setups

were suspended from the side of the boat ~3 m below water level. During recordings, group size, group

spread, calf presence, and surface behaviours were either documented through point sampling every 3

minutes (Mann, 1999) or through continuous vocal notes (Table 3.2). Acoustic encounters were defined

as the start of S. plumbea sightings when the hydrophone entered the water, until the end of

the encounter, whereby the group either moved away from the boat or lost the group out of visual range,

and the hydrophone was removed from the water. Only encounters where both acoustic recordings and

concurrent behaviour and group composition data were collected were used in this study.

                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za

Data processing


Only single species encounters with concurrent behavioural and group composition data were used for

analysis. Encounters where concurrent behavioural and group composition data were available, but no

signature whistles were recorded were still included in the analysis. Data were visually analysed in Raven

Pro v1.6 (Center for Conservation Bioacoustics, 2019). The fast Fourier transformation parameters used

to examine the data were a 1024-point Hamming window and 75% overlap.




Acoustic encounters were visually scanned for S. plumbea whistles, and whistles were manually

annotated and categorised. A whistle was defined as one continuous contour or two or more repeated

contours (a contour being a continuous tonal signal) separated by a period of silence between 0.03 s and

0.25 s in duration (Kriesell et al., 2014). This definition was in line with the definition confirmed in S.

plumbea in Gridley et al., (Appendix 1). Each contour was graded visually based on the signal-to-noise

ratio (SNR) for each contour. SNR was assigned from 1 – 3: 1) the signal was faint or partially masked but

visible on the spectrogram, 2) the signal was clear and unambiguous, and 3) the signal was prominent

and dominated the spectrogram (Gridley et al., 2015). Only good-quality whistles (those graded SNR 2 or

3) were used in the analysis (Table 3.3). These whistles were then visually categorised into signature

whistle types, based on the Sousa signature whistle catalogue (Figure 3.3, collated for Gridley et al.,

(Appendix 1)) with consensus on whistle categorisation reached by two observers (Authors SD and TG).

In this catalogue, stereotyped whistles were visually categorised into groups of highly similar contour

shapes. Likely signature whistle types (SWTs) were identified through a bout analysis approach, termed

SIGID, whereby whistles of a type repeated within 1 to 10 seconds are highly likely to be a signature

whistle (Janik et al 2013). The minimum threshold of three out of four whistles occurring within 1-10

seconds has been verified for T. truncatus (Janik et al., 2013). SIGID has been extensively used to identify

likely signature whistles from populations and species of free-ranging dolphins (Fearey et al., 2019;

Kriesell et al., 2014; Quick & Janik, 2012). During analysis, each whistle of SNR 2/3 was assessed and

compared to the signature whistle type catalogue for South Africa containing 21 types. Where contours


                                                                                                        108
                                            https://scholar.sun.ac.za




matched, the whistle was assigned the catalogue ID. All whistle types unique to this current study (i.e. not

previously catalogued in Gridley et al., (Appendix 1)) were assessed using the SIGID bout criterion. If met,

these were assigned a novel SWT identification number with a representative contour added to the S.

plumbea SWT catalogue.




Across each acoustic encounter, dominant group composition and behaviour variables were assigned

based on point sample data and vocal track notes (Table 3.3). The total number of signature whistle types

(SWT, number of individuals calling) and signature whistle (SW) emissions were calculated for each

encounter. When calculating the inter-signature whistle interval (ISWI) for temporal production, each

signature whistle was sorted by date and then by signature whistle type to be arranged in consecutive

order of production with the nearest corresponding covariates associated with a whistle start time. The

ISWI was calculated as between the start times of consecutive whistles in a bout. A new whistle bout was

classified after an ISWI of 15 or more minutes, set to account for a dominant behavioural or group

composition state across bouts (Lusseau, 2003). ISWI produces an inverse measure, with a greater ISWI

indicating a slower whistle production rate.

                                                                  https://scholar.sun.ac.za

Statistical Analysis


Multiple analyses were performed to investigate the effect of various covariates on signature whistle

production using generalised linear mixed models (GLMMs) R (version 4.0.2; R Core Team, 2020) in R

package lme4 (Bates et al., 2024). The covariates were modelled against three types of signature whistle

production (hereafter whistle production). The response variables investigated were: 1) SWT; count of

signature whistle types, effectively the number of individuals detected, 2) SW; count of total signature

whistles produced, effectively the total group signature whistle production in each encounter, and 3)

IWSI; inter signature whistle intervals, the temporal production rate of signature whistles for each

individual. For SWT and SW full models, using a Poisson distribution, the covariates tested were group

size (count), group spread (categorical), calf presence (binary presence) and surface behavioural state

(Foraging (F); Milling (M); Travelling (Tr); Socialising (SOC) (categorical, Table 3.3). For the SWT and SW

models, the encounter number was modelled as a random effect to remove variance based on multiple

encounters within the same day. An offset variable (Recording Time ss) was set to account for different

encounter durations potentially inflating whistle production. The same four covariates were used in the

ISWI model, using a Gamma distribution, which is effective at modelling right-skewed time between

various events where lower values are more common (i.e. inter signature whistle interval) (Crawley,

2007). In the ISWI model, both encounter number and SWT were included as random effects, as these

data were analysed from SWT bouts and needed to account for variation between individuals and day.



GLMM models were tested on both the full data set and then again tested on a reduced dataset, removing

any potential outliers, to test the robustness of the data set. Visual inspection of the data indicated that

there was a large variation in the number of SWs detected across encounters. The interquartile range

(IQR) method was applied to the SWs variable in the dataset to confirm the presence of outliers. Using

the IQR, the lower and upper bounds for outlier detection were established as any values below Q1-

1.5*IQR or above Q3+1.5*IQR, respectively. On review, the dispersed group spread was observed in only

one encounter. Behaviourally, this category can be grouped with ‘subgroups’, and to retain the data from



                                                                                                        112
                                           https://scholar.sun.ac.za




this encounter, ‘dispersed’ and ‘subgrouped’ categories were pooled in the group spread covariate for the

purpose of modelling.




Collinearity was tested on all three models using a full model that included all covariates by comparing

variance inflation factors (VIF), with covariates with a VIF greater than 2 being removed (R package: car

(Fox et al., 2024).




                                                                                                     113
                                            https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   RESULTS

In total, 1382 whistles were identified from five study locations (False Bay, Struisbaai, St Sebastian Bay,

Mossel Bay, Richards Bay, 2016-2023, Total recording duration 19:15:10 hh:mm:ss). Across the 22

encounters, there were four encounters where no whistles were detected and three encounters where

there were whistles detected but no signature whistle types detected. Out of the total number of whistles,

791 whistles had a good signal-to-noise ratio for further analysis (Table 3.4). Signature whistles

accounted for 28% of total whistles detected across encounters. Group sizes across encounters ranged

from 1 to 10 individuals. Calves were defined by their position in relation to their mother and identified

through consistent presence at the mother's side (Gubbins et al., 1999). Calves were present in nine out

of 22 encounters, and when present, the group contained one to two calves in a group. The most observed

behaviour during recording sessions was milling, with no foraging behaviour observed during

encounters.




Based on inter quartile range (IQR), one data point (20191016_01 (Mossel Bay)) was flagged as an

outlier. The identified outlier was reviewed to assess its impact on the overall analysis. It was determined

that the outlier should be excluded as there was no effect on significance seen across any covariate (p >

0.5) when this data point was both included and excluded, confirming the robustness of the data sets

when the outlier was removed, and the models were run on 21 encounters.




With dispersed and subgroups merged, the covariate ‘group spread’ was removed from SWT (VIF = 2.1)

and SWs (VIF = 3.2) best fit models, both being collinear with behaviour, which was retained as the more

biologically meaningful covariate. The ISWI best-fit model removed behaviour due to a high VIF (2.7) in

the full model.

    1) Signature whistle type (number of individuals)


The total number of signature whistle types identified across all encounters was 20, with a median

production rate of 0.03 SWT/min (IQR 0-0.06) across encounters (Figure 3.4). Across the four covariates,

groups encountered without calves produced more unique SWTs compared to when calves were present

(median Calves A = 3, Calves P = 1) (Figure 3.5). The number of SWT produced per encounter was greatest

when dolphins were clustered in subgroups/dispersed (median subgrouped = 4) and when socialising

(median behaviour Socialising = 4). However, when considered within the modelling framework, the

best-fit GLMM model for SWT occurrence showed no significant effects. Further investigation into the

effects of covariates was then conducted at a class level, with no significant effect seen at the class level

either (Table 3.5).




    2) Signature whistle production (overall acoustic production)


In total, 392 signature whistles were detected, with a median production rate of 0.14 SW/min (IQR 0-

0.49) across encounters (Figure 3.4). Across the four covariates, there was very little difference between

the median number of signature whistles across groups (Figure 3.6). The best fit GLMM model showed

no significant effect of any covariates or any significance at class level either (Table 3.5).

   3) Inter signature whistle interval (temporal production)


ISWI showed a significant effect of all covariates in the best-fit model on temporal production (Table 3.5).

For calf presence, the incidence rate ratio for encounters decreased ISWI by e-0.61 = 0.54 times (Figure

3.7). This indicates that temporal production of signature whistles increases by over 50% with calf

presence. Sub-grouped /dispersed groups had a larger positive effect on ISWI with a higher incidence

rate ratio than group size (Subgrouped / Dispersed e0.43 = 1.54, Group size e0.13 = 1.14), indicating group

spread had a larger effect on a slower temporal production of SWs than group size.

 whistle intervals in Sousa plumbea across 21 encounters from five South African study sites (False Bay,
 Struisbaai, St Sebastian Bay, Mossel Bay, Richards Bay, 2016-2023). Significant covariates indicated with a
 star (p ≤ 0.01). Covariates included in the best fit model include the presence of calves (CalvesP), group size
 and subgrouped and dispersed group spread (GroupSpreadSGD).

   DISCUSSION
Capture-recapture (CR) models provide essential estimates of a population’s size, survival, and

movement patterns and biases in capture probability need to be well understood (Hammond,

2009). Although these are well understood for photo ID work, the application of CR models using

acoustic cues presents an opportunity for different biases, which need to be well understood.

Previous work has, for example, demonstrated that broad-scale (Longden et al., 2020) and fine-

scale (Fearey et al., 2022) placement of hydrophones can affect the detection of individuals and

abundance estimations. Building on this, the current study was undertaken to help understand

whether group behaviour, group size and composition affect signature whistle production and,

consequently, whether these variables could introduce inherent bias in the detection probability

of signature whistles in a capture-recapture framework in S. plumbea. These results, although

based on limited data, indicate that behaviour and group composition have little bearing on SWT

occurrence or emissions of signature whistles for S. plumbea. Calf presence, however, may

contribute to reduced production of signature whistles and warrants further investigation.




Results from this study indicate that behaviour and group composition do not strongly affect

whistle production rates, only temporal production, with overall low individual whistling rates.

Acoustic behaviour in cetaceans is known to be influenced by different group compositions and

contexts (Jensen et al., 2011; Visser et al., 2017). Understanding under what limits and situations

calls are made or potentially not made can affect individuals' detection through acoustic

monitoring (Longden et al., 2020) . Overall, and compared to other studies on dolphins (Cook et

al., 2004; Hawkins & Gartside, 2010), this study did not identify a strong effect of group behaviour

or group composition on SWT and SW whistle production, with only decreasing temporal effects

on signature whistle production. Across other species, increasing group size is linked to increased

call production (Borker et al., 2014; Payne et al., 2003; Radford and Ridley, 2008). In Tursiops

spp., individual whistling rates increase up to a certain group size threshold, beyond which

                                                                                                123
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




whistle production decreases. This suggests that there is an optimal group size for whistle

production to minimize the masking of conspecific acoustic signals (Quick & Janik, 2012). In this

study, group sizes were generally small, with 43% of encounters containing five or fewer

individuals. This follows that of .Koper et al., (2016) and Bouveroux et al., (2018), whose studies

showed significant declines in S. plumbea group sizes in Algoa Bay, South Africa, over a 24-year

period, from mean group sizes of seven to three animals (Bouveroux et al., 2018). Kaczmarski et

al. (1999) described a relatively fluid group structure with a mixture of locally resident

individuals and transient groups which move further along their South African coastal range.

However, more recent studies suggest a higher degree of site fidelity in S. plumbea in recent years

(James et al., 2015), with movements of individuals averaging 120 km with long-term residency

prevalent in some regions (Vermeulen et al., 2018). In dolphins, higher group stability has been

shown to lead to more strongly associated individuals (Connor et al., 2001; James et al., 2015),

with individuals with stronger social bonds displaying lower whistle exchange rates (Chereskin

et al., 2022). With consistently small groups and evidence for long-term site fidelity from previous

studies, there may be evidence for stronger bonds and more stable group dynamics seen in S.

plumbea throughout their South African range, which would affect signature whistle production

rates.




As individuals meet between groups, studies have shown whistle production to increase,

consistent with evidence that suggests signature whistles function as introductory calls to

communicate identity when free-ranging groups encounter one another (Quick & Janik, 2008).

This was not supported in this study, as subgroups/dispersed group spreads did not significantly

affect SWT and SW production. Overall, the median signature whistle production rate for S.

plumbea across encounters (0.14 SW/min) was lower than other sympatric delphinid species

(Kriesell et al., 2014, Fearey et al., 2019). Production rates are variable and context-dependent

when looking at other species that occur sympatrically to S. plumbea. In T. aduncus, Gridley et al.



                                                                                                124
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




(2014) found rates of 32.9 wh/min and 5 wh/min for a single stereotyped whistle from two

individuals during entrapment events. This study investigated a wild free-swimming population,

where signature whistle production is expected to be lower than during capture or entrapment

events (Gridley et al., 2012). In free-swimming populations, Chereskin et al., (2022) found that

vocal exchanges of signature whistles were more favoured between weakly associated

individuals, while strongly associated individuals preferred affiliative contact to maintain social

relationships. Again, in S. plumbea, smaller, more stable group structures, with a higher level of

site fidelity, could be a reason for the lack of group size or group spread effect on whistle

production, as well as account for their overall low production rates. As signature whistles

function as a cohesion method, higher stability in the smaller groups would reduce the

requirement to acknowledge where each other is through calls, which could be accomplished

through visual contact. Within their small group sizes, S. plumbea may not rely on vocal

communication as strongly as in larger fission-fusion groups, such as in Tursiops spp, with further

work required in comparing regions where Sousa are known to occur in larger groups (See the

Arabian region and China regions, Würsig et al., 2016).




The study found a significant effect of calves on temporal whistle production, with calves

increasing the temporal effect of whistle production. In Tursiops species, under both forced

separation and natural contexts, mothers and calves will increase the call rate of whistles to

initiate reunions with one another, especially when out of visual range (King et al., 2016). Call

rate increases specifically during the first few weeks of a calf’s life to initiate maternal bonding

and establish the calf’s own mother in the group (King et al., 2016). Therefore, separation

distances of mother and calf’s do not exceed the active acoustic space of whistles to maintain

consistent contact even when out of visual or olfactory distances (Quintana-Rizzo et al., 2006).

However, none of the calves seen during this study were newborn or neonatal (S. Dines pers. obs.

confirmed with concurrent photo ID data). In this study, although there was a temporal increase



                                                                                                125
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




in whistle production, in line with other studies, there was no significant effect on the number of

individuals or overall calls produced. In China, S. chinensis, where group sizes reach 48

individuals, nursery groups (with mother-calf pairs present) are 2-4 times larger than non-

nursery groups, providing much stronger protection for calves (Liu et al., 2021). The smaller

group sizes in S. plumbea mean they do not have a ‘safety in numbers’ effect, and the addition of

vulnerable calves to a group will increase intraspecific and interspecific predator threats (Frainer

et al., 2023; Plön et al., 2015). In these smaller groups, reliance on other methods to maintain

mother-calf contact, such as visual or physical contact, might be preferred, as is seen in nursing

baleen whales (Nielsen et al., 2019; Parks et al., 2019; Videsen et al., 2017).




There are notable limitations to this study, namely a small sample size. S. plumbea is listed as

Endangered on the IUCN Red List, with low encounter rates throughout their range (Plön et al.,

2021). This was highlighted in the high boat-based effort required to collect sufficient data for

this study. Over a seven-year period (2016 – 2023), 207 boat-based surveys were conducted

throughout South Africa. S. plumbea were encountered on 40% of these surveys, with acoustic

recording possible on 64 occasions. A conservative approach was taken for this study whereby

encounters were removed if the other species were sighted in the vicinity, and only encounters

with behavioural and group composition data were used. This left the study with 22 encounters

across all five study sites, where the encounters had concurrent group data and acoustic

recordings, with one of those encounters further removed as an outlier for modelling. This

reduced sample size is representative of other studies which have utilised Sousa acoustic data

and highlight the difficult nature of working acoustically within this genus (This study: 22

encounters, 19 hrs, 1382 total whistles, Wang et al., 2013: 13 encounters, 40 hrs, 4630 total

whistles, Hoffman et al., 2015: 8 encounters, 2.5 hrs, 1783 total whistles, Bopardikar et al., 2018:

11 encounters, 3.1 hrs, 2260 total whistles, Yang et al., 2020: four recording sessions, four S.

plumbea click encounters). Although the range of applications of acoustic data sets differs across



                                                                                                126
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




studies, an appreciation for high research effort and elusiveness of the study species limits the

strength of this study’s conclusions. Further work is needed to strengthen the study and improve

modelling by including a greater diversity of encounters.




Other limitations to the study include S. plumbea's propensity to occupy regions of periodic high

noise levels, including high site fidelity in harbours, river mouths and high-use coastal areas by

recreational and commercial boats (Plön et al., 2021). Masking of natural sounds is known to

influence acoustic behaviour in delphinids, and an understanding of ambient sound would

provide greater context to how noise pollution affects this species along their narrow range (La

Manna et al., 2013; Lundquist et al., 2012). Data collection methods might also have influenced

acoustic behaviour. Boat-based recordings require proximity to groups to monitor and record

and in some species, boat proximity influences whistling behaviour (Hawkins & Gartside, 2010).

In this study, effort was made to reduce disturbance to animals, such as recording from a distance,

and engines were turned off when it was safe to do so. To explore this question, future work

would look at a comparison to moored data that had concurrent behaviour data, as this would

gauge potential bias or identify no differences due to either methodology used. The use of moored

data would require confirmed species sightings from land-based observations to ensure

consistent species identification. Although there is a chance of differences between moored and

boat-based collected data, a high level of bias due to the recording setup was not expected in this

study. Boat-based acoustic recordings have been shown to cause minimal disturbance to focal

groups during recordings, with acoustic data collection as one of the most passive observation

methods (La Manna et al., 2013). During this study, individuals showed no aversion behaviours

during data collection, and in most instances, the boat engines were off and were within a

reasonable distance from the focal group.




                                                                                               127
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Compared to other published studies on whistle acoustics in the Sousa genus, there are no studies

investigating the effects on whistle production rate. Signature whistles are referred to as contact

calls, providing information on identity, stress and context-dependent relationships (Esch et al.,

2009; Janik et al., 2006; Sayigh et al., 1995). Previous studies reporting evidence of signature

whistle use in Sousa come from opportunistic events of isolation or injury (Cheng et al., 2017; Van

Parijs & Corkeron, 2001). When monitoring an injured S. chinensis, Cheng et al., (2017) recorded

a signature whistle production rate of 0.84 SW/min from one individual over the recording

period. Although higher than the median signature whistle production rate found across

encounters in this study (0.14 SW/min) direct comparison is unsuitable due to differences in

study design. The outlier that was removed from the analysis had a signature whistle production

rate of 2.7 SW/min across the encounter. Studies of signature whistle production rate in free-

swimming populations have shown a lower signature whistle production of 38% and 70%

compared to restrained animals (Janik, 2009a). In the encounter that was removed as an outlier,

the number of SWTs detected, 11 (proxy for individuals) exceeded the best group size estimation

(8) but did not exceed the maximum group size reported in the encounter (12), indicating that

nearly every individual was acoustically recorded within the encounter. In addition, the

encounter duration was not significantly longer than other encounters and there was no

behaviour or context present that indicated higher whistle rates (calves absent and primary

encounter behaviour travelling). Throughout the encounter, one individual dominated the

signature whistle count (SWT10, 50% of SW production in the encounter). The presence of an

outlier highlights that the species is not always vocally elusive, and whistle production rates can

vary, especially when recording free swimming groups.




This study aimed to determine whether group composition and behavioural contexts influence

signature whistle production and whether these factors could bias detection probabilities in a

capture-recapture framework. Although the sample size was limited, the findings suggest that



                                                                                               128
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




group composition and behaviour are not critical variables for optimizing signature whistle

detection in S. plumbea. In passive acoustic monitoring (PAM), array designs are typically based

on research objectives, equipment availability, and species behaviour. For instance, in Namibia,

the highest detection probability of signature whistles in T. truncatus occurred in areas where

dolphins patrolled for extended periods, which were linked to feeding or socializing zones

(Fearey et al., 2022; Longden et al., 2020). However, with S. plumbea, there is only evidence that

the temporal production of signature whistles is affected by these variables, indicating that other

behaviour or group composition effects are either not present, sample size-dependent, or masked

by the overall low whistle production rates in S. plumbea.




                                                                                               129
                                          https://scholar.sun.ac.za

    4 THE EFFICACY OF PASSIVE ACOUSTIC MONITORING VS BOAT-
    BASED PHOTOGRAPHIC IDENTIFICATION OF AN ELUSIVE
    COASTAL DOLPHIN SPECIES



Sasha Dines1,2 Guilherme Frainer1,3, Simon Elwen11,2, Enrico Gennari 4, John Measey5, Tess

Gridley1,2
