Prepared for publication in Remote Sensing and Ecology




                                                                                             138
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   ABSTRACT
Wildlife practitioners commonly apply photographic identification (photo ID) to monitor wild
populations, including many dolphin species. However, for elusive species, encounter rates are
low, and photo ID can be an inefficient method for detection. Dolphins develop individually
unique acoustic signature whistles which convey individual identity to conspecifics. Identification
of individuals through passive acoustic monitoring could allow for individual-based monitoring.
To investigate the effectiveness of acoustic and photo ID methods to detect individual Indian
Ocean humpback dolphins (Sousa. plumbea) in the population, an acoustic array was deployed in
coastal waters, covering 8 km in Mossel Bay, South Africa, from 19th March – 5th April 2021. Boat-
based photo-identification surveys were conducted throughout the same area and time period.
Here, the two methods are compared to investigate the efficacy of the detection of dolphins using
both methods over a short-term survey period. Detection characteristics (spatial variation,
temporal detection and individual detection rates) were compared between methods, together
with the financial and logistical constraints of each method for collecting capture-recapture data,
which are key considerations for effective applied conservation programs in this species. Through
automated species classifiers, 38 acoustic encounters were assigned to S. plumbea during 18
deployment days. Of the 226 good-quality whistles detected in these encounters, 199 signature
whistles were detected, with 10 signature whistle types, SWTs (different individuals) identified.
Seven SWTs were recaptured on multiple encounters, with four SWTs seen on multiple days.
Acoustic detections occurred across all moorings but with substantial variation between
locations. Over 80% of signature whistles were detected on non-adjacent moorings located 6 km
apart (moorings 1 and 4), with three individuals accounting for 73% of all SW detections. A
diurnal pattern was found with whistle detection higher overnight. In comparison, during the 13
boat-based survey days, there were encounters with S. plumbea on seven days (mean duration of
encounter 00:46:22 hh:mm:ss (± 00:21:17 SD)). From these, 16 individuals were photographed,
of which six dolphins were recaptured across different days. Throughout the survey area,
dolphins were visually observed at two locations only, near to moorings 1 and 6. Acoustic
methods had a greater mean detection rate of individuals (0.89 ± 1.48 SD) SWTs
detected/encounter, mins) than photo ID (0.08 ± 0.05 SD) individuals photographed/encounter,
mins). There was no correlation between encounter duration and individuals/SWTs detected,
highlighting that longer encounters are not the determining factor of detection across both
methods; rather, other factors such as environmental conditions, animal behaviour and
equipment likely have a stronger determinant on the rate of detection. However, fieldwork costs
“per dolphin” captured were higher for acoustic methods than for Photo ID methods when
processing and data analysis times were included (Photo ID: 6400 ZAR.dolphin-1; Acoustics:
12266 ZAR.dolphin-1). The study recognises the value of photo ID for monitoring rare species at
reduced costs. However, it highlights that continuous acoustic detection methods offer a viable
alternative to visual-based approaches for elusive species such as S. plumbea for short-term
assessments.




                                                                                               139
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   INTRODUCTION
Conservation action is hindered by the ability of managers, practitioners, and policymakers to

access practical information and data (Ausden & Walsh, 2020). The use of evidence in practical

decision-making requires research to be translatable to implementation (Downey et al., 2022).

Recent development in Conservation Evidence-based studies overcomes such hurdles by

assessing scientific evidence based on the effectiveness of conservation interventions on

biodiversity (Downey et al., 2022). Scientists need to consider the applications of the data they

are collating and the recommendations that their results produce. This can be done by

considering their studies' costs, time, logistical and analytical requirements. Monitoring is an

essential tool to provide objective and sound scientific advice to decision-makers at all levels of

government to implement effective management policies (Hutchings et al., 2009). Developing

these monitoring programs for threatened species is often hindered by life history traits such as

low numbers, low densities, and inaccessible environments (Robinson et al., 2018). Effective

conservation management plans consider these logistical constraints and species-specific

research to produce baseline empirical data that informs effective monitoring studies.




Available monitoring methods vary across taxa and environments, but mark-recapture is one of

the most widely used research tools to assess population trends, especially on free-ranging

cetaceans (Urian et al., 2015). Mark-recapture provides several population parameters including

movement, residency, social relationships, population size and survivorship (Hammond, 1990;

Lukacs & Burnham, 2005). Mark-recapture or capture-recapture initially relied on artificial

marks (‘tags’) applied to animals, but these typically require capturing or harassment of the study

animals at some level (Amstrup, 2010). Many studies, and almost all current cetacean studies,

now prefer non-invasive methods, identifying individuals using naturally distinctive marks or

scars and colouration patterns. This method is used across a suite of species, typically ‘capturing’

the animals photographically, and has been an effective method in monitoring cetacean species


                                                                                                140
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




for over 50 years (Hammond et al., 1990; Würsig & Würsig, 1977). Long-term monitoring of

animals using these non-invasive techniques, requires identifying features to be individually

unique, remain stable over the individuals’ lifetime and be readily identifiable in the field or in a

photograph (Hammond, 1986). This method of photographic identification (photo ID) is popular

due to its relatively low effort, accessible non-specialist equipment and high level of

reproducibility when compared to other forms of identification methods such as physical tags.

Additionally, photographic catalogues are easily contributed to by researchers, managers and

citizen scientists (Cheeseman et al., 2017; Tyson & Wells, 2016; Vermeulen et al., 2018). Two key

requirements of cetacean photo ID studies are the requirement for good image quality and a

reasonable level of fin distinctiveness. However, this leads to an inherent bias towards the over-

detection of highly marked individuals, the extent of which varies widely between focal species

and even research groups (Urian et al., 2015). Standardization of methodology can reduce this

bias, by applying grading scales to assess image quality and fin distinctiveness and only using a

balanced subset of images (Friday et al., 2008). In the field, photo ID methods are limited by a

need for daylight, boat access and environmental conditions such as glare or sea state, as well as

equipment quality and photographer skill. A key assumption of capture-recapture is the

homogeneity of detection across individuals, and the presence of ’trap shy/happy’ or boat-

averse/friendly individuals in a group which could lead to biased sampling (Hammond, 1986;

Marshall & Pierce, 2012). For species with small populations and low encounter rates, the ratio

of manpower and field effort to data collected can be quite poor, with elusive coastal species a

prime example of this, where weeks or months of field effort can result in only 10s of animals

encountered and low encounter rates (Virgili et al., 2018). For species with vocal individuality,

the use of naturally occurring calls has been suggested as a more effective method of non-invasive

mark-recapture monitoring (Terry et al., 2005).




                                                                                                 141
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Bioacoustic monitoring of wildlife has advanced in recent years and can be used to effectively

detect species and individuals across a wide geographic range. Bioacoustic monitoring has been

explored in a suite of species and environments such as coral reefs (Lamont et al., 2022b), fish

(Looby et al., 2022; Popper & Hastings, 2009) , cetaceans (Gridley et al., 2015), frogs (Measey et

al., 2017) and even elasmobranchs (Fetterplace et al., 2022) creating a wide variety of

applications. Underwater, acoustic detection by both animals and scientific instruments can occur

at greater distances than visual detection, as sound transmits far more efficiently through water

(Ladich & Winkler, 2017). Cetaceans produce acoustic signals which transmit well underwater,

for example, in optimum conditions, dolphin whistles can be detected over more than 20 km

(Janik et al., 2000) and the low-frequency tonal sounds of baleen whales can be detected up to

hundreds of km (Janik, 2005), making acoustic monitoring an ideal way to passively and remotely

study cetacean populations. However, for most species, acoustic monitoring is still only possible

at the “species level” and not the level of individuals, as required in capture-recapture, so most

passive acoustic studies focus on species presence or vocal behaviour, and generating relative

density measures typically only possible with arrays of multiple hydrophones where individual

callers can be localised (Hannay et al., 2013; Marques et al., 2009).




For species with small populations that are threatened, individual-level monitoring provides

much richer and more useful data. Individual acoustic monitoring requires stable, stereotyped

signals that can be used to identify individuals as they move throughout their range over time.

Individual voice cues have been studied across many taxa, including several species of birds, as

they are used to identify neighbours and kin from strangers to map territories and to select and

identify mates (see Terry et al., 2005). Most species recognize other individuals through these

vocal cues, where characteristics of the vocal tract produce individually identifiable signals for

identification (Aubin et al., 2000; Tibbetts & Dale, 2007; Zann, 1990). However, dolphins develop

unique signatures in which individual identity information occurs in the frequency modulation



                                                                                              142
                                          https://scholar.sun.ac.za




pattern, as opposed to voice cues (Sayigh et al., 2017). Termed signature whistles (SWs), these

signals have high levels of distinctiveness between individual dolphins and are used to convey

individual identity to conspecifics and to maintain group cohesion (Sayigh et al., 2007). Signature

whistles are formed during the first year of life, developing as a contact call between mother-calf

pairs and can stay stable for up to 43 years (Sayigh et al., 2022). In restrained or captive dolphins,

SWs can constitute 80% - 100% of whistles produced (Janik & Slater, 1998), whilst in wild free-

ranging bottlenose dolphins (Tursiops truncatus), SWs constitute 34% - 70% of whistles (Cook et

al., 2004). Notably, SWs offer high individual distinctiveness and long-term stability and can be

easily detected using noninvasive acoustic monitoring techniques, making them potentially ideal

for individual acoustic-based monitoring programs (Longden et al., 2020; Terry et al., 2005).




Acoustic capture-recapture using individually distinctive signals has previously been limited by

the ability to passively detect individual signals (Stevenson et al., 2022). The availability of

suitable technology, such as affordable autonomous hydrophones, has only been available since

the mid-2000s (Sugai et al., 2019). High financial costs have limited the capacity of researchers

and organizations to utilize passive acoustic monitoring (PAM) methods (Gibb et al., 2019).

Recent developments in low-cost acoustic recorders, especially for the aquatic environment have

vastly increased the accessibility of these methods (Lamont et al., 2022a). In addition to logistical

constraints, identifying signature whistles in the wild is challenging and often requires

simultaneous visual observation or localization (Quick & Janik, 2008). Early studies of signature

whistles were done on captive or temporarily held dolphins where whistles could be confidently

linked to the signal producer. However, Janik et al., (2013) developed a bout analysis approach to

identify signature whistles using SIGnature Identification (SIGID) from free-ranging dolphins in

the wild using a single hydrophone (i.e. not through localization of calls) which has been applied

successfully across multiple study populations and species (Fearey et al., 2019; Longden et al.,

2020; Probert et al., 2023; Rio, 2023).



                                                                                                  143
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




However, when applying signature whistles in a capture-recapture context to study wild

dolphins, there are several additional considerations. As signature whistles are a context-driven

call type, in Tursiops spp., signature whistle production can vary considerably with behaviour

which may be linked to specific areas or periods, so hydrophone placement within the habitat is

an important consideration in previous studies (Fearey et al., 2022; Longden et al., 2020). Duty

cycling is also a key consideration, as hydrophone deployment is limited by battery and storage

capacity. Alternating between active and dormant periods of recording can extend deployment

duration, yet missed detections may influence overall species detection as well as the ability to

conduct SIGID bout analysis (Fearey et al., 2022). Additionally, almost all research on signature

whistles and their potential use in capture-recapture studies are based on Tursiops spp., and their

application to other species, where signature whistle production may be different, has not yet

been tested.




Most cetacean studies to date have conducted capture-recapture using individual photographic

identification and applied a range of ‘open’ or ‘closed’ models. These models can also be applied

to signature whistles (for application in Tursiops spp., See Longden et al., 2020; Romeu et al.,

2024). Longden et al., (2020) successfully compared abundance estimates from photo ID surveys

to SW-type (SWT) detections from moored acoustic deployments (3 weeks per month over 6

months). The study found spatial heterogeneity in the capture of SWTs, with the majority of

detections occurring in a known resting and socialising area. Temporal variability was also

identified, with a majority of SWTs detected in the first 30 minutes of an encounter. The study

identified a closed population of 53 SWTs, which overlapped with the known population size of

54 - 76 individuals, calculated using photo ID methods (Elwen et al., 2019). The study highlighted

the potential for signature whistles to be used as proxies for individual occurrence in capture-

recapture abundance models in a population of known range and abundance (Elwen et al., 2019;



                                                                                               144
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Gridley et al., 2015).



The benefit of individual acoustic capture-recapture as a monitoring tool is only truly appreciated

when it can be applied to species and regions where photo ID data is lacking, or traditional photo

ID methods are not logistically feasible. Compared to many other species, cetaceans are time-

consuming and expensive to survey and monitor (Kaschner et al., 2012). Low densities, extensive

time spent underwater, and wide home ranges mean boat-based detection or aerial surveys often

need to last for many weeks or be repeated over several years to collect enough information to

conduct reliable abundance and density estimates (Jewell et al., 2012). Across their coastal range

in the Indian Ocean, S. plumbea is recognised as “Endangered” on the IUCN Red List, with

monitoring of the species over the past 40 years reliant on aerial surveys, visual surveys and

stranding data (Braulik et al., 2015). These costly methods have proved to be challenging for this

elusive species with its low detection rates. The production of empirical data for these

populations is challenging, as errors are typically high with low recaptures and sighting rates

(James et al., 2015; Plön et al., 2021). Quicker and more cost-effective detection assessment

methods could be invaluable in these species, allowing for more rapid identification of key

regions to prioritise for continued monitoring studies, leading to greater conservation action

(Braulik et al., 2017).




Throughout the whole of South Africa, the S. plumbea population is thought to number less than

500 individuals, split into two populations with little known exchange between the two and with

an extremely nearshore range typically < 20 m water depth (James et al., 2015; Vermeulen et al.,

2018). Vermeulen et al. (2018) collated available photo-identification databases over the

previous 16 years to assess the movement of individuals between sites in South Africa, identifying

only 247 individuals across all study sites. Individuals were shown to have a coastal range of up

to 500 km with movements between False Bay and Mossel Bay (Gridley et al., Appendix 1;



                                                                                               145
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Vermeulen et al., 2018) and Plettenberg Bay and Mossel Bay (James, 2014). Mossel Bay is a well-

studied location in the middle of the south coast's population range. Between 2011 and 2013,

boat-based photo ID surveys identified 67 individual adult dolphins from 31 surveys over the

three-year study period. A ‘super-population’ of 125 individuals (95% CI: 112 - 140) was

calculated over the three-year period, with 32 -71 individuals estimated per year from the open

population modelling (James et al., 2015). James et al. (2014) also collated survey data for all

studies of S. plumbea in South Africa up to that point and highlighted that most studies conform

to similar levels of survey effort (60 to 146 surveys over 7 to 36 months). When looking at the

catalogue sizes across previous studies, S. plumbea were identified at a rate of 0.7 to 2.1 dolphins

per encounter or 0.4 to 1.2 dolphins per survey, reflecting the low numbers and small group sizes

of these dolphins, which make standard boat-based photo ID studies inefficient (James et al.,

2015). Therefore, more efficient methods to detect these elusive species should be investigated

with the inefficiency of boat-based detections and the fragmented efforts of long-term photo ID

monitoring programs in South Africa (James et al., 2015; Vermeulen et al., 2018).




This chapter explores the application of acoustic detection of S. plumbea through signature

whistles and compares this to simultaneously collected visual survey and photo ID data. The use

of signature whistles in the Sousa genus has been suggested from a single-stranded S. sahulensis

in Australia (Van Parijs & Corkeron, 2001) and an injured S. chinensis on the south coast of China

(Cheng et al., 2017) while Gridley et al., (Appendix 1) demonstrated signature whistle production

in free-ranging S. plumbea in South Africa. This chapter will assess individual detection using both

methods. The study aims to test the efficacy of 1) spatial variation of dolphin acoustic detection,

2) temporal differences in dolphin detection levels across each method, 3) individual detection

effort and 4) to discuss logistical constraints and considerations of both methods within the

context of a short-term assessment period.




                                                                                                146
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   METHODS

Data collection: Acoustics

Data were collected in Mossel Bay, South Africa, from 19th March – 5th April 2021. Mossel Bay is

an east-facing, sheltered bay with regular sightings of marine mammals along its coastline (Levy,

2017). A short study period was chosen to reflect what is a typical requirement of rapid impact

assessments, a common use of photo ID surveys for assessments of threatened species (Braulik

et al., 2018). Hydrophones were deployed on moorings in an 8 km linear array following the

coastline within the 15 m water depth range (Figure 4.1). Moorings were spaced ~2 km apart to

ensure dolphins would not be simultaneously detected on multiple hydrophones within the array.

All moorings were deployed in 8-10 m water depth, using identical setups of a ~40 kg railway

track weight with instruments 3 m from the seafloor connected to a mid-water and surface buoy.

Attempts were made to avoid placing moorings on or near loud reef areas to prevent

overloading/excess snapping shrimp noise, but still within the suitable habitat of S. plumbea. All

hydrophones were scheduled to start at 00:00:00 UTC on 19th March 2021 and turned off when

retrieved between the 1st – 9th April 2021. Moorings were visually checked regularly during boat-

based surveys throughout the data collection period to ensure no movement, animal

entanglements, theft or loss of equipment.




Acoustic data was recorded using SoundTraps (Ocean Instruments Ltd., Auckland, New Zealand).

The SoundTraps were set at a 96 kHz sampling rate with a continuous duty cycle of 10-minute

files (this is a safety mechanism to produce individual 10-minute files over a continuous period

to account for possible equipment recording faults) (Table 4.1).




                                                                                              147
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Data collection: Photographic Identification


Concurrent boat-based surveys were conducted in the same survey area when weather

conditions allowed (Beaufort sea state <4 and no mist or fog to reduce visual sightings). Boat-

based surveys followed the coastal tracks of the hydrophone array and were conducted from Cape

St Blaize (more commonly referred to as ‘The Point’, the furthest southerly point in the survey

area) to the Kleinbrak River mouth and back (Figure 4.1). During boat surveys, spotters were

positioned on the cabin roof of the boat (~ 3 m above sea level) to search for animals, and once S.

plumbea were observed and approached within ~ 50 m of the boat, the encounter began.

Photographic data were collected from all individuals in the group where possible. The group’s

behavioural state (travelling, social, foraging, milling) and group size were recorded at the start

and end of each encounter following Mann, (1999) and Henderson et al., (2011). The boat location

(used as a proxy for group location) was also collected on a Garmin eTrex handheld GPS unit,

which stores locations every minute (Figure 4.1). Encounters ended once all dolphins had been

photographed or left the study area (moved out of visual range).




                                                                                               148
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za

Data analysis: Acoustics


Sousa plumbea occur sympatrically with other whistling dolphins in Mossel Bay (Indo-Pacific

bottlenose dolphins Tursiops aduncus, occasionally common dolphins Delphinus delphis, and very

rarely killer whales (Orcinus orca) (Bräger, 2011; Levy, 2017). Therefore, a robust species

classifier was required to identify periods of S. plumbea encounters within the long-term

recordings. Concurrent boat and land-based surveys during the deployment period determined

T. aduncus as the only other cetacean to be present inshore in the bay during this period. During

the study period, the only reported sightings of common dolphins were offshore, well outside the

bay (reported to S. Dines as opportunistic sightings).




To identify S. plumbea whistles from the moored recordings, periods of dolphin encounters first

had to be identified to a species level. To do so, the Whistle and Moan Detector (WMD) in the

program PAMGuard was used (Gillespie et al., 2014), as well as a regionally trained, two species

(S. plumbea and T. aduncus) classifier module to detect S. plumbea encounters from the moorings.

The WMD conducts feature extraction of the whistle after filtering and noise reduction,

                                                                                             150
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




eliminating clicks and background noise. These fragments were then classified using the species

classifier module in PAMGuard. The classifier was built using training data from boat-based focal

follows along the South African coastal range of the species (settings and training data shown in

Figure 4.2). The species classifier provides a likelihood of correct species classification. The

threshold for species confirmation was raised to 0.8 based on the level of correct S. plumbea

classification in the confusion matrix (Table 4.2). Whistle outputs were grouped by encounter

using custom Python code (Chromiński et al., 2021) with acoustic encounters more than 15

minutes apart (15-minute inter-whistle interval) considered to be a new group encounter, and

only encounters with > 80% S. plumbea whistles within the encounter used for further analysis.

This conservative methodology was used to ensure only single species groups were included in

further analysis and removed any encounters with whistles that showed high similarity between

the two species.


Table 4.2. Confusion matrix for the regionally trained species classifier using the whistle classifier
module in PAMGuard for both Sousa plumbea and Tursiops aduncus based on test data throughout
South Africa. The percentage of correct classification in the training model for Sousa plumbea is
highlighted in bold.

                                                        Prediction


                                                        S. plumbea             T. aduncus

                           S. plumbea                   81.9                   18.1

 Reference
                           T. aduncus                   7.9                    92.1




                                                                                                  151
                                                                     https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Figure 4.2. Workflow used to identify Sousa plumbea whistle encounters from passive acoustic recordings. The species occur sympatrically with Tursiops aduncus
in Mossel Bay and therefore a workflow to confidently identify Sousa plumbea whistle encounters from passive acoustic recordings collected in Mossel Bay,
South Africa was necessary. Whistle and moan detector (PAMGuard; Gillespie et al., 2014 www.pamguard.org) was used to identify dolphin whistle encounters
in the recordings and a custom trained species classifier in PAMGuard used to identify Sousa plumbea whistle encounters.                             152
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




In confirmed S. plumbea acoustic encounters, whistles were manually verified and annotated in

Raven Pro v1.6 (Center for Conservation Bioacoustics, 2019). For manual annotation, a whistle

was defined as one continuous contour or two or more repeated contours (a contour being a

continuous tonal signal) separated by a period of silence between 0.03 seconds and 0.25 seconds

in duration (Kriesell et al., 2014). Each whistle was graded visually based on the signal-to-noise

ratio (SNR) (Contrast 24, Brightness 45, fast fourier transformation 1024). Signal-to-noise ratio

(SNR) was assigned from 1 – 3, 1) the signal was faint or partially masked but visible on the

spectrogram, 2) the signal was unambiguous, and 3) the signal was prominent and dominant in

the spectrogram (Gridley et al., 2015). Only good-quality whistles (those graded SNR 2 or 3) were

used in further analysis.




Stereotyped whistles were categorised into possible signature whistle types using a confirmed

signature whistle type (SWT) catalogue or using a bout analysis approach (SIGID, Janik et al.,

2013). A catalogue of S. plumbea SWTs (Gridley et al., Appendix 1) used for categorisation in this

study was generated from acoustic recordings collected from boat-based focal follows of S.

plumbea along the South African coastline. In this catalogue, stereotyped whistles were visually

categorised into signature whistle types (SWTs) following the SIGnature IDentification method

by Janik et al., (2013). As these catalogued whistle types had previously met SIGID, in this study

one capture of a whistle was required to match a whistle type in the catalogue to be classed as a

signature whistle detection. New whistle types seen in this study, not present in the catalogue,

could be added if they fulfilled SIGID criteria. To be considered a likely signature whistle (SW), a

minimum of 3 out of 4 whistles of the same type (the same frequency modulation pattern) should

occur in a bout with inter-whistle-intervals (IWIs) of 1 to 10 seconds (Janik et al., 2013). If a

stereotyped whistle was present in a bout but did not match the catalogue and did not fulfil full

SIGID criteria, it was classified as an unclassified SWT (SWTUC) (these can also be referred to as

a repeatedly emitted whistle type (REWT)). Once the whistle was assigned to a SWT, it was



                                                                                                153
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




counted as a ‘capture’, and the associated date, time and mooring were noted. Acoustic

encounters were defined from the first whistle detected in the encounter to the last, with an inter-

whistle interval of 15 minutes or more to separate encounters (Frainer et al., 2023; Lusseau,

2003). Only encounters with more than one whistle were included in further analysis to

determine encounter duration (time difference from first to last whistle).




Data analysis: Photographic Identification


During boat-based focal follows, efforts were made to ensure that every individual within the

encounter was photographed. Photo quality and distinctiveness were graded from 1 (poor

quality) to 5 (excellent) based on clarity, focus, distance, water spray and other features that

would prevent successful identification (Elwen et al., 2009). Only photographs that scored 4 or

above were used for further analysis. To determine the time taken in an encounter to identify all

individuals, the time-stamp of the photo was taken once all individuals had been photographed

clearly (a photo grade of 4 or above, and left and right sides photographed if possible). Based on

these photos, a daily ID list for each encounter was produced of the best left- and right-hand side

photos for each individual within the encounter. Repeated sightings of individuals across

encounters were noted, and individuals were matched to the national S. plumbea photo ID

catalogue where possible. The national catalogue for S. plumbea was collated through the SouSA

consortium (n = 247 individuals, Vermeulen et al., 2018) Since 2016, Sea Search Research and

Conservation has maintained and updated the catalogue, with ~ 83 new individuals added (SSRC

unpublished data). For this study, if an individual did not match the national catalogue, it was

given a new temporary reference number.




                                                                                                154
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




The efficacy of Acoustics vs Photo ID


The efficacy of photo ID surveys using boat-based focal follow methods was compared to acoustic

survey methods by assessing 1) spatial variation in acoustic detection, 2) temporal differences in

dolphin detection across each method, 3) individual detection rate across encounters in each

method, and 4) assessing financial and logistical consideration of both methods.




Spatial variation in dolphin detection was analysed across the survey area to investigate fine-

scale differences in signature whistle production throughout the array area. To analyse the effect

of different mooring locations on SW counts, a generalised additive model (GAM) was fitted using

a Poisson distribution and log link function. The model used the GAM function in R (version 4.0.2;

R Core Team, 2020) using the mgcv core R package (Wood, 2017). The response variable was the

count of SWs per mooring, and the predictor was mooring. Each mooring location was treated as

a separate level with its own parameter estimate by including the factor variable without an

intercept (factor(Mooring) - 1). The model fit was evaluated using the unbiased Risk Estimator

(UBRE), commonly used to assess the goodness of fit in models with smoothing terms, with scores

close to zero indicating appropriate model fit.




Temporal differences were investigated through the relationship between SWTs (number of

individuals detected) and SWs (total signature whistle count) per hour over the day period.

Generalised additive models were completed in R (version 4.0.2; R Core Team, 2020) using the

mgcv R package (Wood, 2017). The distribution used was Poisson and estimated using log link

function in the GAM model, as the response variable was a count. Model fit was again assessed

using an unbiased risk estimator (UBRE). GAMs are nonparametric extensions of generalised

linear models and were selected as they allow for flexible modelling of the nonlinear relationship

between the predictor and response variables (SW or SWT) by fitting a smooth function to the

predictor variable (mooring or hour) (Wood, 2006).

                                                                                              155
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Individual detection was then investigated across both methods. Descriptive statistics (mean

(SD) and minimum and maximum values) were calculated for acoustic encounter duration and

the time taken to detect all individuals in the encounter acoustically. For the total number of SWTs

detected in an encounter both the median (IQR) and maximum value were calculated. Consistency

in analysis was kept between methods with the same descriptive statistics calculated for photo

ID encounters. Each method's detection rate was measured as the number of individuals in an

encounter (SWTs or photo IDs) divided by the encounter duration (minutes). To compare the

effects of encounter duration on the number of individuals detected, the total number of

individuals detected per encounter was correlated to 1) total encounter duration (minutes) and

2) the time taken to detect all SWT or photograph all individuals within the encounter (minutes)

using Pearson’s correlation.




The detection of unique individuals over the deployment period was investigated using a

discovery curve. Cumulative unique SWTs and photo IDs showed the detection rate of new

individuals over time across both methods. For discovery curves, a population is considered open

if there is a steady accumulation of newly identified individuals over time and considered closed

if the accumulation of new individuals reaches a consistent plateau, indicating that all individuals

in a population have been detected as capture effort/time continues (Baker et al., 2006). This

study period was relatively short, with S. plumbea known to be able to move further east and west

of the Mossel Bay study area within this time period (James et al., 2015; Vermeulen et al., 2018).

Therefore, a closed population was not expected; rather, the discovery curve was conducted to

monitor the degree of accumulation of detections over this short period.




The final measure of efficacy between the two methods was logistical considerations, assessing

the cumulative effort in terms of equipment, boat time, research assistance, and financial costs


                                                                                                156
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




attributed to both methods and including a cost-benefit analysis. This comparison is important to

future researchers and managers who might be assessing the implementation of monitoring

studies and helping to inform experimental design and expectations attributed to both methods.




                                                                                             157
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   RESULTS


Acoustics


The acoustic array was deployed for 18 days with 151,004 minutes of total acoustic recordings

across six moorings. In total, there were 305 S. plumbea whistles, of which 226 were good signal-

to-noise ratios (SNR 2 or 3). Across all six moorings, 199 signature whistles were categorised into

10 SWTs. These signature whistles were classified into SWTs based on the S. plumbea SWT

catalogue from Gridley et al., (Appendix 1). New signature whistle types (that were not already

present in the catalogue) could be included if they fulfilled SIGID bout analysis. However, none of

these new stereotyped whistles met SIGID and were classed as SWTUC (n = 27 whistles).




Whistles were detected on all moorings across 38 acoustic encounters over 11 days (including

SWTUC and low SNR 1 whistles). Mooring 3 only detected low SNR whistles, which were not

considered for further analysis. Of the 10 SWTs detected, four SWTs were detected on more than

one mooring, with SWT66 detected on all moorings except for mooring 3 (Figure 4.3). Seven

SWTs were recaptured on multiple encounters, with four SWTs seen on multiple days (Figure

4.3). Two SWTs were detected on different moorings on the same day (SWT60 and SWT66).

However, the minimum time between these detections was 20 minutes, which is sufficient time

for individuals to travel between moorings (Rohr et al., 2002). No SWTs were simultaneously

recorded on multiple moorings. Moorings with the greatest number of different SWTs

(individuals) detected were moorings 1 and 6 (> 5 SWTs detected on both moorings) (Figure 4.4).

Signature whistle detection (overall whistle count) also varied across moorings, with 80% of

signature whistles detected at moorings 1 and 4 (Figure 4.4). Across encounters, several

individuals dominated, with SWT66, SWT60 and SWT5, accounting for 73% of total SW

detections.

                                      https://scholar.sun.ac.za

south coast of South Africa, representing the spatial variability of dolphin detections between sites.
Bars represent the signature whistle count of SNR 2 and 3 whistles at each mooring. Bars are
divided by signature whistle type (SWT, proxy for individuals).




To investigate the temporal detection of individuals, signature whistle counts were analysed

across a 24-hour period, split into SWTs (individuals) and overall dolphin detections (SWs),

including SWTUC. The diurnal pattern for SWT production (including SWTUCs) showed a diurnal

effect of SWTs (individuals) detected (proportion of SWTs detected; Day 41% Night 59%). Total

dolphin call detections (SW count) also showed diurnal variability, with 62% of acoustic

detections at nighttime (1800 - 0600) (Figure 4.5a). However, one encounter at 0900 accounted

for a significant spike in whistle count (SWT5). Omitting this outlier and SWTUCs, 25% of SWs

were produced during the day, while 75% of SWs were produced at night, showing a higher

diurnal pattern in signature whistle production (Figure 4.5b).

                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za

                                                                                              161
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




For spatial analysis, a generalised additive model (GAM) was used to analyse the relationship

between the number of SWs per mooring (signature whistle count) and the mooring locations

(mooring). SWTUC and outliers were removed from the modelling (SWT5, 0900 encounter). The

model indicated significant differences in SW counts across moorings (p < 0.01, all except for

mooring 3), with estimate effects highest for mooring 1 (4.58). The UBRE score was -0.94,

indicating an appropriate fit for the Poisson-distributed response variable.




To explore the relationship between the number of individuals calling (number of unique SWTs)

per hour and the time of day, a GAM was fitted with a smooth term for hour, again omitting

SWTUC and outliers. The model showed a significant nonlinear relationship between the number

of unique SWTs per hour and the time of day (se = 0.05, z = 19.1, p < 0.005). The smooth term for

hour was significant (edf = 7.7, chi-sq = 59.3, p < 0.005), suggesting that the number of individuals

detected (SWT) vary throughout the day when SWTUC and outliers are removed (Figure 4.6b).

Again, the UBRE score was -0.8, indicating an appropriate model fit for the Poisson-distributed

response variable. When SWTs per hour were investigated across hour time bins, significant

levels of detection were seen for time bins 0000 - 0600 and 1700 -2000 (p < 0.005).




To investigate the relationship between overall signature whistle count per hour and the time of

day, a GAM was fitted with a smooth term for hour, again omitting SWTUC and the outlier

encounter. The model showed a significant nonlinear relationship between the number of SWs

per hour and the time of day (edf = 2.7 se = 0.02 z = 125 p < 0.005). The smooth term for hour

was also highly significant (edf = 8.8 chi-sq = 478.5, p < 0.005), again suggesting a complex,

nonlinear relationship between the time of day and the number of SWs per hour (Figure 4.6b).

The model's UBRE score was 0.7, indicating an appropriate fit for the Poisson-distributed

response variable. When investigated across time bins, signature whistle counts per hour were




                                                                                                 162
                                           https://scholar.sun.ac.za




    significant throughout (p < 0.5). However, estimated coefficients were lowest for periods 0700-

    1600, showing a smaller effect on SW detection during these hours.

                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Individual acoustic detection was measured across the 38 signature whistle encounters

(including whistles of SNR 2 or 3 only and removing SWTUC and outliers). The mean rate of SWs

detected across acoustic encounters was 0.89 SW/minute (± 1.48 SD), with encounter durations

ranging from 00:00:13 to 00:31:57 (hh:mm:ss). The median number of different SWTs (number

of calling individuals) detected in an encounter was 2 (IQR 1 - 2) with a maximum of three unique

SWTs detected in an acoustic encounter. Compared to the full duration of the acoustic

deployment, the cumulative encounter duration (03:53:05 hh:mm:ss) was short, only 0.15% of

the total recording effort, with each acoustic encounter lasting on average 00:06:14 (± 00:08:18

SD). There was no correlation between total SWTs detected in an encounter and 1) acoustic

encounter duration and 2) the time taken to detect all the SWTs within the encounter (Pearson’s

correlation R = -0.03 and R = 0.17, respectively).




Photographic Identification


There was a total of 13 boat-based survey days across the 18-day acoustic deployment period

(28:39 hh:mm at sea). In total, there were eight encounters with S. plumbea on seven different

days. The mean encounter duration was 00:46:22 hh:mm:ss (± 00:21:17 SD), with a mean time

taken to identify all individuals in an encounter 00:24:00 (± 00:15:34 SD). Across all encounters,

the time taken to photograph all individuals was 3.7% of the total recording effort (boat-based

survey time). The boat-based survey covered the inshore acoustic deployment array area with all

photo ID encounters concentrated in two regions along this area, around mooring 1 (Harbour

entrance) and past mooring 6 (Hartenbos river mouth) (Figure 4.1).




The median number of individuals photographed in a group was 3 (IQR 1 - 4), with a maximum

of six individuals photographed in an encounter. Across the eight encounters, 16 different

individuals were identified, and six individuals were seen across different days. Calves were


                                                                                              164
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




visually confirmed and photographed within the group across four encounters. Six individuals

were able to be matched to the national catalogue. With a dedicated photographer on board, the

mean individual detection rate for photo ID was 0.08 individuals photographed/minute (± 0.05

SD) (Table 4.3). There was no correlation between the total number of individuals photographed

in the encounter and 1) photo ID encounter duration and 2) the time taken to photograph all the

individuals in the encounter (Pearson’s correlation R = 0.27 and R = 0.30, respectively).

Acoustic methods had a slightly faster rate of cumulative detections in the first half of the

recording period compared to boat-based surveys, recording more than half the total SWTs in the

first half of the recording period (seven new SWTs were detected in the first nine days, 64% of

total SWTs detected) (Figure 4.7). Across photo ID surveys, only half of the total individuals had

been photographed after eight survey days (out of 13 total survey days). Acoustic and Photo ID

unique detections both plateaued at day 3, for six and five days respectively, during which there

were three boat surveys where no new individuals were photographically identified. Acoustic

                                                                                              165

detections again plateaued toward the end of the survey period on day 15, however, this is likely

limited by deployment duration.

Logistical Considerations


In this study, the survey costs for acoustic methods, including boat-based deployment and

retrieval, with equipment and sustenance costs, had a total cost of 367,264 ZAR due to the

inclusion of hydrophone and equipment purchasing (Table 4.4). Once these costs are removed

(as hydrophones can be used across multiple projects over many years), the costs fall to 17,264

ZAR for the four days required to set up, deploy and retrieve the acoustic array, excluding

personnel time. Operational costs for personnel time for the researcher conducting the

deployment through set up, during deployment/retrieval and post-processing and analysis, at a

daily stipend of 200 ZAR per hour, equates to operation costs of 105,400 ZAR. Equalling a total

cost for the acoustic method of 122,664 ZAR.




Photo ID survey costs were calculated based on the total 18-day fieldwork period with 13 sea

days. This equated to 28:39:00 hh:mm:ss of boat-based survey time of which there was a

cumulative 05:52:00 (hh:mm:ss) S. plumbea encounter duration. The boat-based survey cost for

photo ID was 161,000 ZAR, including land travel, accommodation, subsistence, and boat fuel/hire

costs and skipper costs. Depending on deployment locations, these costs are also incurred during

acoustic deployment and retrieval, usually a maximum of 2-4 days. This cost also included

equipment costs, such as an SLR camera and a lens, removing equipment costs brings the boat-

based survey costs down to 61,000 ZAR. Operational costs to include one researcher for the field

work, data processing and analysis was 41,400 ZAR. Therefore, the total costs for photo ID were

102,400 ZAR for the 18-day deployment period. Most research groups will have access to

camera and hydrophone equipment; therefore, these equipment costs were removed from

the final cost for each method. These total costs were then calculated per dolphin captured to

produce a final result of 12,266.40 ZAR.dolphin-1 for acoustic methods and 6,400 ZAR.dolphin-1

for photo ID methods.



                                                                                            167
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




When evaluating the use of these methods, several factors must be considered (Table 4.4).

Acoustic deployments, for instance, require time for setting up hydrophones and constructing

moorings. In this study, with an array of six hydrophones, the setup time was minimal, taking only

half a day. Similarly, boat-based deployment times can vary depending on the size of the array

area; however, this study focused on a single bay, allowing deployment to be completed in just a

few hours, with half a day allocated for both deployment and retrieval. Although processing

acoustic data can be time-consuming, the relatively small scale of this study, limited to six

hydrophones over an 18-day deployment, meant that processing time was shorter compared to

larger acoustic studies. The most time-consuming aspect was the analytical process, which

involved extracting, annotating, and categorising whistles into an existing SWT catalogue, which

spanned several weeks.




In this study, boat-based surveys were conducted over a two-week period to align with the

acoustic deployment period. This required having a boat on standby throughout the period, ready

to be deployed during suitable weather conditions. The setup time for boat-based surveys was

minimal, as trained personnel only needed to ensure that equipment was charged, and data

sheets were prepared before launching. The primary time commitment for boat-based surveys

was the surveys themselves. Post-processing of the data was also relatively quick, with daily

photo IDs completed within a few hours after each survey day. A direct comparison of costs and

logistical considerations between the methods is provided in Table 4.4, while a general SWOT

(strengths, weaknesses, obstacles and threats) analysis of both methods is presented in Table 4.5.

                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za

   DISCUSSION
The study aimed to investigate the efficacy of both photo ID surveys and passive acoustic

monitoring methods to detect and monitor an elusive coastal delphinid species in South Africa.

For threatened species with low population sizes, boat-based focal follow surveys for photo ID

can be a time-consuming and expensive research method requiring boat access and hire, as well

as repeated surveys over several weeks and repeated over multiple years to generate sufficient

data for managers and policymakers (Braulik et al., 2018). South Africa lacks long-term consistent

studies over time and regions, with all available studies localised and never exceeding 3 years

(James et al., 2015, Table 7). In this study, encounter rates were relatively low across both

methods, indicative of this species’ low population size throughout the coastline, with a lower

encounter rate using photo ID methods, but comparable to those of previous studies on the

species in South Africa (James et al., 2015, Table 7). The study found clear temporal differences

with acoustic activity greater during the night period, showing high dolphin activity during a

period unavailable to boat-based surveying. Spatially, both methods detected high levels of

dolphins around the Harbour entrance (mooring 1), with boat-based encounters also high around

Kleinbrak River mouth areas (mooring 6) and acoustic detections also high around Hartenbos

(mooring 4). Boat-based encounters only occurred in two areas of the bay, whereas acoustic

detections across the whole array show dolphins moving throughout the deployment area.

Financial considerations showed that due to a higher analysis time and the lower total number

of individuals detected, acoustic methods had a greater cost per dolphin than photo ID methods.

It is important to understand the differences between these methods and how variability in both

field-based differences and logistical considerations affects visual and acoustic detection when

deciding to use either methodology for effective data collection.



An important consideration for this study is the short survey period, as this was designed to

reflect a realistic time frame in which field trips to distant locations or environmental impact

assessments are often made. Environmental impact assessments and rapid biological


                                                                                              171
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




assessments are often required in marine spatial planning, harbour construction and marine

construction (Mubanga & Kwarteng, 2020). Rapid environmental assessments for threatened

species are important as many are underrepresented in published studies with ranges and

movements poorly understood (Becker et al., 2021). Effective methods to collect broad-scale

baseline data on threatened species over a limited temporal period are key to enabling effective

conservation policy (Braulik et al., 2017). In this short-term study, understanding how acoustic

monitoring can be best used to detect the presence/absence of S. plumbea under a limited time

frame is an important consideration if this is to be proposed as a viable monitoring method. This

study's key result was the spatial and temporal variability in dolphin detection using both

methods. Acoustically, dolphins were detected throughout the 8 km acoustic array across both

day and night periods, with higher acoustic activity at night, indicating a broader scale of

detection than if only boat-based encounter data was used.



Survey design includes deciding where to deploy hydrophones and recording set-up (duty

cycling). This is often a tradeoff between maximising deployment time and conserving battery

and storage space. The study found no correlation between individuals detected (SWTs) and

acoustic encounter duration. If correlated, this metric could influence future duty cycling settings,

requiring extended recording duration in future experimental designs. However, average S.

plumbea acoustic encounters were less than 10 minutes (mean 00:06:14 ± 00:08:18 SD).

Therefore, the standard duty cycles often used for cetaceans, with periods of 10 or more minutes

off, would mean whole encounters would be missed for this species. Ensuring the periods of

dormancy within duty cycles are short enough not to miss encounters requires the focal species'

acoustic behaviour to be taken into account (Fearey et al., 2022). When designing rapid

assessments and field studies, these temporal impacts on detection are important to understand.

In South Africa, there is a growing need for minimum data requirements to determine basic

empirical data such as species presence, abundance, and distribution. These are typically left up

to the environmental assessment practitioner and often left out of research studies (See Evidence


                                                                                                 172
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Conservation – Downey, 2022). Optimum requirements of a continuous recording schedule in

vocally elusive species, such as S. plumbea, is important information to enable threatened species

of cetacean to be included in global and regional initiatives. This allows for these species to be

included in assessments when identifying important biodiversity areas such as ecologically or

biologically significant marine areas (EBSA), important marine mammal areas (IMMAS) or key

biodiversity areas (KBA) as well as feed into the environmental impact assessment process.



Spatially, there was a variability in dolphin detection between the two methods. Boat-based

surveys covered the whole array area, but S. plumbea encounters occurred in two areas, around

moorings 1 and 6. This could be due to a bias in boat surveys occurring in the morning (during

good weather periods), i.e. aligning to when dolphins rested or socialised in these areas, or a

strong fine-scale distinction of higher use in only these two areas with little to no occupancy

elsewhere. The latter is not the case through acoustic data, and dolphins move throughout the

array area, with the highest level of acoustic activity occurring during the night. This has

previously been reported in other studies, where diurnal activity has been detected in dolphin

species in False Bay, South Africa, with higher levels of acoustic behaviour at night, indicating

inshore feeding areas, previously unknown to researchers (A. Prinsloo unpublished). Land-based

observations in Mossel Bay by James (2014) observed mixed habitat use by S. plumbea

throughout the Mossel Bay coastline, with observed surface behaviours strongly linked to

substrate type. Substrate type varies throughout the array area, from fine-grained sand to wave-

cut rocky platforms. Moorings were purposefully deployed around a sandy substrate for easy

retrieval and minimal noise from reefs or snapping shrimp that would mask or overload the

hydrophone recordings (Aubauer et al., 2000). James (2014) found socialising behaviour

predominantly on sandy beach habitats, a behaviour which is linked to higher signature whistle

production in other delphinid species (Cook et al., 2004), while the primary observed surface

behaviour of S. plumbea in Mossel Bay was travelling. Previous chapters showed behaviour and

group composition to have a minimal effect on signature whistle production in S. plumbea;


                                                                                              173
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




therefore, the effect of mooring location is likely not to have affected signature whistle detection

in this study. Boat-based encounters are behaviour-dependent and are primarily focused on non-

travelling behaviour to safely stay with the focal group, occurring in one area while photo ID can

be collated. Therefore, there is a bias to non-travelling behaviour during boat-based surveys. This

bias is removed using acoustic arrays, as highlighted through the continuous acoustic spatial and

temporal detection of S. plumbea throughout the array.



Alongside spatial and temporal efficacy, individual detection was used as a key determinant of an

effective monitoring method. The individual detection rate was measured to directly compare

individual detection across both methods. Measured as the total number of individuals detected

in an encounter, divided by encounter duration, acoustic detection methods were faster.

Detection probability is a key bias in capture-recapture studies using signature whistles (Fearey

et al., 2022). Naturally occurring sounds (SWs) and marks on an animal (photo ID) have inherent

biases regarding their use in mark-recapture. An assumption of acoustic detection through

signature whistles is the requirement for every dolphin in a group to signal to conspecifics using

their signature whistle. S. plumbea are known to have small group sizes and low signature whistle

production rates (Chapter 3). Therefore, it is reasonable to assume that in S. plumbea, group sizes

are underrepresented using only acoustic methods of individual detection. Yet, all dolphins can

ultimately be detected over time, given the constraints of animal behaviour and hydrophone

deployments. In contrast, many animals can never be identified in photo ID studies due to a lack

of marks or scars. This applies both within groups (so group size is not typically estimated from

photos in most studies but by visual estimation) and across whole studies, resulting in the need

to extrapolate mark-recapture estimates of marked animals upwards to account for these

unmarked animals (see Elwen et al., 2019; Urian et al., 2014). In addition, the observed acoustic

asymptote at the end of the sampling period is likely due to the limited survey period and is more

likely a plateau often seen over sampling periods. The actual asymptote would be reached once a

greater number of individual whistles have been detected, following the idea that all dolphins


                                                                                                174
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




would be acoustically detected given a long enough sampling period. Therefore, the use of a

discovery curve in this study demonstrates the rate of cumulative individuals detected over time

rather than providing any results on the reliability of an asymptote due to the relatively short

sampling period.



The strength of utilising acoustic methods is the versatility of the data acquired and the large

amounts of acoustic data it produces over time. Both photo ID and acoustic methods can detect

non-target species if in the vicinity during the survey; however, the strength of acoustic detection

is the wider array of sound types that can be recorded. Photo ID is limited to species that surface

near the boat during target species focal follows. A moored acoustic array will record all species

that vocalise, even when the target species is not present. This study focused on S. plumbea

detection, yet other whistling delphinid species (including clicks), baleen whales, fish and

anthropogenic sounds were also detected in the recordings. The high initial costs of acoustic tools

(such as equipment costs) can, therefore, be shared between multiple researchers and

organisations, increasing the applicability of the dataset. For example, a key use of these datasets

is the contribution to soundscapes and monitoring of anthropogenic noise. Until 2022, no

soundscape studies had been published in South Africa (Schoeman et al., 2022). Soundscape

monitoring is developing as an important tool in identifying long-term changes in the

contribution of anthropogenic noise to underwater sound environments. Mossel Bay is a working

harbour with several single-point mooring buoys situated 800 m – 1 km offshore, used for

refuelling large tanker ships moored in the bay. Local port authorities have included Mossel Bay

as a priority area for future development, which would directly impact S. plumbea, as seen in this

study, due to their high use of the area near the harbour entrance (Bloom, 2019). At current levels,

anthropogenic noise levels vary across the bay, and although these weren’t measured as part of

this study, there is the potential for this to increase in the future with continued coastal

development (See planned Operation Phakisa, Findlay, 2018). The use of large acoustic datasets

for long-term soundscape modelling has previously been hindered by computational power for


                                                                                                175
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




signal extraction. The recent development of machine learning and automated processing tools

means these large datasets can be processed and analysed more rapidly (Frainer et al., 2023; Gibb

et al., 2019). The future direction of this data and our understanding of S. plumbea habitat threats

requires an understanding of baseline soundscape metrics to assess future impacts on this

threatened species adequately.



While acoustic datasets are incredibly versatile, photographic data can also provide additional

data on a population equally useful for researchers and policymakers. Humpback whales

(Megaptera novaeangliae) are one of the most widely photographed cetaceans, and the recent

development of open-source, online fluke-matching programs has rapidly advanced the reach of

these programs (Cheeseman et al., 2017; Patton et al., 2023). Providing increasingly more

information on the global migration routes of humpback whales, such as tail fluke matches

between South Africa and Brazil, matches that would have previously taken a great deal of

international collaboration and work to find (Ramos et al., 2023). Across cetacean species, photo

ID methods have provided additional information on killer whale scarring in humpback whales

(Elwen et al., 2014), entanglement rates (Robbins & Mattila, 2004), sex assignment (James et al.,

2022), intraspecific aggression (Felix & Novillo, 2015), and skin disease (Kiszka et al., 2009).

Photo ID methods have also provided important data on S. plumbea populations, namely calving

intervals and injuries (Frainer et al., 2023; Plön et al., 2021). In gregarious species with a high

occurrence of surface visibility, photo ID methods are still a useful tool in species monitoring.

However, when using photo ID as a tool for detection, in species that are rarely observed

surfacing, such as Blainville beaked whales (Mesoplodon densirostris), who spend a predominant

amount of time in deep water feeding, it has been estimated that it would require 25 - 30 years of

photo ID data to detect a decline of 5%, further highlighting the ineffective use of this method

across all species (Marques et al., 2019). Yet, when available, and often through opportunistic

sightings, the use of photo ID for threatened species is still an important method to provide




                                                                                                176
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




additional data to population studies, especially in species where targeted boat-based surveys are

infrequent.



Logistical evaluation between the two methods provides ways to reduce the costs involved to

allow both methods to be accessible to a wide range of researchers and managers. Field-work

costs considered for the acoustic deployment cover only the travel to and from the study site and

the four days for deployment and retrieval of the acoustic array. Some variables are constant in

both methods, such as travel to and from the study site (potentially more for multiple acoustic

deployments or complications), daily subsistence, stipends and accommodation costs. The total

costs for the acoustic deployment are greater than boat-based surveys due to the greater start-

up costs of the hydrophone array, with one SoundTrap hydrophone costing ~70,000 ZAR and

greater analysis time required for acoustic data. Excluding equipment purchasing from acoustic

methods, the cost of deploying and retrieving an acoustic array vastly decreases. In this study, the

‘cost per dolphin’ calculated throughout the two-week data collection period using acoustic

methods is nearly twice that of photo ID detection methods, mainly due to the high analytical time

required for processing and analysing acoustic data. These measures are subject to change in the

future as machine learning methods are rapidly improving both fields, in particular methods to

automate the classification of vocalisations (Frainer et al., 2023; Jiang et al., 2019; Patton et al.,

2023).



The main limitation of photo ID studies is the high effort in boat and personnel time required,

which accounts for a large portion of the costs incurred during visual-based surveys, especially

in elusive species with low encounter rates, as seen in this study. The optimum requirement of at

least a minimum of three people to conduct boat-based surveys (data, photo ID, skipper)

considerably increases the costs of photo ID studies. Additionally, effort is highly dependent on

camera equipment, camera lens quality, skipper skill, and skill of the photographer and ultimately

requires finding the focal species and spending extended repeated periods with the animals to


                                                                                                  177
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




collect data. As with camera equipment, some research groups will have access to hydrophones,

utilised across multiple research projects.



The application of acoustic methods in the future will require careful consideration of financial

constraints, particularly the need for multiple hydrophones to extend the detection range, which

remains a key limitation of this approach. The development of automated whistle extractions and

species classifier software now requires far less computational power (Frainer et al., 2023). In

this study, the costs and logistical considerations were comparable because they were conducted

simultaneously. It should be noted that in future applications of either method, there are caveats

that can both reduce these factors. Photo ID can be collected from tourism boats if access for

researchers is free or through the use of public or citizen science data, as has been seen to be

effective through the online free-to-use platform, HappyWhale, for humpback whales (Megaptera

novaeangliae) (Cheeseman et al., 2017). Equipment costs such as cameras and hydrophones are

often already covered by research groups or collaborators and may not be required in set-up

costs. Boat hire, in general, is one of the most expensive costs of any field work and was heavily

subsidised in this study. This should be factored in for larger deployments at considerable

additional cost. However, with the potential for cost reduction in future applications, the

increased affordability of research methods is a hopeful prospect for the field of cetacean research

and conservation.



In conclusion, while photo ID is a valuable and accessible tool, passive acoustic monitoring

provides a useful alternative method due to its broad temporal and spatial coverage and ability

to replicate a study exactly over time. Once initial high start-up costs are removed, passive

acoustic monitoring becomes an accessible method for researchers and conservation managers

due to its ease of data collection, versatile application of acoustic datasets, and continuous animal

detection capabilities. Advances in post-processing tools and data management further ensure

that passive acoustic monitoring will continue to be a valuable approach for gathering data


                                                                                                 178
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




relevant to various species and research questions. In this study, acoustic detection methods

provided useful information on species movement throughout the array, including increased

acoustic activity at night and a greater spatial range of detection than boat-based methods.

Ultimately, both methods can be used alongside each other and often complement one another.

However, in a targeted short-term assessment, continuous acoustic detection methods proved to

be a reliable alternative to photo ID, offering reduced field effort, while effectively monitoring

spatial and temporal movements of S. plumbea.

     5 SWORD: SIGNATURE WHISTLES FOR OCCURRENCE,
     RECAPTURE AND DENSITY FOR INDIAN OCEAN HUMPBACK
     DOLPHINS, SOUSA PLUMBEA IN SOUTH AFRICA


Sasha Dines1,2 Guilherme Frainer1,3, Jack Fearey1,3, Simon Elwen1,2, Enrico Gennari 4, John Measey5,
