Prepared for methods in ecology and evolution (BES) journals


                                                                                                187
                                          https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   ABSTRACT

Capture-recapture (or mark-recapture) is a key tool in conservation for studying population
trends, survival, and habitat use. Passive acoustic monitoring, using signature whistles as proxies
for individual detection, offers a less invasive and time-consuming alternative to standard visual
survey detection methods. This framework of using signature whistles for individual detection
and monitoring has been termed SWORD – signature whistle for occurrence, recapture and
density. This proof-of-concept study deployed a 120 km array of five hydrophones for 42 days
across three high-use bays (Mossel Bay, Buffelsbaai and Plettenberg Bay) within the central part
of the range of south coast population range of Indian Ocean humpback dolphins (Sousa plumbea).
Across all five moorings, 1282 total signature whistles were detected from 25 different signature
whistle types (SWTs) or individuals. Occurrence was seen across the array with high detections
on two moorings at Mossel Bay 1 and Buffelsbaai 7 (77% of total detections). Closed (Huggins)
models were used to estimate the number of animals using each of the three bays during the study
period (Mossel Bay: 8 (95% CI 8-8); Buffelsbaai: 19 (95% CI 14-53); Plettenberg Bay: 22 (95% CI
4012 19-33). For the combined dataset, “open” population models (POPAN JCS) and spatially
explicit mark-recapture models (SECR) were applied to obtain baseline population assessments
for the south coast region. A range of SECR models explored different mask resolutions, truncated
datasets, and mooring configurations. In all models, density, D, was very low (D ranged from
0.000061 - 0.000439 animals.ha-2). Across the different SECR models, abundance was
extrapolated by multiplying the predicted D estimate by the effective study area. These different
models all resulted in similar estimates to those from the capture-recapture open population
model (SECR abundance model estimates ranged from 23-26; POPAN Jolly and Seber super
population estimate of 26 individuals (95% CI 23-30). These estimates from the combined study
sites indicated a high number of recaptures throughout the survey period and little effect on
abundance estimates from fine-scale trap configuration. Therefore, this suggests that when
limited by deployment time and equipment, expanding the geographic range of the acoustic array
is more effective for increasing individual unique recaptures than increasing the density of
recorders within a smaller area. A growing preference for density estimates in conservation
management due to the useful inclusion of spatial range demonstrates that SECR and population
abundance models are adequate for assessing population estimates using individual acoustic
monitoring in elusive coastal dolphins.




                                                                                               188
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   INTRODUCTION

Effective wildlife management strategies rely on accurate estimates of population occurrence,

abundance and density (Marques et al., 2013). Detection of individuals within a capture-

recapture framework is beneficial for assessing small, at-risk populations, where the loss of one

individual can significantly affect population health (Lacy, 2000; Parra et al., 2018). Producing a

population estimate of animal abundance over multiple years can be challenging. However, these

estimates are beneficial for management as it helps identify trends in population size and how

the use of the study area might change over time, even if the estimate produced is not the total

population abundance and variation around these estimates is high (Bejder et al., 2006; Elwen et

al., 2009). Density is often seen to be a more meaningful metric for wildlife management than

abundance (Forsyth et al., 2019; Glen & Dickman, 2005). Calculating the number of animals within

a known area describes a population within its habitat, or sections thereof, enabling more

effective management strategies (Forsyth et al., 2019). Both abundance and density estimates are

helpful, but with the development of spatially explicit capture-recapture models. (Efford, 2011),

estimating density has become more accessible, and both of these methods are beneficial for

managers and conservation (O’Brien et al., 2005).



The most widely used index of conservation status is the IUCN Red List assessment, which guides

global conservation priorities and policy (Marsh et al., 2023). When assessing risk criteria for a

species or population, IUCN assessments consider various factors, including abundance,

distribution or isolation, and anthropogenic and environmental threats. Some of the more critical

risk criteria are knowledge of geographic range, population size and density, and incidents of

population fragmentation across known habitats (Marsh et al., 2023). The extent of a species'

occurrence, recapture rates, and density require empirical temporal and spatial data. These

metrics are difficult to ascertain for many species, especially in the marine environment. Despite

challenges, advancements in capture-recapture methods have made it possible to collect this



                                                                                               189
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




data, and future progress towards these methods is necessary to develop conservation efforts

with more valuable estimates.



Acoustic production is highly prevalent in marine mammals and is used for navigation, foraging,

and communication with conspecifics (Lammers et al., 2006). In species where monitoring is

hindered using traditional visual observation methods, such in the marine environment, acoustic

monitoring has become widely accepted as a method for cetacean detection (Mellinger et al.,

2007), enabling a range of new study designs and methods (Marques et al., 2013; Ryder et al.,

2023). In marine mammals, traditional visual surveys, which require boat-based effort, favour

gregarious species with frequent surfacing behaviour. These methods can be costly and are

limited by weather and daylight (Chapter 4). Cetaceans are highly vocal, and passive acoustic

survey methods, such as moored hydrophones or towed arrays, provide a far more continuous

dataset, enabling the development and applications of long-term acoustic datasets. This is

exemplified by studies on the highly elusive beaked whales (Ziphius spp.) or sperm whales

(Physeter macrocephalus), that vocalise almost exclusively during deep foraging dives but are

primarily silent during their ascent and their short surface intervals (Johnson et al., 2004). Passive

acoustic monitoring (PAM) provides an alternative study method for detecting species that would

be overlooked due to “historical" logistical constraints. Methodologically, the versatility of PAM

utilises a range of sensors to record underwater sound, from single-dipping hydrophones (Fearey

et al., 2019) to autonomous gliders (Cauchy et al., 2023) and even real-time 3-dimensional

detections of porpoises (Macaulay et al., 2022). In recent years, improvements in computational

power, battery life, and storage space have allowed large sets of data to be collected, allowing for

post-processing analyses and in-situ processing to detect, classify and localise marine mammals

(Gillespie et al., 2013). The exponential growth of scale within PAM has provided new

opportunities for scientific research. These developments have especially benefited Critically

Endangered species, such as the North Atlantic right whale (Eubalaena glacialis). Large multi-

disciplinary PAM projects have been conducted throughout the North Atlantic right whale


                                                                                                  190
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




migratory corridors, combining static moorings and autonomous gliders. These studies have

supported recommendations on seasonal adjustments to shipping lanes to safeguard the

migratory corridors of these Critically Endangered species (Durette-Morin et al., 2022).




Although cetaceans have been acoustically recorded since the ~1950s (e.g. Watkins sound

library, Sayigh et al., 2017), PAM as a monitoring tool has only been used for research since the

early 1990s when towed arrays were used to detect sperm whales (Leaper et al., 1992). More

recently, PAM has been used in a variety of ways, with hydrophones deployed from vessels, free-

floating or moored and used to estimate abundance, investigate habitat ranges and density, or

localisation for mitigation techniques (reviewed in Cato et al., 2006; Sousa-Lima et al., 2013;

Zimmer, 2011). More recently, PAM has been used to estimate abundance by identifying signature

whistles in T. truncatus within a capture-recapture framework (Fearey et al., 2022; Longden et

al., 2020; Romeu et al., 2024). Signature whistles are stereotyped whistles produced by an

individual, usually in bouts. The long-term, stereotyped tonal signals function to convey the

identity and presence of the whistling individual to conspecifics, as well as facilitate group

cohesion and assist in mother-calf reunions (Janik & Slater, 1998; King et al., 2016; Smolker et al.,

1993). Signature whistles can account for 38 -70% of whistles produced in free-ranging dolphins,

with their production behaviourally and contextually dependent in Tursiops spp. (Cook et al.,

2004). Applying the same capture-recapture framework widely developed for photo ID (Rogan

et al., 2000; Vermeulen & Bräger, 2015) to signature whistle detections from long-term PAM can

overcome limitations of spatial and temporal resolution. The high production rate of these

individual signals offers a viable alternative to boat-based photo-ID methods (Fearey et al., 2021).

Signature whistle detection using PAM only requires detection on a single hydrophone.

Therefore, with a multiple hydrophone array, density metrics can also be estimated using

spatially explicit capture-recapture models ( Borchers & Efford, 2008).




                                                                                                 191
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




The incorporation of a spatial point process to capture-recapture models allows for an extension

of previous methods as they incorporate spatial information on where captures are made and

where they are absent (Borchers & Marques, 2017; Efford et al., 2009; Noss et al., 2012; Royle et

al., 2009). Spatially explicit capture-recapture (SECR) models estimate density using an array of

traps (Efford, 2004). SECR has become a robust application in many fields (Kalle et al., 2011;

Marques et al., 2013; Sollmann et al., 2011) and enables the estimation of home ranges and

density outside of the survey area (Borchers & Efford, 2008). Applications range from cage

trapping possums (Efford et al., 2005), acoustically “trapping” birds using song and time-of-

arrival (Dawson & Efford, 2009) and camera trapping tigers, identifying individually unique

marks (Royle et al., 2009). Within these applications, many different detectors for “capture” are

possible, such as physical traps, which are limited to capturing one individual per occasion, to

proximity detectors, such as camera traps, or microphones/hydrophones, which can record

multiple individual numerous times within a single capture occasion. Spatially explicit capture-

recapture allows density to be explicitly modelled and estimates the home range directly

(Borchers, 2012). SECR provides an estimate of the spatial extent of the survey range from

detectors deployed, improving the effectiveness of results for managers and researchers to

implement appropriate conservation measures. SECR has previously been applied to non-

individual calls, such as minke whale 'boing' sounds (Marques et al., 2012), as well as being used

in other species, such as owls (Martin et al., 2024) and frogs (Stevenson et al., 2015) through

identifying individuals based on time of arrival. Individual identification of SWTs has successfully

been shown in T. truncatus to produce comparable estimates similar to photo-ID studies

(Longden et al., 2020). This chapter builds on these earlier successes. Rather than arrival time of

calls, uses SECR based on recaptures of signature whistles of S. plumbea across single

hydrophones in an SECR framework to calculate animal abundance and density.



In South Africa, S. plumbea is a highly coastal delphinid and the most threatened marine mammal.

(Plön et al., 2016). Recent studies have suggested that the population is fragmented along the


                                                                                                192
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




southern African coastline and appears to have reduced substantially since the late 1990s (Plön

et al., 2021; Vermeulen et al., 2018). There is no national abundance estimate for this species in

South Africa, but photo ID estimates have been published for several regions. (Conry, 2017; James

et al., 2015), with Karczmarski, (1996), extrapolating local abundance estimates from Algoa Bay

to suggest a national abundance of fewer than 1000 individuals in the late 1990s. Regional

estimates from the south coast population have provided catalogues of 24 – 96 distinct

individuals (Plettenberg Bay: 41 Greenwood, 2013, 63 Jobson, 2006; Mossel Bay: 67 James et al.,

2015; Algoa Bay: 50 Bouveroux et al., 2018, 70 Karczmarski & Cockcroft, 1998). Closed model

estimates on these distinct catalogues vary, with estimated abundances ranging from 42 (SE:42-

55) to 112 (SE: 75-133 Greenwood, 2013; Jobson, 2006). Open population models for the south

coast estimate higher abundances of 84 (SE 72 - 115) – 466 (SE 447- 485) individuals (Conry,

2017; Karczmarski & Cockcroft, 1998; Karczmarski et al., 1999). Across these regional studies,

high boat-based effort and a low encounter rate limit the range of study areas and

the extrapolation of these estimates.



Vermeulen et al., (2018) was the first study to attempt a regional assessment of how these

populations and abundance estimates were related, collating 16 years of photo ID data from

various organisations and researchers across South Africa. The study identified many coastal

movements between sites used in previous matches, with a clear separation of a south and east

coast population, and ultimately identified 247 well-marked individuals. Non-aligned timelines

for the data sets meant capture-recapture models were not possible. Still, the study suggested an

update to the Karczmarski, (1999) estimate of fewer than 500 individuals (Vermeulen et al.,

2018).



The use of naturally occurring, individually distinct acoustic markers, such as signature whistles,

has been suggested as an effective tool for species where other monitoring methods are

inconsistent and difficult to obtain (Janik et al., 2013; Terry et al., 2005). This thesis has explored


                                                                                                   193
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




aspects that might affect detection using individual acoustic monitoring with passive acoustic

monitoring demonstrated to be an effective tool for individual acoustic detection using signature

whistles (Chapter 4). In this chapter, I aim to build on these previous chapters, providing a proof-

of-concept using the SWORD (signature whistles for occurrence, recapture and density)

framework (Fearey et al., 2021). The chapter investigates both fine-scale deployment location

and organisation of the array. It presents a proof of concept for PAM's effectiveness, using

capture-recapture and SECR models across both individual bays and cumulatively, to assess

population dynamics based on a central portion of the south coast population of S. plumbea in

South Africa.




                                                                                                194
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   METHODS
Data collection


A preliminary investigation was conducted into the efficacy of using signature whistles (signature

whistle type, SWT, to denote an individual) as the identifying feature of occurrence. SWTs were

used to create capture histories for capture-recapture (CR) models to estimate abundance,

investigating both open and closed models within each bay and for the entire range. SWTs were

also used in SECR models to estimate density and home range and extrapolate abundance over

the study area.




Data were collected from five moorings deployed in three bays within the center of S. plumbea’s

south coast range (Mossel Bay, Buffelsbaai and Plettenberg Bay) (Figure 5.1), covering ~120 km

of coastline. Where two receivers were placed in the same bay (i.e., Mossel Bay and Plettenberg

Bay), the two receivers were spaced far enough apart (3 km) to ensure that individuals could not

be detected on both hydrophones simultaneously, eliminating the possibility of pseudo-

replication. The two deployment locations in Mossel Bay were based on previous deployment

locations in 2021 and redeployed at locations 1 and 3 (M1_Mossel Bay Harbour and M3_Mossel

Bay Diaz), chosen as one high-use mooring location and one low-use mooring location,

respectively (Chapter 4 methods) to investigate detection differences within the models. One

mooring was deployed at Buffelsbaai (M7), a known hotspot for S. plumbea based on a visual

survey with no previous acoustic behaviour data available (Conry, 2017), and two were deployed

in Plettenberg Bay (M8, M9), in known hotspots from previous visual and acoustic surveys (Hlati,

2018; G Penry, Pers. Comm.). Mooring ID codes from Chapter 4 were used to ensure continuity

within the data: M1 – Mossel Bay 1, M3 – Mossel Bay 2, M7 – Buffelsbaai, M8 – Plettenberg Bay 1,

M9 – Plettenberg Bay 2 (Figure 5.1).




                                                                                              195
                                                                https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Figure 5.1. Mooring locations across the three bays to acoustically detect Sousa plumbea. Hydrophones were deployed along a 120 km central portion
of the south coast of South Africa (Mossel Bay, Buffelsbaai and Plettenberg Bay). Five mooring locations were selected across three bays. Mossel Bay
was located furthest west, and Plettenberg Bay was furthest east. All moorings were deployed within 15 m water depth (orange range indicating 15
m water depth and brown indicating 20 m depth).




                                                                                                                                                196
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Hydrophones were all set to a continuous recording cycle with a sample rate of 96 kHz (High

Gain), using three models of SoundTrap hydrophones (ST300, ST400, ST500 SoundTraps; Ocean

Instruments Ltd., Auckland, New Zealand). Due to the recording settings, deployment time for the

ST400 was limited by battery length to ~ 23 days; therefore, these devices were placed in Mossel

Bay and Plettenberg Bay, where there were two moorings, alongside ST500 units that had a

longer battery time of 45 days. Hydrophones were deployed in 8 - 10 m water depth with sub-

surface buoys secured to ~ 40 kg weights, secured 3 m from the seafloor. In this deployment, pop-

up acoustic releases (Vemco, Innovasea Systems Inc.) were used for ease of retrieval (as used in

the ATAP array, Cowley et al., 2017). Attempts were made to avoid loud reef systems to mitigate

masking (e.g., snapping shrimp noise, environmental noise), but still within the detection range

of the highly coastal S. plumbea habitat.




Data processing


To identify S. plumbea from the long-term acoustic recordings, whistle encounters were identified

using the whistle extraction and species identification method described in Chapter 4. The

process used both PAMGuard’s whistle and moan detector (WMD) and a custom species classifier

to identify single species S. plumbea encounters (Gillespie et al., 2009). Encounters with more

than 80% confidence for S. plumbea were selected and confirmed. Each encounter was visually

scanned, and all S. plumbea whistles were manually annotated and extracted for each mooring.




Signature whistle identification


A whistle was defined as a frequency-modulated tonal signal with a unit of one continuous

contour or two or more repeated contours (contour – continuous tonal signal), separated by a

period of silence between 0.03 seconds and 0.25 seconds in duration (Kriesell et al., 2014). Only

high-quality whistles (SNR 2 or 3) were used in further analyses. Whistles were compared to the


                                                                                             197
                                          https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Sousa SWT catalogue from Gridley et al., (Appendix 1) to identify likely signature whistles.

Stereotyped whistles that exhibited highly similar frequency modulation patterns occurring in

bouts but did not match a signature whistle type in the catalogue were added if they met SIGID

(SIGnature IDentification) criteria (Janik et al., 2013). The SIGID method consists of two

requirements based on the well-studied bout-production nature of signature whistles (Janik et

al., 2013; Sayigh et al., 2007). Both criteria must be met to identify signature whistle types in free-

ranging populations confidently. The first criteria requires the identification of four repetitions

of highly stereotyped whistle contours, and the second requires that on at least one occasion,

three out of four stereotyped whistles occur within 1-10 s of the previous stereotyped signal

(Janik et al., 2013). New signature whistles were only included and added to the Sousa SWT

catalogue if they met SIGID in this study’s acoustic data. Signature whistle types that were

previously catalogued only required one signal to be counted as a capture in this study. As new

SWTs were catalogued, successive and previous whistles were checked to include new SWTs

throughout the whole dataset. All whistles classed as a “capture” were recorded, with the

associated date, time and location, for the construction of capture histories.




Data analysis: Occurrence


Occurrence was investigated through spatial and temporal recaptures of SWT over the whole

survey period. Discovery curves were used to investigate SWT detections at each mooring, as well

as the cumulative detections for the entire array (i.e., all five receivers).




Data analysis: Capture-Recapture for Abundance


To investigate the SWT capture-recapture data in a similar framework to what might be done for

a photo ID study (e.g. James et al., 2015), which typically concentrates research effort into a single

bay over an extended period, data from each bay was analysed separately, using closed capture-


                                                                                                   198
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




recapture models. Days were initially used as capture occasions, but overall recapture rates were

low, so data were combined to make each recapture occasion one calendar week. Bays with

multiple moorings were combined into a single capture history; thus, mooring 1 and mooring 3

in Mossel Bay were combined, and mooring 8 and mooring 9 in Plettenberg Bay with detection

on either or both hydrophones within an occasion being considered as a capture.




Closed population capture-recapture models were run for each of the three study areas

separately, and then for all areas combined as one population capture to provide estimates of the

number of individuals using each study area as well as for the whole study range. The mark-

recapture analyses were conducted in the programme MARK (White & Burnham, 1999) using the

RMark interface (Laake, 2013). For closed models (within the three bays independently, and for

the whole study area, ‘çombined’) mixture models developed by (Huggins, 1989) were used,

which are recommended because of their flexibility and consider heterogeneity within the data

to account for variation in capture probability ((Elwen et al., 2019; Manly et al., 2010). In the

absence of covariates, Huggins and full-likelihood models provide equivalent results (Lukacs,

2012). As there is no biological reason to expect a behavioural change between initial captures

and recaptures (as in the case of physically trapped animals), c (recapture probability) was set

equal to p (initial-capture probability) throughout (Nicholson et al., 2012; Parra et al., 2018).

Similarly, capture probability was assumed to vary between occasions because environmental

conditions were not constant, and only a portion of the population was captured on any occasion.

Thus, models with constant capture probability were not considered, with the exception of

running a null model for comparative purposes. Although animals almost certainly moved out of

the study area during the study, violating some of the model assumptions, the results of the

models can be interpreted as ‘the number of animals using the study area over the study period’,

which is a useful metric for management.




                                                                                             199
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Population abundance estimates generated by the closed population mixture model for the whole

study area as one population was compared to an open population model which takes into

consideration the entering and exiting of individuals into and out of the population (immigration,

emigration, births, deaths). This was done using the POPAN formulation of the Jolly-Seber

(hereafter PJS) mark-recapture approach using the package “marked” (Laake, 2013), calculating

population estimates using the RMark interface (Laake, 2013). PJS models estimate total

                 ̂) using four parameters: survival probability ("phi" (φ)), detection/capture
population size (𝑁

probability (“p”), the total number of individuals able to enter the population (“N”,

superpopulation), and the probability of entry into the population (“pent”). A subset of candidate

PJS models was run and compared, subsequently selecting the model that minimises the Akaike

Information Criterion (AIC) and the negative log-likelihood (neg2lnl). Within this subset,

parameters Phi, p and pent were set as constant, assuming a constant survival rate, capture

probability, and probability of entry. The parameter N was set as constant, assuming the

superpopulation does not change over time during this short study period. Upon model selection,

̂ was derived from the estimated parameters within the selected model using RMark.
𝑁




Data analysis: Spatially Explicit Capture-Recapture for Density


Density throughout the south coast range was investigated using spatially explicit mark recapture

using models fitted using the R package: ‘secr v4.5.10’ (Efford, 2016). Preliminary models were

fit to evaluate mask size and data efficacy. Given the duration of the study period and the aim of

estimating density across the home range, captures were grouped into a single occasion,

modelling constant density. All models were fit using a hazard exponential detection function

(HEX), with the three default null model parameters: density, lambda and sigma. Only a null

model was fit, and no further parameterisation was investigated due to inherent limitations in

the trap design. The purpose of this analysis was to determine if SWT detections from moored

acoustic receivers were viable identifying markers for SECR density and sigma estimates.


                                                                                              200
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Logistical limitations prevented a more viable trap design. However, using the null model, three

different model types were investigated 1) capture history truncations, 2) mask objects, and 3)

trap configurations.




   1. Capture history truncation


Initial model investigation resulted in NA values for all parameters in the null model. This issue

was determined to be caused by one individual (SWT13) which was detected 482 times over the

study period, a significantly higher recapture rate than all other individuals. By truncating the

data to limit the number of recaptures to 200, the model was able to converge. Consequently, the

data was further truncated to 100 recaptures as well, for further investigation into this effect on

density and sigma estimates.




   2. Masks


To generate density, SECR models require a mask, or geographic area, in which to estimate the

model parameters. These can either be automatically generated using the trap locations or can

be manually limited based on various parameters (e.g., spacing, buffer), and constrained using

.shp files. SECR models are able to estimate home ranges and density outside of the mask

constraint based on the size of the buffer. The mask area represents the area in which the

detectors are operating and usually does not extend to an entire population. For investigation

into survey design and effectiveness of model inputs, three different masks were tested. These

included a 1) basic mask (Mask.Def) with no unsuitable habitat excluded (i.e., land), 2) a low

resolution mask (Mask.LR) and 3) high resolution mask (Mask.HR), covering their coastal habitat

up to 30 m water depth (Figure 5.2). Mask.LR and Mask.HR included coastal range 100km both

west and east from M1 and M9, based on S. plumbea known recaptures to these distance




                                                                                               201
                                      https://scholar.sun.ac.za




(Bouveroux et al., 2019; Vermeulen et al., 2017). A buffer of 10 000 m was determined, after

performing mask diagnostics, functions: suggest.buffer() and mask.check() in the R:secr package.




   3. Trap Configurations


Given the study design, with two locations containing multiple moorings (Mossel Bay and

Plettenberg Bay), two possibilities to improve the model were investigated:


   1. CaptHist.Rem – Removed the two least active receivers from the dataset (M3 and M8)

   2. CaptHist.Comb – Combined the captures from the two receivers within the same bay

       (M1+M3, M8+M9).

                                     https://scholar.sun.ac.za

Figure 5.2. A) Locations of the three bay locations for acosutic detection of Sousa plumbea along the
south coast, South Africa. Mossel Bay and Plettenberg Bay (furthest west and east) contain two moorings

                                                                                           203
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




With these three tests (1. capture history truncations, 2. mask objects, and 3. trap configurations),

various combinations thereof were fitted to a null SECR model. The null model contained three

parameters: Density, Lambda, and Sigma. Density (D) is an estimate of the number of individuals

over the effective sampling area. Lambda is an estimate of the proportion of individuals that are

captured during a study period. Sigma is an estimate of the average distance an individual moves

between capture occasions and over the study period, representative of an individual or

population’s home range. Given the null model, using one capture occasion, density can be

interpreted as a uniform density of all individuals in the study area, and sigma is the average

distance all individuals moved during the study period. To help the model converge, starting

values were provided: D = 0.0001 (starting density to get model to converge), lambda0 = 20

(based on lower boundaries of individuals detected in the study), sigma = 15000 (starting

estimation from literature ~15 km) (M. Efford, Pers. Comm.).




Estimates from all these model combinations were then evaluated by extrapolating abundance

by multiplying the predicted D estimate by the effective study area or total area. These were

compared to the open capture-recapture estimates for the combined survey area.




                                                                                                 204
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




     RESULTS
 Over the full 42-day period, individual hydrophone recording periods ranged from 19 – 42 days

 based on battery usage, memory, and planned retrieval times (Table 5.1) Moorings 3 (MB_Diaz)

 and 8 (Plett_RP) recorded for around 20 days and then ended due to battery failure. Moorings 1

 (MB_Harbour) and 7 (Buffelsbaai) both recorded for around 30 days, whilst mooring 9 (Plett_BI)

 had an extended recording period of 42 days due to delayed retrieval of the unit (Table 5.1).

 Spatial and temporal detection of SWTs was conducted on the full 42-day survey period to

 investigate overall occurrence throughout the study. However, the data was truncated to 32 days

 (five weeks) for further analysis of occurrence (discovery curves) and recapture (abundance

 models). SECR models were able to utilise the full dataset due to the use of a single capture

 occasion (see below).

A total of 4425 whistles were annotated from all recordings in the acoustic array (total recording

duration; 3559.44 hr) of which 2946 were considered SNR 2/3 (Table 5.2). Across all five

moorings, 1282 total signature whistles were detected from 25 different signature whistle types

(SWTs), or individuals. Of these 25 SWTs, 12 matched the catalogue with 13 new whistle types

added from detections on moored data using the SIGID bout analysis method (Figure 5.3).

Occurrence


Over the full 42-day survey period, geographic and temporal variability in SWT detections was

seen across moorings. Across moorings, the majority of total signature whistle detections

occurred on mooring 1 (MB_Harbour = 7 days of SWT detections) and mooring 7 (Buffelsbaai = 5

days of SWT detections), detecting 77% of the total signature whistles (n = 991). Mooring 3 and

mooring 8 had lower detection rates, with mooring 3 only having SWT detections on two days

and M8 having SWT detections on three days (n = 548). However, these hydrophones also had

shorter recording periods.




When looking at the number of captures of SWTs over both time and space, over half the SWTs

were detected on more than one mooring (16 of the 25 SWTs) (Figure 5.4). However, the number

of recaptures varied, with a majority of SWT detections occurring across two moorings only

(52%), with only SWT50 and SWT66 recaptured on four of the five moorings (Figure 5.4). No

SWTs were detected on all five moorings. Over the full six-week survey period, capture occasions

were classed as week periods, with 56% of SWTs detected on one or two capture occasions, 40%

encountered across three different occasions, and 4% of SWTs encountered on four occasions. No

SWTs were detected across all capture occasions (Figure 5.5).

When investigating the cumulative detection of new SWTs over time, the extended recording

duration of mooring 9 in Plettenberg Bay created a bias in the dataset, so the dataset from

Plettenberg Bay was truncated at day 32 (five weeks). Only one encounter occurred over week

six on this single hydrophone and was therefore lost in this truncation. The rate of cumulative

detection of unique signature whistle types varied across all five sites (Figure 5.6a). Low

detection rates at mooring 3 and mooring 8, coupled with their shorter deployment duration,

meant that a limited number of SWTs could be detected at these moorings. No complete

asymptote was reached at any of the five moorings (Figure 5.6a) but when combined across all

five moorings, the rate of new detections reached an asymptote from day 28 of 32 onwards

(Figure 5.6b).

Recapture


Population models were run on the truncated dataset of five capture occasions as only one

hydrophone recorded in week 6 (mooring 9, see above). In Mossel Bay, the best-fit model was

pi(~1)p(~1)c() and in Buffelsbaai and Plettenberg Bay, the best-fit model was

                                                                                        ̂;
‘pi(~1)p(~time)c()’. Closed population models produced the following regional estimates 𝑵

Mossel Bay: 8 (95% CI 8-8); Buffelsbaai: 19 (95% CI 14-53); Plettenberg Bay: 22 (95% CI 19-33)

(Table 5.3). For the combined closed population model, the best-fit model was

pi(~1)p(~time)c(). This combined model produced the best regional estimate of 26 (95% CI 25-

32) (Table 5.3).




Model selection for the open population POPAN Jolly-Seber models showed that model

Phi(~1)p(~time)pent(~1)N(~1) best fitted the data, and assumed a change in capture

probability over time, and a constant probability of survival rate, entry of individuals into the

population and population size as births or deaths were not expected over this period. This model

estimated a superpopulation size of 26 individuals (95% CI 23 – 30) (Table 5.3).

Density


A null model was used to estimate density across the full survey area and duration. Models were

tested with three types of combinations 1) capture truncations, 2) three distinct spatial masks

with varying resolutions and spatial constraints, and 3) three trap configurations to investigate

survey design and the viability of using signature whistle types in an SECR framework. The SECR

model used investigates captures between ‘traps’ but ignores time variation effectively using a

single capture occasion for the entire study period, hence the full deployment period was used.




Truncating the data to reduce detections of SWT13, helped to improve model convergence.

Multiple levels of truncation (Trunc) were investigated (200, 150, 100, 50), but only Trunc200

and Trunc100 are reported, as the effect on the output were the same. At Trunc200, SWT13,

which had 482 detections, was limited to 200 detections; only individual SWT13 was affected. At

Trunc100, three individuals were affected, SWT13, SWT44 and SWT66. Across these truncated

data sets, the number of detections was reduced, but the total number of individuals remained at

25, for the single capture occasion.




The SECR design configurations reported (Table 5.4) include the full capture history and high-

resolution mask (Full.HR), combinations of the two truncation levels with both the high and low

resolution mask (200.HR, 100.HR, 200.LR, 100.LR), and the trap configurations adjusted by

removing the two least active traps (200.HR.Removed) and combining the traps that were placed

in the same bay (200.HR.Combined). The predicted estimates for both density (D) and sigma

throughout these model combinations were very comparable. Sigma (the average distance an

individual moves between capture occasions) was estimated to be between 10 km and 16 km and

likely constrained by detector distribution throughout the array than overall movement of




                                                                                             213
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




animals. Abundance estimates, extrapolated from the SECR models were all close to 25

individuals, for the study area and period, as seen in Table 5.5.




                                                                                214
                                                                https://scholar.sun.ac.za

   DISCUSSION

This study provides a proof of concept on the effectiveness of PAM at detecting individual

dolphins within this small population over a multi-bay deployment. Detections across the survey

period suggested a high capture rate of a portion of the total population within the survey area.

The discovery curve for the combined dataset showed a clear asymptote, indicating that over the

full survey area, nearly all available individuals using the study area over the study period were

detected. This was confirmed in the abundance estimates across both the CR and SECR model

estimates, resulting in values not much higher than the total number of identified SWTs. All

capture-recapture models run on the various sets of data produced similarly low abundance

estimates and density estimations for the survey area.




Abundance estimates in this study were based on a continuous acoustic deployment (3559.44

hrs) with five capture occasions (weeks) included in the final abundance modelling, producing a

combined abundance estimate of 26 in both the closed and open combined abundance models

across the whole survey area (Closed 95% CI 25-32, Open 95% CI 23-30). Comparatively, in

Mossel Bay, James et al. (2015) produced a closed abundance estimate across years of 2011 – 71

ind. (95% CI: 30–167); 2012 –28 ind. (14–55); and 2013 – 43 ind. (19–98) but over a much longer

timeframe of 8 to 12 months of survey effort per year (Table 5.6). Although these estimates are

higher, the number of S. plumbea encounters throughout the three-year survey period in James

et al., (2015) was very low, with 0 - 3 encounters per month across this period. When further

looking at abundance estimates in this study across the three bays separately, the number of

SWTs captured, and abundance estimates produced across the three bays in this study are

comparable to similar local photo ID assessments in Plettenberg Bay over short time periods

(Table 5.6). In this study, in Plettenberg Bay, 18 individuals were acoustically detected, producing

closed abundance estimates with 95% CI of 19 to 33 individuals throughout the area. By

comparison, Conry, (2017) encountered 33 individuals over 39 survey days within a 25-month

                                                                                                217
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




study period in the same area. Jobson, (2006) detected 63 individuals and estimated an

abundance of 112 (95% CI 75 - 133) during a 24-month study in Plettenberg Bay. Additionally,

Greenwood, (2013) identified 41 individuals with a closed population estimate of 42 (95% CI 42-

55) during a 17-month study period. The lower estimates within this study are likely reflective of

the shorter study duration in comparison to these previous studies, the limited movements of

individuals within this study period, and the caveat of signature whistles captures occurring at

lower rates than photographic capture. Direct comparison between studies is difficult due to

different methodologies and study efforts. One of the key differences within this study was the

reduced survey period of weeks in comparison to months and even years in previous studies.

                                                                https://scholar.sun.ac.za

In using signature whistles as markers in capture-recapture models, it is important to consider

that their production can be behaviourally dependent and context-driven, and when used as an

individual marker for detection, they can have inherent biases due to these behavioural effects

(Fearey et al., 2022; Longden et al., 2020; Romeu et al., 2024). One of the assumptions of mark-

recapture is capture (or tag) homogeneity between sampling periods and consistent

identification. Signature whistles show stability over time, highlighting their suitability to be used

in this context (Sayigh et al., 2022), but their production is context-dependent, and dolphins can

be present in an area and not produce these specific calls (Fearey et al., 2022). In other capture-

recapture studies, the use of hair traps or camera traps provides a more homogenous rate of

detection between individuals, although they are still vulnerable to behavioural variations, such

as the amount of ‘hair captured, the angle of animal in the photo, the general quality of the

photograph etc. In relative density studies, the use of call detections in species such as birds

(Dawson & Efford, 2009) and frogs (Stevenson et al., 2015) is simplified as these species have

uniform call rates between individuals, which is shaped by basic fitness and evolutionary drivers.

These factors provide an overall detection probability that is homogenous over a population. In

other dolphin species, group size, composition and behaviour highly influence individual whistle

production (Kriesell et al., 2014; Quick & Janik, 2008). In S. plumbea, these contextual effects have

been demonstrated to have less influence on signature whistle production rates, potentially

producing a more homogenous detection rate amongst individuals (Chapter 3). Although based

on a small available sample size., low overall whistle production rates are unlikely to have affected

detection as only one detection within a week-long sampling occasion is required for an

individual to be classed as ‘captured’. As the Sousa SWT catalogue grows, the strength in detecting

individuals will increase, as seen in other populations with larger, more extensive SWT catalogues

(Longden et al., 2020).




                                                                                                  220
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




In this study, abundance estimates in both closed and open models were similar to but below

abundances from previous studies in the region and highlighted that nearly all individuals were

captured in the region. When looking at the number of recaptures across the survey period, five

capture occasions over a five-week period produced a high number of recaptures across two and

three capture occasions, and very few animals were caught only once. This implies a high number

of available individuals were detected throughout the survey period, as opposed to a higher

number of single captures seen in mark-recapture studies for ‘open’ and very large populations

(Reisinger & Karczmarski, 2010). The use of weekly capture occasions likely contributes to the

higher number of recaptures within this study's models. The decision to use weeks as capture

occasions rather than days was made to ensure a sufficient period of mixing and movement of the

population between sampling occasions based on known residency and resighting rates

(Vermeulen et al., 2018) and serve to reduce the number of zeroes in the data set which can affect

model convergence. Vermeulen et al., (2018) found the shortest time span between two

resighting’s of an individual was one day (between Knysna and Plettenberg Bay), across a 45 km

area. However, for distances > 100 km, the shortest time span between resighting was 24 days.

With the maximum array distance between mooring 1 and mooring 9, ~120 km and the

deployment duration limited to around six weeks, weekly capture occasions were deemed the

most reliable period to ensure sufficient mixing of the population, considering the levels of

extended site fidelity seen in previous studies and ensuring likely detection of all available

dolphins within the deployment period. (Vermeulen et al., 2018). The estimates produced within

this study are likely a reliable estimation of the available individuals within the 120 km area

throughout the survey period, highlighting the limited movement of individuals entering or

exiting the survey area. The limitations of centring the array within a 120 km region of the south

coast will have led to uncaptured individuals as they moved beyond Mossel Bay or Plettenberg

Bay or didn’t enter into this region within the survey period. To increase capture rates, future

efforts should prioritise expanding study sites along the south coast range with greater distances

between moorings as fine-scale coverage of bays did not increase capture rates within this study.


                                                                                              221
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Optimising for a larger geographic scale and weekly capture occasions would enhance the

potential for new captures and encourage increased population mixing.




The final aspect of the SWORD framework is density estimation, conducted through SECR in this

study, which found extremely low densities across the survey area. Across all SECR models tested,

density estimates were very low, and movement estimates (Sigma) were limited by detector

location. Survey design requires consideration of both logistical constraints and the budget

available, as this will affect the scale and scope of the study's ability to detect and estimate

animals, especially within spatially explicit models. The variation in the scale of PAM survey

designs is large, with the US Navy heavily investing in passive acoustic arrays to investigate the

impacts of sonar on marine mammals at both the individual and population levels (Mellinger et

al., 2007). Selecting array dimensions is especially important if investigating call rates or density

estimations based on time of arrival (Marques et al., 2013), with greater distances between

hydrophones increasing the magnitude of the acoustic time delay of multiple detections of the

same sounds (Ryder et al., 2023). The use of SWT in this study removed these aspects from the

survey design, with single hydrophone detection providing individual captures of individuals as

long as individuals called as they moved throughout the survey area. This study was restricted in

scope through the number of hydrophones available, hence the investigation into placing multiple

hydrophones in single bays or a single hydrophone in a bay. The manipulation of trap design,

through the combination or removal of these within-bay traps, did not affect estimates, indicating

that SECR is robust to limited survey efforts or elusive species that are difficult to capture. Using

a single hydrophone in bays provides a greater spatial range to be covered, with reduced effects

from potential variation in production rates in S. plumbea. This would enable a more useful

estimate for Sigma that is less biased from hydrophone location with a greater range of movement

within the model. Yet, regardless of mask size, accuracy, or resolution, density estimates had

estimates of 23-26 individuals – in line with the combined closed and open population models.



                                                                                                 222
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




In producing population trends, the rate of population change, natural variation in population

size and the frequency of surveys across geographic and temporal scales will be species-specific

to the degree of these effects (Thompson et al., 2000). With naturally small populations, S.

plumbea are more vulnerable to genetic problems such as loss of genetic variability and

inbreeding depression (Avery & Hill, 1977). They can also be driven towards extinction by natural

variations in individual reproductive and mortality rates (demographic variation) (Stacey, 1992).

These small populations can be more vulnerable to anthropogenic or natural changes in

environmental conditions. This is likely already occurring in S. plumbea off the KwaZulu-Natal

coastline, which has recorded the highest levels of PCBs and DDT of any delphinid globally (Gui

et al., 2016). Problems from such pollutants include reproductive abnormalities and impaired

testosterone production (Duinker et al., 1979; Subramanian et al., 1987), the full effect of which

has not yet been monitored in this population. Overall, for S. plumbea, throughout their whole

global range, there has been little empirical evidence on population trends, but available studies

have shown a worrying degree of regional decline throughout southern Africa. On the south coast

of Zanzibar, S. plumbea population estimates declined by 63% since 2002 for non-calf individuals,

with an abundance of 19 (95% CI 14-25) in 2015, meeting the threshold for Critically Endangered

on the IUCN Red List criteria (Sharpe & Berggren, 2019). In Plettenberg Bay, South Africa, the

population also declined by around 50%, from 93 (95% CI 72–114) to 41 (95% CI 28–54)

individuals between 2002-03 and 2012-13 (Greenwood, 2013). In comparison, these studies'

estimate of 22 (95% CI 19-25) individuals in Plettenberg Bay is lower but, with a combined closed

estimation of 26 (CI 95% 25-31) for the survey area, indicates that Plettenberg Bay forms are a

large proportion of the studies detected individuals. Expanding the geographic ranges of the

survey area further east in future deployments would provide a more robust estimate for the

whole south coast that could produce more comparable trends with a higher degree of

repeatability to monitor more useful trends to population size.



                                                                                              223
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




This study used signature whistles as individually unique acoustic markers in a capture-recapture

framework to provide abundance estimates for S. plumbea across both individual bays and a

combined range along the south coast of South Africa. This method provided low estimations

across models, similar to that of detected SWTs, indicating that the majority of acoustically

available individuals in the area were detected throughout the study period. Further scope for

improving these estimates in future deployments includes expanding the geographic range of the

acoustic array, rather than increasing the density of recorders in the array, to optimise captures

of unique SWTs throughout the south coast.

                                          https://scholar.sun.ac.za
