    6 CONCLUSION


The field of acoustic ecology is emerging globally and has undergone an exponential increase

within the African continent in recent years (Becker et al., 2022). This is in part due to the

development of more accessible and cost-effective equipment, along with the recent development

of the African Bioacoustics Community fostering capacity-building and training opportunities

(Becker et al., 2022; Lamont et al., 2022). Passive acoustic monitoring (PAM) has proven to be an

effective method for coastal studies and applied acoustic ecology, with great potential for

generating much-needed knowledge of low-density and threatened species (Rogers et al., 2013).




In 2015, the Indian Ocean humpback dolphin, Sousa plumbea, was listed as “Endangered” by both

the International Union for the Conservation of Nature (IUCN; Braulik et al., 2015) and the Red

List of Mammals of South Africa (S Plön et al., 2016), making them the first and only officially

endangered marine mammal resident in South Africa. The preference for shallow habitats by S.

plumbea coincides with the region’s most heavily utilised waters, affected by coastal fishers, the

shipping industry, coastal construction, and agricultural run-off (Braulik et al., 2015). These

anthropogenic pressures on the coastal regions of South Africa overlap with S. plumbea’s range,

however, single, quantifiable threats are difficult to identify, as highlighted by Plön et al., (2021).

Given the small number of individuals remaining in the population and the widespread threats

they face, individual-based monitoring is particularly important. The understanding of home

ranges for South African populations of S. plumbea is currently limited by the ability to adequately

study them using traditional boat-based surveys and photographic capture-recapture techniques.

Given the threats to the remaining population, this thesis explores how individual passive

acoustic monitoring using signature whistles could be used as a cost-effective and non-invasive

method to detect S. plumbea throughout their range which could best inform managers and

policymakers.

                                                                                                   232
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Chapter 2 presents the first description of whistle contour features of S. plumbea in South Africa,

with an analysis of geographic variation between local regions within South Africa and across

ocean basins (comparisons made with a population of S. plumbea in Maharashtra, India). The

study showed a degree of increasing level of acoustic variation with increasing geographic

isolation throughout the study range with the least variation demonstrated amongst the four

south coast sites in South Africa, which is likely attributed to the regular mixing of individuals

throughout this range. However general whistle features overlap. which supports the use of

whistle characteristics for acoustic monitoring, in particular species classification.




One of the novel results from this chapter was the presence of ultrasonic whistle contours

throughout S. plumbea’s South African range. The ability to produce ultrasonic whistle contours

(> 20 kHz) has been demonstrated across the genus, but the presence of whistles over 40 kHz

throughout South Africa provides the first evidence of these high-frequency whistles consistently

throughout a region. The production of high-frequency whistles is a morphological adaptation of

the sound-generating structure present in Sousa spp. (Frainer et al., 2019), which can also be seen

across several other high-frequency whistling delphinids across separate evolutionary lineages

(Lammers & Au, 2003; Rasmussen et al., 2006). Morphologically, Sousa plumbea has a specialised

structure next to the melon's small left posterior branch, which is associated with higher

frequencies and directionality of sound (Frainer et al., 2019). This structure is present in several

other species that also feature high-frequency characteristics, including Spinner dolphins Stenella

longirostris, Risso’s dolphins Grampus griseus, white-beaked dolphins Lagenorhynchus albirostris,

and Franciscana dolphins Pontoporia blainvillei (Frainer et al., 2019). Equipment limitations and

limited encounter durations in previous studies may mean these upper bandwidth capabilities

are likely widespread within this genus but were either not recorded (Schultz & Corkeron, 1994;

Van Parijs & Corkeron, 2001) or investigated across other studies (Sims et al., 2012; Zbinden et


                                                                                                233
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




al., 1977). These features of the whistle repertoire within S. plumbea are important markers that

can be used for species monitoring, for example within the development of regional species

classifiers. Regionally specific species classifiers were developed for this thesis to extract

signature whistles from long-term moored acoustic data with high overlap in whistle features

seen throughout the South Africa study sites. These classifiers were employed to extract S.

plumbea whistles from long-term moored acoustic data and are now available for use by other

researchers and organizations to identify S. plumbea from passive acoustic recordings.




Comparison between the south and east coast sites indicated that Richards Bay was more distinct

than the south coast sites with little misclassification of Richards Bay whistle contours to south

coast study sites. This evidence supports the two-population hypothesis, suggesting greater

acoustic variation between the south and east coast populations than within the south coast

population due to increased geographical isolation. Lampert et al., (2021) used mtDNA to show

that these two populations were historically a single population with further range contraction

and habitat fragmentation causing these populations to become geographically distinct, with little

mixing today (Caputo et al., 2017; Vermeulen et al., 2017). Acoustic variation between the south

and east coast populations may be explained by the acoustic adaptation hypothesis, where signals

are adapted to environmental factors such as water depth, temperature, and salinity to maximize

transmission and minimize degradation (Ey & Fischer, 2009). Richards Bay is an industrialised

harbour area with distinct habitat differences to the south coast sites. In addition to habitat

differences, boat activity has been shown to cause dolphins to modify their vocalizations and

acoustic behaviour (May-Collado & Quiñones-Lebrón, 2014; Perez-Ortega et al., 2021). Boat

activity has been observed to cause both an upward shift in whistle frequencies (Guerra et al.,

2014; Heiler et al., 2016) and lower frequencies with fewer frequency modulations in areas of

high ambient noise (Morisaka et al., 2005). This follows the acoustic niche hypothesis, which

states that cetaceans shift their communication sounds to be below or above ambient noise and



                                                                                              234
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




was developed from studies of the gray whale (Eschrichtius robustus, Dahlheim & Fisher, 1983)

and the bottlenose dolphin (Tursiops spp., Gao et al., 1995). The high use of the harbour

environment and habitat characteristics, such as high ambient noise, likely contributed to the

distinctiveness between the east and south coast populations. The use of S. plumbea classifiers

(Chapter 4), with the future inclusion of clicks (Yang et al., 2020), could then utilise long-term

acoustic arrays throughout the east coast region to further explore S. plumbea range movements.

Richards Bay is a study site 220 km from the southern Mozambique border. Presently, there is no

knowledge of individuals moving between these two countries. With S. plumbea listed as locally

Endangered on the IUCN Red List of mammals for South Africa, there is little additional protection

in Mozambique (Plön et al., 2016). Bycatch has been highlighted as a key threat to S. plumbea

populations further along their Indian Oceans range (Amir et al., 2002; Stensland et al., 2006),

and Mozambique is a country where the threat of bycatch is high (Kiszka et al., 2009). The next

available study on the species in Mozambique is in Maputo Bay, 100 km north of the southern

border, where transient individuals were high throughout the area (Guissamulo & Cockcroft,

2005). Boat-based surveys demonstrating connectivity between these areas have not been

previously attempted, but acoustic deployments covering this stretch of coastline throughout

iSimangaliso wetland park, south of the Mozambique border and into Ponto do Ouro reserve, in

southern Mozambique could provide the data required to understand individual movement

across this border using PAM. Therefore, providing evidence to extend conservation priorities

into Mozambique, if movement of these individuals were shown to occur throughout this region.




Chapter 3 extends these findings by examining individually distinct whistles, known as signature

whistles (SWs), and explores individual acoustic behaviour and how variables such as group

composition and behavioural states might affect whistle production rates. In recent years, using

individually distinct calls for acoustic monitoring has been proposed as an alternative to tagging

or photographic methods for capture-recapture studies (Longden et al., 2020; Terry et al., 2005).



                                                                                              235
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




However, using natural vocalizations as detection markers comes with biases, such as varying

detection probabilities across species, groups, and individuals. Understanding what influences

acoustic production is key when designing the timing and locations of long-term passive acoustic

arrays to detect animals using these passive acoustic methods.




The results from this chapter demonstrated a relatively low signature whistle production rate in

free-swimming S. plumbea, supporting vocally elusive behaviour. It was hypothesized that clear

effects on signature whistle production would be observed from group size, calf presence, or

group behaviour, as suggested by existing literature from other, more widely studied species

(Cook et al., 2004; dos Santos et al., 2005). However, across the 21 encounters included in the

study (with the removal of one outlier group), behaviour and group composition do not strongly

affect whistle production rates, only temporal production. Passive Acoustic Monitoring (PAM)

can theoretically be conducted continuously, but practical constraints such as battery life and

storage capacity currently limit the duration of deployments (Riera et al., 2013; Thomisch et al.,

2015). Duty cycling, which alternates between periods of active recording and dormancy, can

extend deployment time, by conserving both battery and storage, yet will miss detections when

dormant. When selecting an optimal recording period for acoustic recorders, it is important to

consider the vocal behaviour of the species, the production rates of the species, and the study's

overall research questions (Riera et al., 2013). Therefore, with little effect from selecting mooring

locations around specific activity centres, it is more important to consider appropriate recording

schedules to optimise signature whistle detection. With short acoustic encounters and low

production rates, duty cycling needs to consider this, especially when small group sizes further

influence limited detection opportunities. In S. plumbea continuous recording periods were

deemed most effective for optimising detection and therefore used throughout the thesis during

long-term acoustic moorings. Where possible, continuous recording schedules are




                                                                                                 236
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




recommended, or if required, very short periods of dormancy within a duty cycle when S. plumbea

is the focal species.




Across all the covariates tested, the presence of calves in a group increased the temporal effect of

whistle production and although there was a small relationship between SWT (individuals) and

SW counts during encounters without calves present, there was no significant effect on the

number of signature whistles produced. Mothers and calves have been shown to increase the call

rate of signature whistles to initiate reunions with one another, especially when out of visual

range (King et al., 2016). The low whistle rates in mother-calf groups in this study indicate that

either mother-calf pairs were not moving apart from one another during encounters, (this was

later confirmed through boat-based photo ID S. Dines pers. obs.), or that mother-calf pairs do not

utilise vocal communication in the same way as other species do. In other species, other forms of

contact are often recorded when vocal communication is less preferred, such as in nursing baleen

whales, who use visual or physical contact with calves, to avoid predator detection (Nielsen et al.,

2019; Parks et al., 2019; Videsen et al., 2017). Across S. plumbea’s coastal range, the possibility of

inter- or intraspecific detection is high, which could be dangerous for the calf (Koper & Plön,

2016). In China, S. chinensis group sizes reach 48 individuals, and nursery groups (with mother-

calf pairs present) are 2-4 times larger than non-nursery groups, providing much stronger

protection for calves (Liu et al., 2021). The consistently smaller group sizes in S. plumbea in South

Africa mean they do not have a ‘safety in numbers’ effect (Lehtonen & Jaatinen, 2016), and the

addition of vulnerable calves to a group will increase intraspecific and interspecific predator

threats (Frainer et al., 2023; Koper & Plön, 2016). It is known that within cetaceans mother-calf

pairs are generally in very close proximity (Gubbins et al., 1999) and within these smaller groups,

reliance on other methods to maintain mother-calf contact, such as visual or physical contact,

might be preferred. In future studies, calf presence on vocal production is certainly one aspect of

vocal behaviour that warrants further inspection.



                                                                                                  237
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Behavioural states have been demonstrated to have some of the strongest effects on whistle

production, with increased arousal linked to increasing levels of production in both wild and

captive populations (Cook et al., 2004; Luís et al., 2016; Probert et al., 2021). However, in this

study, no greater effects on whistle production were seen during socialising contexts than when

travelling or milling behaviours were observed. Signature whistles function as a contact call and

a stronger effect during social events would have been expected (Janik, 2009). Socialising

behaviours such as tail slaps, events that are associated with high levels of arousal, were not

observed during encounters (Luís et al., 2021). Longden et al., (2020) found clear, behaviourally

linked capture heterogeneity for signature whistle detections of T. truncatus. A key element for

optimal acoustic detection in other species is prior knowledge of habitat use and behaviours

within the study area, to maximise capture probability (dos Santos et al., 2005; Fearey et al.,

2022). Passive acoustic monitoring for S. plumbea is novel throughout the south coast with little

information available on acoustic behaviour from moored hydrophones throughout this region.

The lack of strong behavioural or group composition effects on signature whistle production in S.

plumbea is an important result for optimising passive acoustic detection in this species,

suggesting that group composition and behaviour are not critical variables for optimizing

signature whistle detection in S. plumbea.




Chapter 4 investigated individual detection through both traditional boat-based surveys with

moored acoustic deployments during a short-term assessment in Mossel Bay. The reason for

focusing the chapter on a short-term rapid assessment was the need to investigate the

effectiveness of alternative methods for individual detection that could be invaluable in these

threatened species (Braulik et al., 2018). The rapid identification of key regions to prioritize for

continued monitoring studies has been highlighted as a requirement for greater conservation

action (Braulik et al., 2018). Effective conservation management plans consider logistical


                                                                                                238
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




constraints and species-specific research to produce baseline empirical data that informs

effective monitoring studies (Downey et al., 2022). For acoustic monitoring to be suggested as an

effective method, spatial and temporal differences in individual detection were investigated using

concurrent PAM and boat-based surveys, including an evaluation of the logistical and financial

constraints and considerations of both methods within the context of a short-term deployment

period.




The strength of the acoustic array was in the broad spatial scale it covered and its continuous

recording capabilities. Static passive acoustic moorings detected animals across all six sites, but

detection levels varied, with mooring three only detecting low signal-to-noise ratio calls (and

therefore excluded from analysis), and moorings one and six showed the greatest diversity in

individual detection (> 5 SWTs). Boat-based surveys were conducted throughout the bay with

photo ID encounters concentrated in two locations within this array area. The high encounter

rate at these locations would have indicated that these were high-use areas for these dolphins

from the boat-based assessments. The bias of these surveys to good weather conditions and a

short survey period, usually in the middle of the day, limits any conclusions on habitat use that

can be made from boat-based encounters alone. One of the inherent limitations of boat-based

surveys is they are constrained by good weather and daylight (Baird et al., 2005). In addition,

boat-based encounters are behaviour-dependent and in the presence of boats, dolphins are less

likely to stay socialising and more likely to shift to travelling (Kassamali-Fox et al., 2020). Through

passive acoustic detections, it was apparent that S. plumbea moved throughout the whole survey

area, with higher levels of vocal activity during the night. The broader spatial component of

acoustic surveys provides a stronger level of data on species occurrence, including nocturnal

activity, as demonstrated in this chapter. Mossel Bay is one of the harbours that are planned for

development over the next 25 years (Bloom, 2019). As part of Operation Phakisa, the South

African government plans to develop the country's blue economy, and coastal development,



                                                                                                   239
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




namely harbour expansions, will be implemented over the next 25 years (Guerreiro, 2022). If

these developments are to go ahead, the inclusion of S. plumbea activity levels and fine-scale

habitat use throughout this area in environmental impact assessments (EIAs) could dissipate the

impact on the population. The inclusion of recommendations, such as suggesting construction

outside of high activity periods or avoiding high-use areas altogether, could be provided through

these PAM datasets. This has been done effectively in Walvis Bay, Namibia, to investigate the

spatial use of T. truncatus around planned port developments and led to the inclusion of a dolphin

priority area in the marine spatial planning process prior to coastal development (Finke et al.,

2020).




Overall, when comparing the two methods over a short period, both had low detection rates yet

successfully recaptured individuals during the study period. When evaluating ‘cost per dolphin’

(CPD), while the costs associated with fieldwork were lower for acoustic methods, photo ID

proved to be a more cost-effective approach overall. This conclusion was largely due to the

significant amount of time required for data processing and analysis in acoustic methods, which

offset their initial cost advantages. When considering these methods, especially in the context of

long-term monitoring for managers, the inclusion of desk-based time is an important factor.

Time-based measures for analytical processes are subject to change in the future as machine

learning methods are rapidly improving processing capabilities (Frainer et al., 2023; Jensen et al.,

2024; Jiang et al., 2019). Moreover, the broader application of acoustic studies to include

soundscape analysis and other species detection widens the applicability of these surveys.

Furthermore, as data storage continues to become cheaper, these hurdles will continue to

optimize passive acoustic monitoring as an option for a wider number of organisations. Photo ID

serves as a valuable and accessible method, ultimately proving to be more cost-effective for this

short-term study. However, in a targeted short-term assessment, continuous acoustic detection

methods proved to be a reliable alternative to photo ID.



                                                                                                240
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Chapter 5 builds on the work from previous chapters and covers a 6-week static acoustic

deployment across three study locations throughout the central portion of the south coast of

South Africa, covering ~ 120 km of the south coast range. The chapter aimed to provide a proof-

of-concept using the SWORD (signature whistles for occurrence, recapture and density)

framework (Fearey et al., 2021) to present an analysis of PAM's effectiveness, using both capture-

recapture and SECR models in Sousa plumbea.




Capture-recapture and SECR models provided reliable estimates for the survey area and

highlighted the limited movement of individuals throughout the survey period. The three sites

used in this study were known as high-use areas based on previous photo ID studies and were

successful as proof-of-concept deployments. The deployment region for this chapter was selected

as central within the south coast population range, incorporating sites where individuals were

known to move between and previous regional estimations had been made (Conry, 2017; James

et al., 2015; Vermeulen et al., 2017). Buffelsbaai, a newly established site for acoustic deployment,

accounted for 77% of all SW detections and was monitored using a single hydrophone. This

demonstrates that future moorings can effectively utilize bays with little to no prior behavioural

or acoustic data, offering valuable movement insights in previously unexplored areas along the

greater south coast. In addition, the study found nearly all the available individuals in the region

were acoustically detected, as shown through the low population estimates, high number of

recaptures, and asymptote seen in the combined moorings discovery curve. Over this period, the

movement of individuals beyond the acoustic array was limited. Through trap configuration

investigation, the chapter showed that the increased density of units within a bay did not increase

estimates, and therefore future priority should be given to expanding the linear range of the array,

utilising single moorings in locations and increasing the spacing between units to increase the

number of unique SWTs captured across the south coast.


                                                                                                 241
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




In using signature whistles as markers in CR models, it is important to consider that their

production can be behaviourally dependent and context-driven, and they can thus have inherent

biases (Fearey et al., 2022; Longden et al., 2020; Romeu et al., 2024). In S. plumbea, behavioural

and contextual effects have been demonstrated to have less influence on signature whistle

production rates, potentially producing a more homogenous detection rate amongst individuals

(Chapter 3). One of the assumptions of capture-recapture is capture (or tag) homogeneity

between sampling periods and consistent identification. Unlike other CR studies, where

seasonality affects population estimates (Elwen et al., 2019), no consistent seasonality patterns

were seen over a three-year photo ID survey conducted in Mossel Bay (2011-2013, James et al.,

2015). Therefore, removing the possible effect of seasonality on passive acoustic monitoring

studies in this species. Signature whistles show stability over time, highlighting their suitability

to be used in this context (Sayigh et al., 2022). Low overall whistle production rates by individuals

were unlikely to have affected detection rates as only one detection within the weeklong sampling

occasion was required to be classed as present. As the Sousa SWT catalogue grows, the strength

in detecting individuals will increase, as has been seen in other populations with larger, more

extensive SWT catalogues (Longden et al., 2020).




In conclusion, the work presented in this thesis provides information on whistle features,

geographic variability, acoustic behaviour, and production rates of signature whistles in S.

plumbea in South Africa. The thesis increases our understanding of how S. plumbea acoustically

communicate along their South African coastal range, leading to the first population and density

estimates for S. plumbea along a portion of their south coast range based on individual acoustic

detection. This work has highlighted challenges and logistical considerations when monitoring

an acoustically elusive species, with recommendations moving forward. The nature of PAM

provides large datasets, and previously, species identification has been a limiting factor in the


                                                                                                 242
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




application of these datasets, as well as data storage. Over the course of this thesis, custom-

trained classifiers were used to identify S plumbea from moored recordings, a first for the south

coast region and available for future studies. The development of machine learning will further

advance these analytical processes, with Frainer et al., (2023) recently developing a four-species

classifier based on convolutional neural networks that provide additional applications of these

datasets. The thesis also shows that PAM for S. plumbea requires a continuous recording schedule

for optimal detection that considers their vocally elusive nature and intermittent encounter

durations. Final recommendations show that expanding the south coast array to cover S.

plumbea’s full range, will provide a more reliable population estimate for S. plumbea, due to their

limited movement during the survey period.




The conclusions presented here using individual acoustic detection through signature whistles

provide a baseline dataset with recommendations for future studies. With the considerations and

recommendations implemented, passive acoustic monitoring can provide a tool that can support

managers and policymakers in developing effective conservation efforts for this threatened

species with scope to strengthen these results in the future.

       ABSTRACT

When species are on the brink of extinction, individual rather than population, based monitoring

is both feasible and necessary, proving crucial information for conservation management. The

Indian Ocean humpback dolphin (Sousa plumbea) is South Africa’s most endangered marine

mammal. Due to their elusive nature, data on population demographics are minimal. Here we

investigate whether capture-recapture of individuals is possible over time and space, using

naturally occurring signature whistle types and photographic identification data collected during

focal group follows. Contours were visually grouped into whistle types of similar frequency

modulation pattern. The temporal production of whistles was similar to that of bottlenose

dolphins (Tursiops truncatus) and likely signature whistles were identified through a bout

analysis approach applied to whistle types. Overall, 410 whistles were grouped into 21 whistle

types, resulting in 14 likely signature whistles following bout analysis. Acoustic and photographic

recaptures were identified through catalogue matching. We provide evidence of acoustic

recapture of signature whistle types over distances spanning 480 km and eight years. By

presenting strong evidence for signature whistle use and in demonstrating the feasibility of

capture-recapture using individually distinctive acoustic signatures, we provide the foundations

for an alternative, non-invasive method for individual based monitoring of this endangered

species.




KEY WORDS


humpback dolphin, signature whistle, mark recapture, AIID, identity




                                                                                               250
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




        INTRODUCTION

Traditionally, wildlife conservation and management action has been based on patterns observed

at the population and species level. However, for small populations of endangered species, every

individual counts and individual-based monitoring becomes necessary. Understanding individual

differences can improve and enable adaptive management of small populations and is possible

through individually unique identifiers such as man-made tags or naturally occurring scars and

marks. In Capture-Recapture (CR) individuals are identified by these marks and re-captured over

time and space (Hammond, 1986) generating a capture history which enables calculation of

individual ranging patterns (e.g. Vermeulen et al., 2018) and survival rates (Koivuniemi et al.,

2019), as well as social associations (Lusseau et al., 2006). Population abundance and density can

be estimated by applying various modelling approaches to capture histories over time and within

a spatial framework (spatial capture recapture, SCR, Efford et al., 2009). A fundamental

assumption of capture-recapture is that marks must be individually distinctive and stable to

persist over time (Hammond, 1986). Historically, CR relied on artificial marks or physically

marking animals, e.g. though ear clipping, freeze branding or paint marking or attaching tags.

(Hammond, 1986). However, financial, logistical and ethical considerations have promoted

natural non-invasive markers. For example, pigmentation patterns (Koivuniemi et al., 2019),

nicks or scars are recaptured through photographs and hair snares enable recapture through

genetic identification.




Recently passive acoustic methods have been proposed for use in CR of individuals (Marques et

al., 2013) through individual acoustic identification (AIID, Terry et al., 2005; Klenova et al., 2009;

Janik et al., 2013). AIID can be achieved by recognition of subtle but consistent differences in the

time or spectral parameters of the same call types which is common across taxa, mammals(e.g.,

Charrier et al. 2003), birds (e.g. Mathevon, 1997), reptiles (e.g. Mathevon et al., 2013), and may

arise from inter-individual differences in sound production morphology (i.e. by-product

                                                                                                  251
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




distinctiveness, Boughman & Moss, 2003). Alternatively, the communication system of some

species is based on call types with high levels of inter-individual distinctiveness (Boughman &

Moss, 2003), enabling AIID though individually distinct acoustic labels (Longden et al., 2020).




Some dolphin species produce whistles which encode identity information in their distinct

frequency modulation pattern (reviewed by Janik & Sayigh, 2013). These whistle types, termed

signature whistles (Caldwell & Caldwell 1968; Caldwell et al., 1990) are the strongest example of

a designed individual signature in the animal kingdom (Janik et al., 2006; Sayigh et al., 2007).

Signature whistles are structurally stable over time (Sayigh et al., 1990; Sayigh et al. 2022),

commonly exchanged to communicate between individuals (Quick and Janik 2012; King and Janik

2013) and used to maintain group cohesion (Janik and Slater, 1998). Although best studied in

common bottlenose dolphins (Tursiops truncatus) (reviewed in Janik & Sayigh, 2013), several

other dolphin species including the Indian Ocean bottlenose dolphin (Tursiops aduncus, Gridley

et al., 2014; King et al., 2018) and the common dolphin (Delphinus delphis, Caldwell & Caldwell,

1968; Fearey et al., 2019a) likely also use signature whistles to communicate identity

information.




Due to their long term stability (up to 43 years, Sayigh et al., 2022) and high inter-individual

distinctiveness, dolphin signature whistles have been identified as candidates for long term

individual acoustic monitoring (Terry et al., 2005; Janik et al., 2013). Kriesell et al., (2014)

demonstrated recaptures of wild bottlenose dolphin (T. truncatus) from coastal Namibia over

time and space through acoustic monitoring of signature whistles. They applied a bout analysis

approach (SIGID, Janik et al., 2013) to acoustic recordings made during focal group follows, to

generate a catalogue of likely signature whistles to which future recordings could be matched and

recaptures determined. Results were ground-truthed with simultaneously collected photo-

identification data demonstrating acoustic (with photographic) recapture over time and space

                                                                                              252
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




The first CR population abundance estimate individually distinctive signature whistles (Longden

et al., 2020) were similar to photo-identification estimates of the same population (Elwen et al.,

2019). Combined, these studies demonstrate the application of signature whistle monitoring for

generating CR data on coastal dolphin populations, including generating empirical information

on individual ranging patterns as well as population abundance estimates. Extending this, spatial

capture-recapture using dolphin signature whistles to generate information on habitat usage and

even density estimates has been explored, with promising results (Romeu et al., 2024).




Humpback dolphins (species from the genus Sousa) are obligate coastal species inhabiting waters

less than 30 meters in depth, estuaries and even rivers (Jefferson & Curry 2015). This habitat

makes them especially vulnerable to human impacts, which are greatest in the near-shore

environment. Of the four species in the genus, the two which occur in African waters are

considered the second (S. teuszii, IUCN Critically Endangered) and eighth (S. plumbea, IUCN

Endangered) most 'at risk' cetacean species globally (Jefferson, 2019). However, no formal

assessment of absolute species abundance exists for either species. For S. plumbea, the best

available estimates are in the low 10's of thousands, with small populations spread thinly and

discontinuously across their 27,000 km range between South Africa and India (Braulik et al.,

2015).




Within South Africa, S. plumbea is the only endangered marine mammal resident in national

coastal waters, an area where human activities have substantially altered the ecosystem which is

undergoing a possible regime shift. Sighting rates are declining, group sizes are reduced and their

distribution range is shrinking, indicating reductions in overall population size (Plön et al., 2015).

Direct mortality through entanglement in bather protection nets is unsustainable (Atkins et al.,

2013; Atkins et al., 2016; Plön et al. 2015). Concentrations of harmful organic pollutants in

bycaught dolphins are among the highest for any dolphin globally (Gui et al., 2016) and 12% of

                                                                                                  253
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




those recently observed suffered rostrum abnormalities. They are naturally predated on by

sharks and face competition for resources from other dolphin species, notably the Indian Ocean

bottlenose dolphin (T. aduncus, Schoeman and Plön 2016). Fewer than 500 individuals likely

remain in South African waters (Vermeulen et al., 2018), split between two sub populations,

further increasing their susceptibility to local depletion, inbreeding depression or catastrophic

events. Urgent conservation action is therefore required to prevent further declines in S. plumbea

in coastal South Africa (Plön et al., 2021a).




Up to date scientific information on S. plumbea ranging patterns, abundance and density is

essential to inform managers and contribute to regional biodiversity action plans (BAPs), (Plön

et al., 2021a). However, most work on S. plumbea took place over a decade ago with limited

temporal and spatial scope. Key data are missing, especially for the south coast population, where

their inaccessible habitat and low density have made studies of the population and individual

health metrics close to impossible. Their near shore distribution, often located within the surf

zone where boat-based research is dangerous, make data acquisition of S. plumea problematic

throughout their range, but particularly so along this stretch of exposed coastline. Specimens

strand infrequently and when found are often in a degraded state and hard to reach location,

further preventing comprehensive genetic or health assessments of beach-cast animals.




Acoustic monitoring offers benefits beyond boat-based photo-identification surveys for CR, in

that data can be collected near to shore (where swells can make boat based photo-identification

hazardous), 24 hrs a day and over multiple weeks or longer (reviewed in Zimmer, 2011).

Therefore, signature whistle CR is increasingly being explored as a valid tool for monitoring

coastal dolphin populations (Longden et al., 2020; Fearey et al., 2022). A programme of long term,

individual monitoring of S. plumbea along the SA coastline has the potential to generate important

information on individual behaviour and ranging patterns over time and space, population

                                                                                              254
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




abundance and even density, essential data for conservation management of the species. This

study explores the feasibility of individual based passive monitoring acoustic monitoring of S.

plumbea using signature whistles. By combining acoustic and simultaneously collected photo-

identification data from several sites in South Africa we investigate the potential of individual CR

using passive acoustic monitoring of stereotyped whistle contours. We highlight some of the

prominent characteristics of S. plumbea acoustic behaviour, which may reflect acoustic

adaptation for signal propagation and localisation within the coastal zone, but present challenges

to automated identification of signature whistles. We discuss the strength and weakness of this

acoustic method for understanding endangered S. plumbea in Southern African waters.




                                                                                                255
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




        METHODS

Field data collection:


Continuous recordings of S. plumbea acoustic signals were made during focal follows of single

species groups from multiple locations along South Africa's south coast (SC) and in Richards Bay,

on the east coast (EC). Recordings were made whenever possible when dolphins were within 200

meters, using the equipment and recording set-ups summarised in Table 1. When using the single

element HTI-96 MIN hydrophone this was mounted to a 1cm diameter steel chain. The SoundTrap

(Ocean Instruments Technology) was attached to a bungee and weighted with a divers’ weight.

In both instances, the weighting helped to hold the hydrophone vertically in the water column,

even when there was current, and the hydrophones were tethered to the research vessel and

deployed to a depth of 2-3 meters. A concerted effort was made to generate recordings with the

research vessel's engines switched off, however as S. plumba were frequency located close to

rocky shore, within the surf zone, this was not always possible. Some recordings contained

intermittent periods of high engine noise during manoeuvring of the research vessel or unusable

periods when the hydrophone was temporarily lifted out of the water, neither of which were

included in the analysis. During data collection, standard information on the group size,

composition and distance from the research vessel were collected at the start and end of each

encounter. High quality dorsal fins images were taken of dolphins within each group encountered

using a Canon digital SLR (200-400 mm lens), to assess the individuals present, using natural

nicks, scars and dorsal hump shape (Hammond 1989; Vermeulen et al., 2017). Photographic data

collection was focussed to the start of each encounter and continued throughout recording

periods when animals were within range.




                                                                                             256
                                          https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Data Analysis: Terminology


The most basic unit in delphinid whistle communication is the frequency modulated whistle

contour, defined as a continuous narrow band tonal sound exceeding 100 ms in duration (Janik

et al., 2013). Contours can be categorised into stereotyped whistle types, containing all whistles

of a particular frequency modulation pattern (Janik & Sayigh, 2013). Signature whistles may be

composed of a single continuous contour with a unique frequency modulation pattern (single

element signature whistle, SE) or as a looped form where the contour is unbroken but produced

with a repetitive pattern (continuous multi-looped signature whistle, CML). Alternatively, several

contours may be repeated in a sequence with a short, stereotyped period of silence separating

them, which in T. truncatus is around 100 ms but can last up to 250 ms in (disconnected multi-

loop, DCML). In the latter case, the constituent contours, termed 'loops' can have an identical or

non-identical frequency modulation pattern (Esch et al., 2009b). A simple transition analysis may

be applied to identify likely DCML and non-identical DCML in acoustic recordings of dolphins

(Janik & Slater 1998; Gridley, 2011). Operationally, signature whistles can be identified as the

most commonly produced whistle type produced by an individual when it is in isolation (Caldwell

et al., 1990) or temporarily restrained (Sayigh et al., 2022). Alternatively, a bout analysis

approach, termed SIGID can be used on recordings of free ranging dolphins, whereby whistles of

a type repeated within 1 to 10 secs are highly likely to be a signature whistle (Janik & Sayigh,

2013). The minimum threshold of three out of four whistles occurring within 1 to 10 secs has

been verified for T. truncatus (Janik et al., 2013) and is commonly used (Fearey et al., 2022). SIGID

has been used to identify likely signature whistles from free ranging dolphins (Quick and Janik,

2012, King and Janik, 2013, Kriesell et al., 2014; Fearey et al. 2019a; Fearey et al., 2022), where

tagging or isolation is not logistically feasible.




                                                                                                 257
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




Acoustic Analysis


Acoustic data from single species groups of S. plumbea were standardised through decimation

and removal of stereo tracks, to ensure that all recordings were mono, 96 kHz sample rate and

saved at 16 bit. The entire acoustic dataset was then batch loaded to Raven Pro v1.6 (Center for

Conservation Bioacoustics, 2019) viewed in the spectrogram display using the page function (10

sec window), facilitating contour assessment and classification cross-checking across the entire

recording dataset. Recordings were scanned in the spectrogram display (settings: FFT : 1024,

Hann window, 50% overlap) to detect whistle contours. Once found, contours were boxed using

the selection function which automatically logs the signal start and end time from the selected

contour as well as other user defined measurements. Each selected contour was graded according

to a visual assessment of the signal to noise ratio (SNR) following Gridley et al., (2012) - 1: signal

is faint and barely visible on the spectrogram, 2: signal is clear and unambiguous, 3: signal is

prominent and dominates.




Both visual and automated methods can be used to verify categorisation of bioacoustic signals

(Janik 1999, Deecke and Janik 2006). Signature whistles can be produced with multiple loops and

the number of loops does not preclude matching to the signature whistle catalogue (Sayigh et al.,

2007; Janik and Sayigh, 2013) when matching is done visually. However, whistle production with

extensive looping (whereby a contour is produced with a short, stereotyped period of silence)

can pose a contour extraction and classification challenge using automated categorisation

involving methods such as contour overlapping or spectrographic cross correlation or image

comparison (Frainer et al., 2023). Even the application of dynamic time warping (Deecke and

Janik, 2006) within automated categorisation through neural networks struggles with how to

approach these looped whistles and their constituent periods of silence. Our dataset of whistle

contours recorded from S. plumbea was not extensive but contained relatively complex whistle

types with multiple disconnected loops, often with a non-identical loop structure. Consequently,


                                                                                                  258
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




we used visual categorisation to identify whistle types, which is a reliable method for signature

whistle identification in Tursiops spp. (Janik, 1999). On scanning the data, a whistle catalogue was

created using clear examples of stereotyped whistle contours, and this was used to classify

whistles during analysis. All high-quality whistles (i.e. SNR 2/3, not masked) were either assigned

as 1) a whistle type in the catalogue and given the associated unique ID, 2) a variable whistle i.e,

unique frequency modulation pattern which does not match the catalogue) or 3) a copy (overlaps

in time with another contour with very similar frequency modulation pattern, Kriesell et al.,

2014). Poor quality whistles (rated SNR 1 and/or masked) were left ‘unclassified’. Throughout

the analysis, a high proportion of the poor-quality whistles could be assigned to a whistle type

with reasonable confidence, particularly when considering the context of recording and if they

were produced within a whistle bout. Where possible, these were assigned to the whistle type

within an exploratory ‘best guess’ sub-category which is reported on but not included within any

of the formal analysis.




The grouping of whistles into discrete types was reviewed multiple times by two authors (TG/SD)

until consensus on categorisation decisions was made. In instances of discrepancy, a conservative

approach was taken and whistles were grouped into one type, rather than split. Once the

catalogue was finalised, we assessed the reliability of the whistle grouping. Five naive observers

were presented with the catalogue of whistle types composed of a single, clear example of each

whistle type in the dataset and asked to match 5 examples of each whistle to the catalogue. Where

categories exceeded 6 whistle repeats (i.e. the catalogue whistle and 5 examples to classify), a

subsample of five whistles were selected at random from the available pool of clear whistles in

that type. Whistles used in the classification trial were saved as electronic spectrogram images

(.png) with standardised time/ frequency axis before being presented to the observers in a

randomised order for the classification trial. Inter-rater reliability in the classification of the




                                                                                                259
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




images was statistically assessed using Fleiss' kappa statistics to assess the degree of agreement

over chance.




For types containing 4 or more whistles, the inter-whistle interval between whistles of the same

type was then calculated – from the end of the whistle to the start of the following whistle of the

same type. The temporal patterning of whistle production was explored using log survivorship

plots of inter-whistle-intervals (IWI) from 0.25 to 60 sec. This threshold was chosen, as intervals

longer than 60 sec might reflect movement of dolphins out of the detection radius or recording

interruption, rather than the true temporal pattern in whistle production.




To explore these features of the humpback dolphin acoustic behaviour, all good quality (SNR 2/3)

whistles of each type were classified as one of four categories (SE, CML, DCML, NID-DCML) and

the number of loops counted. Loop duration and the inter-loop interval (from the end of the loop

contour to start of next one) were calculated for all disconnected multi-looped whistles. We

explored the characteristics and variability in whistle production, including the duration and

structure of the introductory loop (loop 1) compared to subsequent loops and the distribution of

inter-loop intervals.




We applied the SIGID criteria to identify likely signature whistles (signature whistle types, SWTs)

using the sequence application of the SIGID bout criteria (Janik et al., 2013). To be considered a

likely signature whistle, a minimum of 3 out of 4 whistles of the same type (the same frequency

modulation pattern) should occur in a bout with IWIs of 1 to 10 sec (Janik et al., 2013). Whistle

copies are rare (Janik, 2000) and can be identified as a) variable examples of the catalogued

signature whistle (King et al., 2013), b) similar whistle contours overlapping in time (Janik 2000,

Kriesell et al. 2014). Where the production of stereotyped whistles did not meet the SIGID criteria,


                                                                                                260
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




the type was classified as a repeatedly emitted whistle type (REWT) and the occurrence of SWTs

and REWTs was considered in the following analysis. Reporting on the characteristics of REWTs

is relevant when assessing signature whistles in new species, as the distinction between a SWTs

and REWTs may be related to sample size effects (Janik et al., 2013) or species differences in

whistle temporal production (i.e. IWI, Fearey et al., 2019b) and understanding these constraints

or bias can help inform future PAM effort.




Capture-Recapture using acoustic and photo-graphic data


The encounter data of SWTs and REWTs were converted into a capture matrix of whistle types

over time and space. Evidence for acoustic recapture events was considered depending on

whether the following criteria were met. There was strong evidence for signature whistle

recapture if, on the successive encounter > 4 whistles of the same type were detected and

independently matched SIGID production criteria. Should recapture locations differ, they were

over likely ranges for individual S plumbea movement as previously demonstrated through

photo-identification (mean 140 km, max 500 km, Vermeulen et al., 2017). There was moderate

evidence of acoustic recapture when one to three whistles of a type were recaptured, and

recapture locations were over likely ranges for individual S plumbea movement. However, if one

to three whistles were recaptured and recapture locations were beyond S plumbea home ranges,

this was considered weak evidence for recapture.




Associated photo-ID data of S. plumbea, based on natural marks on dorsal fins and dorsal humps,

were processed using standard approaches (Elwen et al. 2019) to confirm the identity of dolphins

present during each encounter. The number of unique individuals present from photo-ID data

was taken as the minimum group size per encounter. However, animals can be present during an

encounter but not adequately photographed (particularly if the group is located within the surf

zone), therefore if best estimates of group size made in the field were greater that photographic

                                                                                             261
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




counts of individuals present, the field estimate was used. The count and identity of individuals

present was compared across the acoustic and photographic capture matrices. Assuming no two

dolphins have exactly the same signature whistle (Janik & Sayigh, 2013), we compared the

number of individuals captured photographically and acoustically. Given that individual dolphins

can be present but their signature whistle not captured in recordings if a) it did not produce its

signature whistle, b) the recording of was not of sufficient quality to facilitate matching to the

catalogue, c) the dolphin whistled but was out of (clear) detection range, and that whistle copying

is rare (Janik, 2000), we expected the number of whistle types to be less than or equal to the best

group size estimate per encounter.




       ETHICS STATEMENT

Data were collected under University of Stellenbosch and University of Cape Town ethics permits

(reference: 2020/V11/TG) awarded to TG and University of Pretoria ethics permit awarded to

SE/SA. Research was conducted under a series of research permits issued by the Department of

Environment Forestry and Fisheries (and its predecessors) to SE/EV/SA




                                                                                               262
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




       RESULTS

Recording effort was spread over 8 years (2016 – 2023) with 21 recording encounters over 27

days. When encountered, S plumbea were in small group sizes ranging from 1 to 12 individuals

and close to shore, estuaries, and harbours. Groups were recorded across a range of group sizes

and compositions, but whistles were not detected in all recording sessions. Overall, we detected

1551 whistle contours from 17 encounters spanning 20 recording hrs with S. plumbea in coastal

South Africa. The whistles of S. plumbea were highly stereotyped. Of the 591 good quality whistles

recorded (clearly detected, SNR 2/3, not masked), 410 (69%) were grouped into one of 23 whistle

types, containing between 2 and 100 whistle emissions per type. A further 276 whistles of low

recording quality (SNR 1 or masked) could be allocated to these types with moderate confidence

(i.e. considered a ‘best guess’) but to ensure standards of data quality, were not included within

subsequent analysis. After removing types with fewer than four whistles, 21 whistle types

remained which were identified over 13 encounters with S. plumbea (Table 1). Visual

categorisation of signature whistles was well supported, demonstrating highly stereotyped

groupings.




The distribution of IWIs and log survivorship plot (Figure 2) demonstrate a clear pattern in the

temporal production of whistles in the 21 types, with a peak at 1 to 3 sec, and 65% of whistles in

a bout occurring within 0.25 to 10 sec of another whistle of the same type (Figure 2). By applying

the SIGID criteria to this temporal production data, two thirds of the whistle types identified (n =

14) were confirmed as likely signature whistles (SWTs). Notably, the size of each whistle type

category, i.e., the number of whistles grouped together, strongly influenced whether a type met

or exceeded the SIGID bout criteria threshold. All whistle types where ten or more whistle repeats

were grouped together were subsequently identified by SIGID as signature whistles. However,

only one type (SWT13), with 15 whistle emissions failed to meet the SIGID production criteria.




                                                                                                263
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




This suggests an influence of sample size on whether whistle types will subsequently meet the

bout criteria thresholds which enable them to be assigned as signature whistles with confidence.




Most whistle types were produced in both single element and disconnected multi-looped forms

(12 of the 14 SWT, Figure 3). Recording SWT with up to 4 loops was common (at least once in 9

of 14 SWT) with a maximum of 13 loops recorded (SWT5). When produced with loops, the single

element version matched the frequency modulation of the introductory loop of the DCML version.

Of the loop sequence, the introductory loop was usually the longest in duration and most complex

in terms of frequency modulation pattern (Figure 7). From loop two onwards, these repeated

loops were short contours and simplistic in form (e.g. Figure 2, SWT5, 6 and 11). The two SWT

which were never recorded in a DCML form were recorded relatively infrequently, i.e. SWT1 (n =

12) as a connected multi-loop and SWT61 (n = 10) as a single element. With greater recording

effort of these whistle types, a higher degree of variability in the whistle form may become

apparent.




The recapture of dolphins through whistle monitoring is indicated in Table 2. Over the eight years

with recording effort and 14 SWT, most were recaptured at least once - i.e. recorded on separate

occasions. From these, five were recaptured over multiple years and five over multiple locations.

In rare cases, SWTs were recorded over multiple years and locations. For example, SWT11 was

recorded three times in False Bay in 2016, in St Sebastian Bay in 2019 and then in Mossel Bay in

both 2019 and 2023 (Figure 8).




There is a weakly positive linear relationship between the number of individual animals

identified through photographs during encounters and the number of whistle types (grey, SWT +

REWT) and signature whistle types (red, SWT). The relationship is stronger (R2 = 0.11 compared


                                                                                              264
                                    https://scholar.sun.ac.za




to R2 = 0.06) when all whistle types are considered compared to when only signature whistle

types are considered.




                                                                                       265
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




        DISCUSSION

Species extinction is rapid within the Anthropocene Epoch with an increasing number of species

recognised as threatened, vulnerable or endangered. Conservation actions for Sousa species have

been limited and until recently they received almost no management attention (Jefferson & Curry

2015). However, the IUCN, International Whaling Commission (IWC) and Convention on

Migratory Species (CMS) are now united in calling for urgent global action to conserve Sousa

species and prevent them following other cetaceans species, such as the Baji and vaquita on the

path to irreversible extinction. Generating empirical data on Sousa plumbea is difficult. However,

here we demonstrate that a novel acoustic approach can be used to determine animal presence

and ranging patterns, with individuals re-captured over time (up to 8 years) and space (up to 480

km). Passive acoustic monitoring is becoming a common tool in conservation biology used to

document the occurrence and distribution of severely threatened species such as the critically

endangered Vaquita (Rankin et al., 2009), Maui's Dolphin (Rayment et al., 2011) and the recently

extinct Baji (Wang et al., 2006) as well as Sousa species (Caruso et al., 2020). However, this is the

first attempt to use individually distinctive acoustic signals in the conservation of an endangered

marine mammal.




Acknowledging individual behavioural differences can help avoid sampling and experimental

bias and increase explanatory power. Where actions and responses differ at the individual scale,

such variation provides the raw material for natural selection and therefore understanding

personality differences may help predict the effects of environmental change on wild populations

(Roche et al., 2016). Only recently are we starting to understand a) the extent of individual

variation in behaviour within and between species b) the methods by which this variation can be

measured, c) the impact this variation has on individual survival, reproductive success and

wellbeing, and d) the impact this variation has on the evolution of populations. Our results

indicate that certain individuals within the South African Sousa population are ranging along the

                                                                                                 266
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




coastline, over several hundred km. This was evidenced through both the photo-graphic data and

the acoustic data collection. In fact, the recapture through acoustic data, indicated a range greater

than for the Photo-ID, whereby there was confident recapture of SWT11 between False Bay and

Mossel Bay. Assuming this is reflective of the individual present these acoustic data are providing

important insight into both the occurrence and ranges of individual humpback dolphins. These

data can therefore be used within a risk analysis approach, to determine the exposure of

individual to likely threats along the south African Coastline.




In addition to encoding identity information, the general form of whistles within a repertoire (i.e.

single element, connected multi-loop, disconnected multi-loop or non-identical disconnected

multi-loop) may encode population or species information. Additionally, variability in loop

duration and / or of the silent gap between loops (the inter-loop interval) in disconnected

whistles could encode additional identity or arousal cues (Esch et al., 2009a, Briefer 2012;

Probert et al. 2021).




Although the relationship between whistle types and animals present was far from 1:1, it was

linear and positive. As signature whistles function as cohesion calls (Janik & Slater, 1998), their

production is strongly context dependent (Cook et al. 2004; Quick and Janik 2012; Probert et al.

2021). Consequently, with the limited focal group follow recording effort presented, it is

unsurprising that the number of photographically identified animals usually exceeded that

identified through acoustic methods. Longer recording effort, has in other species, show string

alignment to photographically determined estimates of population size (Longden et al. 2020).

That the relationship was stronger when all whistle types are considered compared to when only

signature whistle types are considered s provides some support to the proposal that REWTs are

putative signature whistles but undefined as such due to sample size restrictions. In rare cases,

the number of photo-identified identified individuals exceeded the number identified

                                                                                                 267
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




acoustically. However, on assessment of the field best estimates, this could be explained by the

incomplete photo-identification coverage, due to the inshore distribution of the group and the

need to be stationed safely further from shore to collect acoustic data. Furthermore, on the date

when the largest discrepancy occurred, the field estimate of group size exceeded the number of

animals identified, compared to only 6 animals identified. On this day, it is apparent that the

photo-identification data was not a complete representation of the group present.




In recognition of the challenges facing S. plumbea, a nationwide, 'SouSA Consortium' was

established in 2016, involving 18 individuals from 13 institutions (Plön et al. 2021b) with the goal

of collaborating to share knowledge and improve conservation actions. The consortium has

already produced several peer reviewed articles (Vermeulen et al. 2017; Plön et al. 2021b;Plön et

al. in press), IWC reports and successful funding applications. This study uses data from

consortium members and collaborations generated across the geographic range of the species in

South Africa. By combining local knowledge, resources and data the SouSA consortium the

consortium is finding innovative ways to generate important information which will facilitate

species conservation management. The results of this initial investigation demonstrate the

applicability of passive acoustic monitoring for this endangered species upon which future

studies can be based.

     APPENDIX 2


Case study: Evidence of long-term stability in a
stereotyped whistle in a single free-ranging humpback
dolphin (Sousa plumbea) found in sympatry (Tursiops
aduncus)
Submitted JASA Express Letters


Dines S*,1,2 Probert R,1,2 , Gullan A3, Elwen S1,2, Frainer G2,3, and Gridley T2,3

1Department of Botany and Zoology, Faculty of Science, Stellenbosch University, Stellenbosch, , South

Africa 7605
2. Seasearch Research and Conservation, 4 Bath Road, Muizenberg, Cape Town, South Africa, 7950
2 Dolphin Encountours Research Center, Ponta do Ouro, Mozambique, 0025
3Centre for Statistics in Ecology, Environment and Conservation, Department of Statistical Sciences,

University of Cape Town, South Africa 7700


sashafrancess@gmail.com



This 11-year case study describes the acoustic behaviour of a resident Indian Ocean humpback
dolphin, (Sousa plumbea), during commercial swim-with-dolphin activities in Mozambique.
Combining data collected using low-cost action cameras with full bandwidth hydrophone recordings,
a temporally stable stereotyped whistle contour was identified that met the SIGID bout criteria used
to identify signature whistles in other dolphin species. This whistle was produced with potential
information-enhancing features (biphonation and subtle variations in frequency modulation).
Despite evidence in other Sousa species, signature whistle-based communication has not been
described in S. plumbea. This case study provides evidence for a single, stable, stereotyped call type.
from a single individual in a mixed species group, contributing to the growing body of evidence for
possible signature whistle use in the Sousa genus.




Keywords: Acoustic, Sound, Stereotyped whistle, Sousa, biphonation



                                                                                                   284
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




    Introduction

The acoustic repertoire of Sousa spp. comprises acoustic signals common in delphinids, typically

grouped into echolocation clicks, burst pulses and whistles (Herzing, 2014; Van Parijs et al., 2000;

Yang et al., 2020). Whistles are frequency modulated tonal calls strongly associated with social

contexts (Tyack, 1997), and humpback dolphins seem to present anatomical adaptations for high

frequency communication sounds (Frainer et al., 2019). Whistle frequency parameters vary

across the genus with Atlantic humpback dolphins (S. teuszii) producing whistles with the lowest

fundamental frequency across the genus, with a mean maximum of 8.2 kHz (Weir, 2010).

Australian humpback dolphins (S. sahulensis) produce whistles with a higher mean maximum of

10.58 kHz and a maximum recorded fundamental frequency of 22 kHz (although this maximum

frequency may have been limited by recording equipment, Van Parijs & Cockeron., 2001). The

whistle repertoire of Indian Ocean humpback dolphins (S. plumbea) has only been reported from

India where mean minimum and maximum frequencies ranged from 7.6 – 10.2 kHz, respectively,

and an absolute maximum frequency of 33 kHz was reported (Bopardikar et al., 2018).



Humpback dolphins (Sousa spp) are listed as endangered on the IUCN Red List (Braulik et al.,

2017), and effective methods to monitor populations at an individual level are needed. Passive

acoustic monitoring (PAM) is a well-established research tool for the study of wild cetacean

populations through the identification of species-specific calls in long-term acoustic recordings.

More recently, the use of PAM for studying delphinids has been extended to individual capture-

recapture using ‘signature whistles’ (Erbs et al., 2017; Fearey et al., 2022; Longden et al., 2020;

Romeu et al., 2023). These are individually distinctive calls used to communicate identity

information and maintain group cohesion and are well demonstrated in bottlenose dolphins

(Tursiops truncatus) (Janik & Sayigh, 2013). Since the 1960s, evidence has been presented that

supports likely signature whistle use across other odontocete species (Tursiops aduncus Gridley

et al., 2014 Stenella longirostris Rio, 2023, Stenella plagiodon Perin 1973, Delphinus delphis Fearey




                                                                                                 285
                                         https://scholar.sun.ac.za




et al., 2019, Sousa chinensis Van Parijs & Corkeron, 2001, Pseudorca crassidens Rio & Rosales-

Nanduca, 2023, Legenorhynchus obliquidens Caldwell and Caldwell 1970, Sotalia guianensis Lima

& Le Pendu, 2014, and Monodon monoceros Shapiro, 2006). Evidence for signature whistle use

has been presented in Sousa spp. under heightened instances of stress, including an injured Indo-

pacific humpback dolphin (S. chinensis) (Cheng et al., 2017) and from a stranded Australian

humpback dolphin (S. sahulensis, formally described as S. chinensis) (Van Parijs & Corkeron,

2001). Evidence for signature whistle use has not yet been demonstrated in free swimming Indian

Ocean humpback dolphins (S. plumbea).



To reliably communicate identity information, the acoustic signal should have high inter-

individual (i.e., distinctive) and low intra-individual (i.e., stereotyped) variability (Janik et al.,

2006; Sayigh et al., 1990, Charrier et al 2003). This can be achieved through inter-individual

differences in the vocal apparatus, resulting in by-product distinctiveness (so called voice

features) (Taylor & Reby, 2010). Provided that these differences remain stable over time and the

receiver can perceive these differences, identity information can accurately be encoded and

decoded by the signaler and receiver, respectively. Alternatively, and more cognitively complex,

is use of ‘designed individual signatures’ which can develop through a process of production

learning. For bottlenose dolphins, there is strong evidence that signature whistles are developed

through a process of vocal production learning (Janik et al., 2006), where the acoustic

environment experienced by the dolphin can influence the overall frequency modulation pattern

of the ‘crystalised’ signature whistle (Fripp et al., 2005, Janik & Sayigh, 2013). Signature whistles

are established in calves as early as the first two months of life, with high production rates in

mother-calf pairs who use signature whistles to facilitate reunions after short separation periods

(Smolker et al 1993; Janik, 2009; King et al., 2016). As cohesion calls, signature whistles are

produced at elevated rates during separation or isolation events and high arousal states including

times of stress (Caldwell et al., 1990; Esch et al., 2009a; Probert et al., 2021, 2023a) In these




                                                                                                  286
                                        https://scholar.sun.ac.za




contexts, emissions may become less stereotyped and more variable in nature, occasionally with

the production of non-linear phenomena (Sportelli et al., 2023).



Like other species within the genus, the habitat preference of S. plumbea includes estuaries and

murky rivers, and they are rarely held in captivity, therefore underwater observations of

humpback dolphins are rare and the context of sound production often unknown. It is generally

accepted that the acoustic signals produced by dolphins originate from the monkey-lips dorsal

bursa complex (Cranford et al, 1996), and that bi-phonation is possible whereby dolphins can

produce whistles and pulsed calls (clicks and burst pulses) simultaneously (Madsen et al., 2013;

Ridgway et al., 2015; Kriesell et al., 2014). Unlike other wild dolphin populations, information on

individual acoustic behaviour, including signature whistle production, and biphonation, is

restricted (Herzing, 1996; Papale et al., 2015). However, Ponta do Ouro, Mozambique, is one of

the few places where long term in-water interactions with this species have been recorded. Using

historical data, the acoustic behaviour of S. plumbea was investigated using a case study of a

resident individual in Ponta do Ouro, Mozambique, named Herme, using in-water video and

acoustic recordings.




                                                                                               287
                                       https://scholar.sun.ac.za




   Methods

Study site and species

Ponta do Ouro Partial Marine Reserve (26.84°S, 32.88°E) is Mozambique’s southernmost

conservation area. Boat-based research and dolphin tourism involving swim-with-dolphin

experiences have been taking place in Ponta do Ouro since 1994, mainly targeting inshore Indian

Ocean bottlenose dolphins (Tursiops aduncus). Over 250 bottlenose dolphins have been

catalogued within this population, of which 60 are considered ‘resident’, and live sympatrically

with S. plumbea (AG pers. comm). Comparatively, only a small number of S. plumbea (n = 6) have

been identified and catalogued during this time (AG pers. comm). One humpback dolphin, locally

named Herme, was born in 2006 is the most commonly sighted humpback dolphin in the study

area and is exclusively encountered with T. aduncus groups.



Data Collection

Acoustic and video data were opportunistically collected between 2009 and 2019 during

commercial swim-with-dolphin activities in the Ponta do Ouro bay. During 2020, additional

acoustic data were collected with a focus on S. plumbea. Acoustic data were collected by permitted

tour operators and/or under research permits from an 8 m dive boat with two 90 hp four stroke

engines. Encounters were limited to 30 minutes and a maximum of two in-water encounters were

permitted per launch (up to three launches per day).



Once dolphins were sighted and before they were approached, the group were observed for 5

minutes and swimmers did not enter the water if the dolphins showed restless behaviour, if they

had newborns in the group, or if they were travelling. If conditions were suitable, dolphins were

close (< 20 meters) and it was considered safe to do so, swimmers entered the water to the side

or ahead of the group according to strict regulations to minimise disturbance. During underwater

encounters, audio and visual data were collected in close proximity to both T. aduncus and S.

plumbea using the equipment summarised in Table 1.


                                                                                              288
                                           https://scholar.sun.ac.za
